| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| **Status** | `approved` |
| **NNN** | `021` |

## Purpose

Define the Electron desktop shell (main process + preload) and React + Vite renderer for AI Sidekicks — the second client delivery track after the CLI (per `container-architecture.md` §Client Delivery Sequence). This spec specifies:

- the shell/renderer process boundary and the preload bridge capability surface
- main-process responsibilities (windowing, native dialogs, notifications, deep-link handling, daemon supervision, auto-update, native keystore access, WebAuthn orchestration)
- renderer composition for each V1 Signature Feature view
- code-signing, notarization, and distribution across macOS (arm64 + x64), Windows 10/11 (x64), and Linux (x64 + arm64)
- the explicit renderer-untrusted trust stance required to keep auth material out of renderer reach

## Scope

In scope:

- Electron main-process architecture and lifecycle
- Preload bridge contract (capability surface between renderer and main)
- Renderer composition of V1 Signature Features (timeline, approvals, runs, multi-agent channels)
- The console design language (Meridian), the console surface set, the pane and window model, the budgets, and the fixture bridge the renderer is built and tested against (§Console Design (Meridian))
- The console's own surfaces and what each one shows, offers and refuses: the session screen — one user, any of their linked devices, one runtime node executing, over a session that lives where the repository is — with its icon rail, its all-sessions list, its session workspace, its composer, its pane kinds, its auxiliary windows, its settings and operator pages, and its notification surface (§The surface set)
- Daemon supervision from the shell (start, stop, health, crash recovery, version pinning)
- Auto-update flow, signature verification, and rollback safety
- Code-signing and notarization for all three V1 platforms
- WebAuthn (including the PRF extension) orchestration for desktop credential flows
- OS-keystore integration for persistent auth material
- Deep-link handling for `sidekicks://` URLs
- Security hardening posture (contextIsolation, sandbox, Electron Fuses, CSP)
- Crash reporting for main, renderer, and supervised-daemon process crashes
- Accessibility baseline (OS-level screen-reader and high-contrast compliance; WCAG 2.2 AA for every console route and dialog per §Console Design (Meridian))

## Non-Goals

- Pixel-exact measurements. `§Console Design (Meridian)` fixes the design language, the surface set, the pane and window model, the budgets, and — through `§Console Libraries` — the component-library and theme decisions, and its surface set fixes what each surface shows, offers and refuses. What it does not fix is a figure in pixels: every width, floor, ceiling and gap in the console is derived from what it holds at the current text size, and a pixel figure quoted for one of them anywhere in this spec is an illustration at the default text size rather than a requirement. The few figures the design language does fix outright — the icon grid and its stroke, the radius scale, the hairline — are named in `§Console Design (Meridian)` and are the exceptions. A surface's own density budget, its fixture scenarios and the code that draws it are Plan-021's console registration.
- Multi-person surfaces. There is one user. A read-only spectator mode, a side pane of people, a people-only chat, request-to-drive controls, hand-over and take-back, approval routing to a named person, and turn ownership are all out; `Interrupt` is available on every linked device instead.
- A Remote Control front end. The multi-device surface is gated on its own backend work ([Spec-028](./028-remote-control.md)); the console's session screen is the single-user surface, and the one thing another device is given today is a live picture of a previewed page.
- The session event log as a feature. It is substrate: it powers crash recovery and the transcript projection, and it is never named on screen and never rendered as a record of everything that happened.
- A replay surface. No scrubber and no speed presets; scrollback is scrollback. The one reading aid beside the scrollbar is the ask rail at the transcript's right edge, hidden until the pointer rests there.
- An overlay the design has not named. A modal or pop-up over the transcript exists only where this spec names one — the plan reader, the command palette, the find box, the quick look over a picture or a document, and the box that searches every session — and each is named there rather than admitted as a pattern.
- A surface that ships a dead control. Absent, not disabled, is the standing rule, with the named exceptions §Rules every console surface obeys lists; each of those is greyed in place with its reason in its own hover title.
- A surface that re-derives daemon truth. The console renders what the daemon reports.
- A tenth settings page, or a second settings search. Settings is nine pages in one fixed order; the one field above the page list finds the controls on every page, and the Keyboard page's own box, which filters that page's rows, is the only other search on the screen.
- A model-visibility control, and a provider-wide list of tools held off. The model control lists the resolved provider's whole catalogue, read per provider and refreshed when it goes stale so a model that has left it is never offered; an agent's own tool allowlist carries the provider's built-in tools beside the tool servers, and a standing per-provider disabled set would be a third precedence layer over posture and allowlist.
- Theme authoring. Two themes ship as a closed set: a third is not written, imported or loaded from a file, and no control anywhere edits a colour.
- Archived sessions as a settings page. They stay in the sessions list as their own group, the way archived projects sit at the foot of the Projects page. Each of these four absences is absent rather than disabled — nothing refuses them, because nothing offers them.
- Daemon internals (owned by `component-architecture-local-daemon.md` and Spec-006)
- Control-plane authentication protocol details (owned by [Spec-028](./028-remote-control.md) and ADR-010)
- Mobile or browser-hosted renderer surfaces (out of V1 per ADR-015; browser-only local clients explicitly out of scope per [Spec-006 §Resolved Questions and V1 Scope Decisions](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#resolved-questions-and-v1-scope-decisions))
- The CLI client (Spec-006 owns the IPC contract the renderer reuses; CLI-specific UX is out of scope here)
- Provider-driver internal protocols (owned by Spec-004)
- Workflow authoring semantics — the engine, the definition model, the human-phase forms — owned by [Spec-015](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md) / Plan-015 (V1 feature 17 since the 2026-04-22 [ADR-015](../decisions/015-v1-feature-scope-definition.md) amendment) with the node-graph builder under [ADR-026](../decisions/026-visual-node-graph-workflow-authoring.md); the renderer composes those surfaces as the `workflow-run` and `workflow-builder` pane kinds of `§Console Design (Meridian)` and this spec restates none of their behavior. _Erratum corrected 2026-09-01: the prior bullet still called the engine V1.1._

## Required Behavior

### Process Model

The desktop application must run as three cooperating processes:

1. **Shell (Electron main process).** Node.js runtime. Owns windowing, native dialogs, notifications, deep-link protocol handler, daemon supervision, auto-updater, OS-keystore access, WebAuthn orchestration, and all session-scoped auth material.
2. **Renderer (Electron renderer process, one per window).** Chromium with `contextIsolation: true` and `sandbox: true`. Loads the React + Vite bundle. Has no direct Node.js access and no direct filesystem, network, or OS access — all such capabilities flow through the preload bridge.
3. **Local Daemon (spawned child process of the shell, or external service).** Runs `packages/runtime-daemon/`. The shell supervises it via the `DaemonStart`, `DaemonStop`, `DaemonRestart`, and `DaemonStatusRead` surface from Spec-006. The daemon owns all execution authority.

The renderer must never fork, spawn, or exec a process. The renderer must never open a filesystem handle or a network socket directly — every such operation flows through the preload bridge and is enforced in the shell's main process.

### Trust Stance

The renderer is **untrusted** relative to the shell and daemon, consistent with `container-architecture.md` §Trust Boundaries and `component-architecture-desktop-app.md` §Trust Boundaries.

The shell (main process) holds all of the following; the renderer never holds any of them:

- the local daemon session token from `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/ai-sidekicks/daemon.token` per Security Architecture §Local Daemon Authentication (loaded at shell startup)
- the PASETO v4.public access token issued by the Control Plane per ADR-010
- the PASETO v4.local refresh token per ADR-010
- the ephemeral Ed25519 DPoP private key bound to the access token per Security Architecture §Control-Plane Authentication
- WebAuthn PRF-derived credential-wrapping keys per ADR-010
- any user-identity private-key material stored in the OS keystore

All renderer-originated daemon or control-plane requests flow through the preload bridge, which:

1. validates the request against a narrow capability-typed contract brokered to the renderer at session start
2. attaches the session token (for daemon calls) or the PASETO access token + DPoP proof (for control-plane calls) in the main process
3. forwards the request over the Spec-006 Content-Length JSON-RPC transport (daemon) or the ADR-014 tRPC / WebSocket transport (control plane)
4. returns only the sanitized response payload to the renderer — never the raw auth headers

`security-architecture.md` §Local Daemon Authentication was reconciled with this spec's renderer-untrusted stance under BL-056 on 2026-04-18. It now states that the renderer is **not a direct daemon client**; all renderer-originated requests flow through the preload bridge to the Desktop Shell, which forwards them to the daemon with attached auth headers. The Shell and CLI both present the 256-bit session token at daemon-connect time (token is primary; socket permissions 0700 are defense-in-depth). The prior "trusted local process" framing and the "token optional for renderer / CLI" permission have been removed.

### Security Hardening Baseline

Every window the shell opens is a `BaseWindow`, and every document it shows — the renderer itself, each auxiliary window's renderer, and every page the `browser` pane hosts — is a `WebContentsView` added to that window's content view. A `BrowserWindow` is not used anywhere, because it owns a never-navigated `webContents` of its own that appears in the Chrome-debug target list as a mute page target: an external debug client auto-attaches to that target and then blocks on it forever, so `connectOverCDP` against a `BrowserWindow` times out at thirty seconds, while against a `BaseWindow` it completes in 36 to 37 milliseconds and the process count is four rather than five (measured 2026-09-14 on Electron 44.1.0, Chromium 152, macOS arm64). The embedded browser subsystem attaches through that endpoint, so the window shape is a hardening and a correctness requirement at once ([ADR-036](../decisions/036-embedded-browser-for-preview.md)).

Every such view must be created with the following `webPreferences`:

```ts
{
  contextIsolation: true,          // must be true; isolates preload from renderer
  sandbox: true,                   // must be true; renderer runs in OS-level sandbox
  nodeIntegration: false,          // must be false; no Node APIs in renderer
  nodeIntegrationInWorker: false,  // must be false
  webSecurity: true,               // must be true; enforces same-origin
  preload: '<absolute path>',      // preload script registered here
  // no remoteModule (removed in Electron >= 14)
}
```

Every renderer document must be served with a strict `Content-Security-Policy`. At minimum:

```
default-src 'self';
script-src 'self';
style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline';
connect-src 'self' https://<configured-control-plane-origin> wss://<configured-relay-origin>;
img-src 'self' data: blob:;
font-src 'self';
frame-src 'none';
object-src 'none';
base-uri 'none';
form-action 'none';
```

Electron Fuses — the shell binary must be packaged with the following fuses (see [Implementation Notes §Electron Fuses](#electron-fuses) for rationale and primary-source citations):

- `RunAsNode`: **disabled** — prevents the shipped Electron binary from running as a generic Node.js runtime via `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`
- `EnableCookieEncryption`: **enabled** — encrypts the cookie store at rest using OS-level primitives
- `EnableNodeOptionsEnvironmentVariable`: **disabled** — prevents `NODE_OPTIONS` / `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` from injecting code at startup
- `EnableNodeCliInspectArguments`: **disabled** — prevents `--inspect` and `--inspect-brk` debugger flags at the Electron command line
- `EnableEmbeddedAsarIntegrityValidation`: **enabled** — verifies asar-bundle integrity at load time (graduated from experimental to stable in Electron 39; now available on Linux via digest mode as of Electron 41)
- `OnlyLoadAppFromAsar`: **enabled** — refuses to load the app from anywhere other than the signed asar
- `LoadBrowserProcessSpecificV8Snapshot`: **enabled** — prevents renderer V8 snapshots from leaking into the browser process
- `GrantFileProtocolExtraPrivileges`: **disabled** — refuses to grant privileged APIs to `file://` origins; the renderer is served via a custom protocol, not `file://`
- `WasmTrapHandlers`: **enabled** (default) — required for WASM memory safety without perf cost

In addition to the Fuses above, release builds must embed an **ASAR Integrity Digest** (new in Electron 41 via `@electron/asar` v4.1.0+, invoked as `asar integrity-digest on /path/to/YourApp.app`). Digest generation must run **before** the code-signing step because toggling fuses and embedding the digest both invalidate the signature.

### Preload Bridge Contract

The preload script exposes a single typed object on `window.sidekicks` via `contextBridge.exposeInMainWorld`. The object surface must be declarative, narrow, and capability-scoped.

**One window carries a different bridge, and deliberately not this one.** The `SidekicksBridge` above is the surface of the session windows. The **secret-entry window** — the modal that collects the passphrase for the §Fallback Behavior password-derived KEK — is built on the I-021-12 hardening pattern (its own hardened window through the same locked-`webPreferences` constructor, its own preload, its own bridge instance, no shared in-memory store, loaded from the packaged bundle over the renderer scheme) and exposes **exactly one write-only operation**: it hands main the entered passphrase once and receives nothing back — no echo, no read accessor, no derived key, no success payload beyond a resolved `void` — after which main closes the window. It is **not** a `SidekicksBridge` namespace and is not reachable from any session window, because a namespace on the shared object would be callable from every renderer that holds it, and a surface that accepts a passphrase must be reachable only from the one window that exists to collect one. It is **not** a member of the two auxiliary windows §Main Process Responsibilities names either: it hosts no pane, subscribes to nothing, and is opened by main rather than from the menu bar, so the auxiliary-window set stays at two.

```ts
interface SidekicksBridge {
  // daemon RPC — request/response over Spec-006 JSON-RPC contract
  daemon: {
    call<M extends DaemonMethod>(method: M, params: DaemonParams<M>): Promise<DaemonResult<M>>;
    subscribe<E extends DaemonEvent>(
      event: E,
      handler: (payload: DaemonEventPayload<E>) => void,
    ): Unsubscribe;
  };

  // control-plane RPC — request/response over tRPC; device-presence events over WebSocket JSON-RPC 2.0 (relay traffic rides the relay's binary wire frames, not JSON-RPC). Session-timeline / run-output streams are tRPC SSE per [Spec-028](./028-remote-control.md) — this bridge does not yet expose them; a typed `subscribe` surface lands with the plan wiring renderer control-plane subscriptions (the contracts bridge shape in `packages/contracts` is verbatim-bound to this block; that binding fixes the **Tier-1** shape, and a Tier-7 namespace joins it only where a spec section names its owner — `onboarding`, delegated by [Spec-023 §Desktop Surface](./023-first-run-onboarding.md#desktop-surface) to Plan-021 for the surface and Plan-023 for the flow body. A namespace with no such named delegation is drift)
  controlPlane: {
    call<P extends CpProcedure>(procedure: P, input: CpInput<P>): Promise<CpOutput<P>>;
    subscribeRelay(sessionId: SessionId, handler: RelayEventHandler): Unsubscribe;
  };

  // native capabilities — renderer requests, main performs, sanitized result returned
  native: {
    // the composer's Attach control opens this with files only and several at a time,
    // never a folder: one picker cannot offer both files and folders on Windows or
    // Linux (Electron `dialog` API reference, read 2026-09-14), and a folder reaches a
    // agent as a path typed or dropped into the message instead.
    showOpenDialog(options: OpenDialogOptions): Promise<OpenDialogResult>;
    showSaveDialog(options: SaveDialogOptions): Promise<SaveDialogResult>;
    showMessageBox(options: MessageBoxOptions): Promise<MessageBoxResult>;
    showNotification(options: NotificationOptions): void;
    // main-process allowlist-validated. Every address the console hands outward goes
    // here except a loopback address printed in a reply, a tool row or a shell, which
    // opens in the `browser` pane instead; an address main refuses is refused in
    // words on the surface that asked, never silently dropped.
    openExternal(url: string): Promise<void>;
    copyToClipboard(text: string): Promise<void>;
    revealInFileExplorer(path: FilePathRef): Promise<void>;
  };

  // the `browser` pane's native page hosts — the renderer measures, main performs
  // (ADR-036). The renderer owns no page: it publishes the rectangle a page is
  // positioned to and renders the acts' outcomes. Wired live with that record; until
  // then it resolves through the growth port.
  browser: {
    publishPaneRect(pane: PaneRef, rect: PaneRect | null): void;
    act(pane: PaneRef, act: BrowserPaneAct): Promise<BrowserPaneActResult>;
    capturePage(pane: PaneRef): Promise<CapturedPageImage>;
    subscribe(pane: PaneRef, handler: (state: BrowserPaneState) => void): Unsubscribe;
  };

  // window controls — the renderer-initiated detach of a `timeline` or `agent-console`
  // pane into its own window. On Plan-021's console growth slate; the menu-bar and
  // keybinding paths ship first because they need no namespace.
  window: {
    detachPane(pane: PaneRef): Promise<void>;
  };

  // WebAuthn — the renderer's navigator.credentials.* is not a usable path here: on the
  //           `sidekicks-renderer://` scheme the renderer is already a secure context
  //           (isSecureContext === true), yet create() rejects with SecurityError, because
  //           Chromium gates public-key credentials on an origin allowlist (https, localhost,
  //           extension) and not on the secure-context test (measured 2026-09-01). §Trust
  //           Stance keeps the ceremony and its PRF output out of the renderer regardless.
  //           Main drives it per-platform (§WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module)
  //           No ceremony parameter crosses this bridge (I-021-16, re-derived 2026-09-01).
  //           Main obtains the server-issued options — rpId, origin, challenge, and the
  //           outstanding transaction id — from the control plane over its own channel
  //           (uncredentialed on the sign-in path, which is pre-authentication by
  //           construction), validates them against the **pairing-pinned** control-plane
  //           origin — the origin this install was bound to, never a build constant, or a
  //           self-hosted operator domain would be refused before any authenticator is
  //           invoked (§WebAuthn Credential Flow; corrected 2026-09-01, Codex round 7) —
  //           and only then invokes a native binding. The renderer asks for a ceremony and learns its
  //           outcome; the assertion, the PRF output, and every key derived from them stay
  //           in main (I-021-1). The prior shape took caller-supplied `options` and returned
  //           `deriveKeyMaterial` bytes, which let the untrusted renderer choose the
  //           challenge and the RP and handed it derived key material — both withdrawn.
  webAuthn: {
    register(): Promise<WebAuthnCeremonyOutcome>;
    signIn(): Promise<WebAuthnCeremonyOutcome>;
  };

  // auto-update — renderer observes state; main process drives
  update: {
    getState(): Promise<UpdateState>;
    subscribe(handler: (state: UpdateState) => void): Unsubscribe;
    requestCheck(): Promise<void>;
    requestRestart(): Promise<void>;
  };

  // app meta — read-only
  app: {
    version: string;
    platform: "darwin" | "linux" | "win32";
    arch: "arm64" | "x64";
    locale: string;
  };
}
```

**What each region of the console reads.** Every fact on a console surface arrives through `daemon.call` or `daemon.subscribe` and is rendered as received. The session workspace reads the session's identity, shape (project or chat), project, worktree and base, status, elapsed, ahead count, snapshot count, goal, pending worktree move, the unsent draft and its staged files, and the spend rows of the session cost receipt's per-account axis. The transcript reads the user's turns, the agent's prose, its reasoning, and the state-changing rows the console itself appends, and per tool run its verb, target, live elapsed or final duration, result summary, diff hunks, failure mark and held mark. The sidekicks pane reads each child's identity, parent, model, how it was reached, state, activity, tools, tokens, spend, timer and transcript rows. The approvals card reads the one pending request with its title, summary, raising child and three answers. The worktree switcher reads the per-project list with its ahead, dirty and occupancy figures, the root branch and setup progress. The `diff` pane reads the scope tabs, the base list, the files with their hunks, the commits, the pull-request state, its checks and threads, the held notes and the staleness signal. The `terminal` pane reads the session's shell output and each shell's control lease. The working line reads the running commands of the session — identity, command text, folder, start, the tool row each belongs to, output as it prints, and the end with its result and duration — the tokens received this turn, and the turn's task list. The `browser` pane reads the open page's address, title and whether its history has anywhere to go, the discovered development servers with their port and framework where one is known, which machine the page runs on, and the staged marks. The rail's attention count and the notification surface read one attention projection, and the shell reads the same projection for the count outside the window.

**Where the embedded browser's calls go.** The session's page verbs — list the open pages, open one, close one, navigate (which also serves back, forward and reload), set a page's zoom factor, subscribe to the discovered development servers, send the marks attachment, and subscribe to the screencast, which is another device's path only — are daemon methods under `preview.*`, reached through `daemon.call` and `daemon.subscribe` like any other. So are the node-wide site-data verbs under `browser.*`, which are what the Browser settings page reads and writes: list the sites with saved data, open one site so the person signs in to it in the `browser` pane, sign one site out, forget one site, clear all of it, and write the two node-wide browser settings. None of them is registered today, so each is a row on Plan-021's console growth slate and each resolves to the growth port's typed refusal until it lands. The `browser` bridge namespace above is a different surface with a different owner: it is main-process work on this machine's own pages, and it carries no session state. The agent's own browser tools are on neither surface — they are a tool server the daemon hosts and the provider process reaches over loopback HTTP, and the renderer learns of a browser act exactly as it learns of any tool call, as a row in the transcript.

The bridge must not expose:

- raw `ipcRenderer` or `ipcMain`
- `require`, `process`, `global`, or any Node built-in
- auth material (daemon session token, PASETO tokens — including the R3-issued relay `connectionToken`, a PASETO v4.public `aud=relay-connect` credential — DPoP key, WebAuthn PRF output) in any form — PRF output is derived and consumed inside main-process-owned caches; the relay `connectionToken` is likewise main-process-confined (relay negotiation runs main-owned and consumes it to open the relay WSS — see §Interfaces And Contracts), never returned across the bridge
- arbitrary file paths as strings — path-as-capability never crosses the bridge as a raw string: every NATIVE/file-system OPERATION the renderer requests takes or returns opaque `FilePathRef` tokens (`revealInFileExplorer(path: FilePathRef)`), and dereferencing a token requires a second main-process round trip. Display-only path VALUES carried inside daemon- or control-plane-owned payload data (e.g. Spec-007's `canonicalRoot` / `fsRoot` repo-and-workspace fields, which the repo-attach renderer renders verbatim so a user can verify what was attached) are data, not capabilities: the renderer may render them as text, but no bridge surface accepts a raw path string for any OS or filesystem action. Renderer-SUPPLIED path strings inside daemon request payloads (Spec-007's user-entered `RepoAttachRequest.localPath`) are permitted as data for the same reason; the daemon's trust-envelope validation ([Spec-007 §Required Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#required-behavior)) is the enforcement point, never the renderer. _(Amended by the Tier-5 plan-readiness audit, Plan-007 Phase 4 walk — co-recorded for the Plan-021 Tier-7 audit.)_

> **Parameterized daemon subscriptions — the `daemon.subscribe` signature is a Tier-1 placeholder (pin).** The generic `daemon.subscribe<E>(event, handler)` above carries an event name and a handler but **no request-parameter channel**, so it is incomplete for any daemon subscription that needs per-subscription parameters. `presence.subscribe` — the device-liveness subscription — is the concrete case: the daemon **requires** a `{ sessionId }` request body for it — `PresenceSubscribeRequestSchema` (`packages/contracts/src/presence.ts`) is `z.object({ sessionId }).strict()`, the runtime-daemon `presence.subscribe` handler validates that schema before dispatch, and the client SDK's `subscribePresence` already sends `{ sessionId }` on the wire. The generic signature cannot express that today (contrast `controlPlane.subscribeRelay(sessionId, handler)`, the one subscribe that already threads a parameter). **Decision pinned:** the daemon-subscribe bridge surface MUST be able to carry each subscription's request parameters; the current param-less signature is a placeholder, not the target. **Deferred to [Plan-006: Local IPC And Daemon Control](../plans/006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md):** the signature **shape** — positional param vs options bag vs an event→params/payload map — is owned by the plans that narrow `DaemonEvent` / `DaemonParams` / `DaemonEventPayload` (today Tier-1 `never`-shaped brands in `packages/contracts/src/desktop-bridge.ts`) across **all** daemon methods at once. Fixing the subscribe-params shape here from a presence-only vantage would be premature abstraction that conflicts with that holistic narrowing. This clarifies the flow and pins the requirement, not the data type.

### Main Process Responsibilities

- **App lifecycle.** Single-instance lock (`app.requestSingleInstanceLock()`), keyed to the install so a development build and a shipped build never collide; launching the console again brings the window that is already open to the front rather than opening a second copy. Graceful shutdown on `before-quit` — signal the daemon to flush, wait up to a 10-second budget, then force-terminate. Relaunch on update apply. Closing the last window quits the app on Windows and Linux and leaves it running on macOS, where the menu bar and the menu-bar icon stay behind and a click on the dock icon brings the window back to the same warm daemon; `⌘Q` quits there. The daemon flush runs before quit on every platform, and a daemon started from the command line is untouched either way. Quitting ends no shell: the daemon keeps every open shell running, so a server started in a terminal tab is still serving after a relaunch. No banner, tray string or console surface states any of this.
- **Window management.** One main session window, a `BaseWindow` hosting the renderer as a `WebContentsView` per §Security Hardening Baseline. Two auxiliary windows — the full-screen transcript and the detached sidekicks pane — each the same shape, with its own preload and its own bridge instance and sharing no in-memory store with the main window (§Auxiliary windows); both ship in V1. The window opens centred at a size taken from the display's own work area and refuses to shrink below a floor summed from the parts that must fit at once — the rail, the sessions track, the transcript's own floor and one pane's floor — beside the vertical floor the header, the transcript and the composer still stack in; both floors are recomputed from those same parts whenever the text size changes, so neither is a written figure. Platform-appropriate menu bar: an Edit menu whose undo, redo, cut, copy, paste and select-all reach the focused field through the system's own chain, a View menu carrying the three colour-scheme choices with the one in force ticked, and on macOS a Window menu with the platform's own verbs. On macOS the window draws no system title strip — the console fills it, and the rail opens with an inset above its first destination as deep as the three window buttons and the platform's own offset need, absent in fullscreen; on Windows and Linux the system strip stays with its own buttons and the rail has no inset. The rail and the session header are the window's drag regions, with every control inside them cut out of the drag region so pressing one presses it rather than dragging or zooming the window. Fullscreen is the platform's own — its window button, its double-click on the drag region, its menu row — and the console binds no chord to it. The window title is one product string wherever a strip is drawn, carries no session name, and on macOS is set but not shown. Tray icon with status (connected / offline / update-available).
- **Native dialog surface.** File open/save, message boxes, system notifications — exposed to renderer via the bridge above.
- **Notifications.** Cross-platform via `Notification` API. Must honor OS Do-Not-Disturb. Click-to-focus must surface the window and navigate to the event source. A notification is posted only while the app is not in front, whichever console screen is showing, and only for the kinds the attention projection carries; it says the thing's name and its state and never what was said. Each one carries the stable moment identifier the projection supplies, so a session that moves from waiting to finished while nobody is looking replaces its banner in place instead of standing a second one beside it, and the banner is withdrawn when its moment resolves. Moments that resolve together are merged by the thing they belong to, so one session is named once and two different sessions still get their own. The app icon outside the window carries the same count the console's own attention control carries — the dock icon on macOS and Linux, a number drawn onto the taskbar icon on Windows — read from that one projection so the two can never disagree, counting only what waits on the person right now, and absent rather than a zero when nothing does. Two settings govern both, each on by default: the count on the app icon, and notifying outside the app.
- **Deep-link handling.** The shell must register a protocol handler for `sidekicks://` on all three platforms. On invocation, the shell parses the URL in the main process and routes the renderer to the addressed surface. Any credential-bearing component of the URL stays in the main process and is never handed to the renderer — it is exchanged for a session capability in main, and the renderer receives only the resulting navigation target. Every session has exactly one address, `sidekicks://session/<id>`, carrying the session identifier and nothing else — no device, no account, no workspace, no token — so nothing a person can copy, screenshot or find in a log carries a secret; a window's page is handed its credential by the process that owns the window instead. The address is untrusted input from any application on the machine: main parses it and acts on it only by navigating. Opening one brings the app forward and performs the whole screen switch a press on that session's row performs; with no app running the platform starts it and the address is answered once the session list has loaded; an address this install's list does not hold says so in one line rather than opening anything. `sidekicks open <address>` hands the same address to the running app through the platform's own registered link type, so the command does exactly what following the link does, adds no second path into the app, and carries nothing the address does not.
- **Daemon supervision.** Start, stop, restart, and monitor the local daemon via Spec-006's `DaemonStart`/`DaemonStop`/`DaemonRestart`/`DaemonStatusRead` surface. Crash detection triggers automatic restart with exponential backoff (`100ms, 300ms, 1s, 3s, 10s`) up to five attempts, then surfaces a persistent UI error state. Version mismatch (per [Spec-006 §Fallback Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#fallback-behavior)) blocks mutating operations but preserves read-only visibility.
- **Auto-update.** Scheduled checks against the configured feed. Download, verify signature, stage, and apply on relaunch. Surface update state to the renderer via the `update` bridge channel. Rollback on signature-verification failure or post-install daemon handshake failure.
- **OS keystore access.** Store and retrieve the PASETO refresh token, the daemon session token (cached between daemon restarts for fast handshake), and any user-identity private keys. Encrypted at rest via OS primitives (macOS Keychain, Windows Credential Manager, Linux Secret Service / libsecret with KWallet fallback). A provider account's pasted token is sealed here and nowhere else: it is masked as it is entered, never read back to any surface, cleared from the form when it is submitted, and is in neither the machine's settings file nor the renderer's own store. The keystore is the only home for credential material of any kind — no token, key or header value is written to either of the other two.
- **WebAuthn orchestration.** Drive the WebAuthn create and get ceremonies on behalf of the renderer through the per-platform main-process binding of §WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module — macOS through Apple `AuthenticationServices`, Windows through the OS `webauthn.dll` surface, Linux through `libfido2` — and never through the renderer's `navigator.credentials.*` (corrected 2026-09-01; the prior wording said "via Electron's WebAuthn bindings", which names an API that does not exist for this path). Resolve the typed `WebAuthnCeremonyOutcome` of §WebAuthn Credential Flow through the bridge and **never the assertion** (corrected 2026-09-01, Codex round 2 — this bullet previously read "Return the authenticator assertion through the bridge", which contradicts §Trust Stance and the §Preload Bridge Contract prohibition three subsections above it): the attestation object, the assertion signature, and the client-data bytes are submitted for verification by the main process over its own authenticated control-plane channel, so no part of them, and no handle to them, reaches the renderer. Handle the PRF extension per ADR-010 and pass derived key material into the main-process-owned credential-wrap cache — never into the renderer.
- **Crash reporting.** Electron `crashReporter` for main and renderer process crashes. Supervised daemon crashes surface through the Spec-006 supervision contract. All crash payloads must strip PII (session IDs replaced with stable hashes; file paths truncated to extension; no content payloads).

### Renderer Responsibilities

- Render session, orchestration, repo, diff, approval, settings, and workflow-viewer surfaces
- **Own one region each.** Every region of the console has exactly one owning family — a view family inside one of the surface families this section enumerates — and a family sits at its own place in the console family layering §Console Test Tiers fixes: the rail, its destinations, the colour-scheme control and the route into Settings belong to the frame and routing families; the all-sessions list with its groups, its picker and its search to the sessions family and the sidebar's seat sections; the session header, its chips and its menu to the frame family; the transcript flow and every row kind in it to the ledger family; the composer, its command list, its send control, its working line and its context ring to the composer family the shell registers; the approvals card to the approval family; the worktree switcher, its setup card and the ship strip to the repo and workspace families; the inspector to the inspector family; the review surface and the read-only file view to the family that draws the `diff` pane kind; the sidekicks pane, its child tree and its child view to the orchestration family that draws the `agent-console` pane; the shell surface to the terminal family; the previewed page, its acts and its marks to the browser family that draws the `browser` pane; tokens, themes and motion to the tokens and primitives families; and every read, subscription and fixture scenario to the bridge and store families. Two families never draw one region.
- **Derive presentation and nothing else.** The daemon owns every fact: run state, tool runs, child state and spend, approval requests and their resolution, worktree occupancy with its dirty and ahead counts, diff and pull-request state, tree staleness, the per-project running / waiting / done tally, and the running-child count behind the sidekicks badge. The renderer derives only presentation — which pane is open, pane widths, hover and focus state, the ship strip's one next action as a pure function of the daemon's uncommitted / ahead / pushed / pull-request facts, and the elapsed readings it holds in its own model rather than reading back out of the drawn page.
- Merge live projections from daemon subscriptions with control-plane subscriptions into a coherent session experience (per `component-architecture-desktop-app.md` §Data Flow)
- Route all privileged operations through the preload bridge
- Never cache auth material (the bridge enforces this; renderer code treats every call as authenticated-by-main)
- Handle disconnection states gracefully: daemon disconnect → reconnect or read-only mode per [Spec-006 §Fallback Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#fallback-behavior); control-plane disconnect → the session keeps running against the local daemon with cross-device sync degraded
- Render every surface in the Meridian design language, inside the console surface set, and under the console rules of `§Console Design (Meridian)`: a control whose capability the bound driver does not declare is absent rather than disabled, a control the daemon may refuse is offered with its typed refusal rendered, and no read runs on an interval

### Console Design (Meridian)

_This subsection fixes the console's design language, its surface set, its pane and window model, its fixture bridge, its budgets, and what each surface shows, offers and refuses. What stays with Plan-021's console registration is the code that draws each surface, its fixture scenarios and its own density budget. Every wire call a surface makes names a method registered by that method's owning plan, or a growth-slate row until one is._

#### The four bars

- **Richness.** The console carries a signature set no chat client has: handoff threads drawn as structure, approval moments that leave visible residue, boundary seams for provider switch, compaction, and rollback, an honest park banner, and an all-clear state. The first sixty seconds and the flagship frame are designed compositions, reviewed on their own captures rather than gated on pixels (§Console Test Tiers).
- **Elegance.** Every surface names its single job and its density budget — what is collapsed by default and what is one click away. No surface carries two visible controls for one act. Attention is steered by luminance and the two-hue rule, never by motion.
- **Zero copy.** Mechanics are adopted from the field; skin, names, and copy are ours.
- **Light on the machine.** Richness is paid for in design, never in RAM or CPU. Nothing polls: every refresh is event-driven through one scheduler with an absolute deadline. Every cap, window, and timeout is a named constant with a one-line rationale, and every subsystem states its memory floor. Heavy work — highlighting, diff compute, graph layout, markdown for long messages — runs off the main thread or lazily, and streaming paints through a bounded reveal budget so four concurrent lanes cost one frame. A library is admitted only after its bytes, heap, and frame cost are measured against an own build, and the budgets below are measured from the first commit.

#### Meridian, the design language

1. **Ledger rows.** The transcript is one continuous flow — no card, no panel and no framed container around a turn — and role is carried by asymmetry alone ([ADR-034 §Decision](../decisions/034-transcript-is-one-flow.md#decision)). The user's words sit in a tinted, right-aligned bubble with an asymmetric corner and no width cap, sized to its own text, well inside the column when short and out to the column's own edge when long. The agent's words are flat prose wrapping at that same edge, followed by a foot carrying the reply's stamp and a hover-revealed copy control rather than a row of its own. Every other row is a flush-left ledger line: a tool call is a line and never a card, with a verb column as wide as the longest verb it must hold, the target, and a trailing cell; an act row names the act and never the actor. There is no left-and-right alternation, no avatar, no name label, no eyebrow, and no identity mark of any kind for the one user. Rows sit in a centred column with a gutter either side, one derived gap apart whatever kind they are, and no row kind carries a margin of its own. The screen reads as a work log because it is one.
2. **The actor hue system.** Each actor in a session — you, each agent, and the system — takes a deterministic hue from a twelve-step OKLCH wheel at fixed lightness and chroma. **Allocation is total and order-independent (Codex round 3):** actors are ordered by the position at which they enter the session event log — the order every client replays identically — and each takes its preferred step (a hash of the actor id modulo twelve), a taken step resolving to the next free step clockwise in that same order; the thirteenth and later actors wrap onto the wheel at their preferred step with collisions allowed, distinguished by the initials glyph and ring pattern, which is why the hue is never the sole attribution channel. An actor keeps its hue for the life of the session and a detach frees nothing, so a later attach cannot swap two existing hues. The hue answers "who" everywhere — attribution edges, pane focus rings, diff-gutter attribution, handoff ticks. Hues are never used for attention.
3. **The two-hue rule.** Amber means you are needed. Red means something failed. Nothing else is colored for attention; the brand accent is one desaturated cyan used only on interactive affordances. A view with nothing amber and nothing red is, by construction, a view that needs nothing from you. **Contrast floors, enforced at the token level on both schemes (WCAG 2.2 AA):** body and UI text ≥ 4.5:1 against its ground and large text (≥ 24 px, or ≥ 18.66 px bold) ≥ 3:1; non-text controls, their boundaries, and every focus ring ≥ 3:1 against adjacent colors; the twelve actor hues are generated at a lightness that clears 3:1 as edges, rings, and marks on both grounds and are never used as text; amber and red each carry a text variant that clears 4.5:1 and a mark variant that clears 3:1 on both grounds; the cyan accent clears 3:1 as an affordance and 4.5:1 wherever it colors text. **Two themes, two schemes, one contract.** The console ships two themes — Meridian, the default, and Graphite — each drawn in a light and a dark scheme. The theme and the scheme are persisted and restored before the first paint, the window's own background is painted from the theme before the first frame so the frame never comes up the wrong colour, and the platform is told which appearance the window is in so its own window buttons and system strip are drawn to match; both follow the theme when it changes and follow the system while the scheme is set to follow it. The scheme is reachable three ways that all set the one value: the rail's control cycles follow-the-system, dark, light; the Appearance settings page carries the three side by side; and the platform's View menu carries the same three with the one in force ticked. A press on a theme swatch previews that theme live, with no restart and no reload, and the Appearance page draws the three scheme choices as cards, each painting a small live picture of the console from the tokens the chosen theme is using at that moment — the follow-the-system card painting its picture split, light on one half and dark on the other — so a picture can never disagree with the screen; the chosen card carries the accent on its edge and on its name, and choosing a theme repaints all three at once. Every component reads tokens and no literal colour appears anywhere in the console tree; the token contract is the union of both themes' needs and is proven at run time against the running console, never by parsing a theme file. Layout, behaviour and copy are identical under every theme and scheme, and the status indicators stay static under both. **The check asserts the floors rather than restating them:** in each of the four renderings — two themes by light and dark — text against the surface it sits on reaches 4.5:1, the accent against the surface it sits on reaches 3:1, and words on the accent reach 4.5:1 against it; a token edit that drops a pair below its floor fails the check, which names the rendering, the pair and the ratio it measured. The accessibility tier asserts every token pair against these ratios and fails on the first miss. **One switch draws the console flat.** The Appearance page carries a screen-reader simplification, off by default; with it on the panes lose their decorative borders and washes, no animation or transition runs anywhere, a tool call reads as one plain line of words rather than as a glyph, a column and a cell, the transcript reads as headings and paragraphs in order, and each provider is told the person is using a screen reader wherever that provider takes it as a setting.
4. **Type and figures.** UI text is a humanist grotesque; every wire-true figure — costs, counts, SHAs, durations, token totals, timestamps — renders in mono — verbatim as a string, `Intl`-formatted from the exact value as a quantity (the wire-figures rule under §Rules every console surface obeys). Mono is the signature that a number came from the wire; prose never paraphrases a figure. The faces are IBM Plex Sans and IBM Plex Mono, variable builds self-hosted from the foundry packages with the slashed zero and tabular figures enabled, and both ship inside the app, so nothing is fetched to draw the interface, no family is ever chosen, and there is no substitute to announce. Numbers that sit in a column are tabular. A live figure under sixty seconds reads in whole seconds and at or above it as minutes and seconds. Every clock figure is written the way the machine's own clock writes it — on a machine set to a twelve-hour clock, `2:20 PM`, `Mon 9:58 AM`, `Yesterday 5:31 PM`, and never `14:20` — with the day word in front of the time and seconds only where the figure already carried them. Text truncates with an ellipsis and keeps its full value in its own hover title; it never wraps inside a fixed-height row. **One scale, one root.** Every text size and line height in the console's own styles comes from the type scale and nowhere else, and every width, floor and ceiling of the chrome that carries them — the rail, the sessions track, the side panes, the transcript's measure, its gutters, its row gap, and every pane's default and floor — is root-relative and derived from what it holds; only the hairlines, the corner radii and the icon strokes stay in pixels. The Appearance settings page's `Text size` offers four steps — `Small · Default · Large · Larger` — driving the root size through four values, 15, 16, 18 and 20 px, the default a 16 px root carrying 14 px body text, so a step grows the reading matter and the chrome in one proportion and leaves what is drawn as drawn. No chord steps it, and a step re-measures every drawn transcript row and drops the height remembered for every row that is not drawn, so the rows grow in place and the row being read stays under the reader's eye. **One width for every transcript.** The same page's transcript-width slider sets the single measure every session's transcript reads at, kept as a multiple of the root size the way every other measure that carries content is, so it steps with the text size and a line of prose holds its measure at all four steps. The figure beside the slider is what the value gives at the text size in force and is read again at every step; the default is the reading measure the transcript already holds, 57.5 root sizes, about 920 px at the default text size; the floor is the narrowest a transcript row still reads at, where a tool row still fits its verb column, its target and its timing on one line, 35.5 root sizes; the ceiling is the widest a line of the agent's prose still reads comfortably at the largest text size, 74.5 root sizes; and one step of the slider is one root size. The value is published as one root token in root-relative units and every session reads its column's width from that token and from nothing else, so every session follows it at once, no session overrides it, and there is no second value per text size.
5. **Motion.** Settles, never bounces: 120–180 ms ease-out, entrances fade with a 2 px rise, zero overshoot in chrome; attribution threads draw with a 240 ms line-grow. Hover and press take the quick step, the grid and the flyout the settle step, an approval's fade the thread step. Motion is platform-native (CSS transitions, `@starting-style`, View Transitions, the Web Animations API) with an own spring sampler emitting `linear()` easings; no animation library sits on the render path. **Live figures move on one beat.** One ticker advances every live figure on the screen, beating once a second, which is the finest figure any of them shows; there is no per-row interval, every clock's value lives in the model and is never read back out of the page, a figure that finishes freezes in place rather than being replaced, and the duration cell holds a width floor so nothing shifts when a call ends. A timer is never tinted — not amber while waiting, not accent while running — and a status indicator never blinks: the live timer is what says an agent is working. **Streaming settles rather than animates.** A reply's first word is on the screen the moment the runtime has it, with no lead-in; the reveal runs word by word at the pace the words arrive, catching up at no more than twice that pace when the wire runs ahead, and keeps draining after the turn closes so nothing is left unshown. A word never lands half-drawn, and arriving text changes only its opacity, so a line filling in never reflows the lines above it and settled text never shifts. Returning to a session draws everything that arrived while the reader was away as settled text at full strength; only words arriving from that moment on animate. **The follow is the reader's.** The transcript follows the bottom only while the reader is within a couple of lines of it; a scroll away releases the follow at once, however small, and it re-engages when the reader returns to that band or presses the jump control. While the follow is on, the page glides after arriving text rather than jumping, and a layout correction — a picture that typesets late, a row that remeasures, a reflow at a new width — is placed at once instead. A drag on a pane edge, and a selection being dragged inside the transcript, hold the follow until the pointer is released. **Nothing animates unwatched.** Motion runs only where something changes under the reader's eye, is armed by an act rather than by a scroll, and is placed at once in a region that is not in view. `prefers-reduced-motion` removes every animation and transition on every element without exception, places everything at once, and resolves a stream straight to the full text — which is the one case a switch would have served, so there is no setting that turns streaming off and no second rendering of a reply.
6. **Copy.** Calm authority: sentence case, past-tense receipts, no exclamation marks, no celebration copy. Refusals name the actor and the rule; absences name their kind and their escape hatch. No copy claims a capability the code does not implement, and no copy names a vendor as a product feature. **The word a person reads is sidekick, never agent.** The types, methods and events keep their own names and the rule reaches only what is on the screen; two things are the provider's own spelling and stay as it spells them — the mention syntax a provider offers for addressing one of its own agents, and a command name drawn from that provider's own list. **The screen never shows the wire's own spelling.** An error code, a refusal reason, a status, an event kind, a tool name, a policy action or a node kind reaches the eye as words in sentence case, followed by the daemon's own message in plain words; each tool and each policy action registers the label a person reads, a node shows its kind's display name, and an unlabelled code falls back to its own words in sentence case. Only what a person or an agent wrote or would type stays as written — model identifiers, file names and paths, commands, diffs, terminal output and prose — and the mono face is what marks it as written. An account is named only by the identity its provider reports when it signed in: the email address that provider reports, then the plan in the provider's own word, then the organisation where the plan has one — never a label the console or the user invented. **Words live in one place.** Every string a person reads is declared once and read from there; no component writes its words inline, and because every row is sized by what it holds, a longer wording fits wherever it comes from. There is no welcome wizard, tour, checklist or dismissible tip anywhere: a surface with nothing in it says so in its own words and offers exactly one next act, named against what the session is bound to.
7. **Density budgets.** Tool rows render as one line until opened; run chapters collapse once terminal and the live chapter stays open; diff cards expand to a height cap and then offer "show all"; sidebar sections stay collapsed unless they carry an amber or red item; the inspector shows one entity at a time; settings is two panes, one page at a time; secondary controls live one click away — a row's hover footer or its context menu — never as a second visible button.
8. **Kinds of nothing.** Five absences render differently because the operator's next move differs for each: _not loaded_ (a skeleton in the row's shape), _empty_ (a quiet line with the escape hatch), _error_ (a red-edged row carrying the reason in words with the daemon's own message under it), _not checked_ (a dotted badge), and _unknown, still computing_ (a badge with a clock glyph). A renderer that collapses two of these into one is wrong. **An empty surface names one next act.** The empty transcript says there are no messages yet and invites the first, naming what the session is bound to; a list with nothing in it says so and names the one control that fills it; a search that matched nothing says what it searched for and keeps its group headings drawn. **A subject that goes takes its surface with it, and says so.** A pane whose subject no longer exists closes itself, and a one-line note between the header and the surface says which pane closed and why; the note outlives the chip that went with it, and opening any pane or leaving the session answers it. A session whose remembered pane has lost its subject opens no pane and says nothing, because nothing closed under the reader. **A region that fails says so where it stands.** One line naming that region with a retry beside it, in the slot a pane uses to say why it closed, while the transcript and every other region keep updating; a press draws the region again. A read the daemon does not answer keeps the rows a list already had rather than emptying it, says in amber on the list's own header that it could not be refreshed, and counts no figure it could not read until the next answer lands. **Failure is the only red.** A failed call is marked; a call that succeeded carries no tick, no "done" and no green, because success is shown by absence. Failure is read from the run's own durable record, so a restart replays the same answer and a failed run still reads failed in the morning, and a refusal a person made is not drawn in the waiting colour — it is a row whose reason says the person declined.
9. **The refusal grammar.** Controls are offered; refusals are rendered, in one of three shapes — **inline** on the control that was pressed (the reason in words, the daemon's own message under it, the next move when one exists), as a **card** in the ledger when the refusal changes history, or as a **banner** across the workspace when it changes what the whole session can do. A refusal never hides the control that produced it and never re-derives the daemon's rule. An inline refusal is visible words on the surface that asked, at that surface's own width, on one line under the control that was pressed; it stays until the next act and is never carried in a hover title alone. A whole dimmed list carries its reason once in its header, in amber, and again on every row, and a control greyed by one of the named exceptions §Rules every console surface obeys lists carries its reason in its own hover title. A banner sits directly under the session header, above the pane note and the surface, carries its own dismissal, blocks nothing and refuses nothing, and enters no attention count; a dismissal is for that occurrence alone, so the same condition arising again draws it again. A destructive act is confirmed in place, inline where it was started, saying in plain words what it removes and whether it can be undone before it offers the act; it is never a dialog and never switchable off.

**Layout grammar.** The app frame is three columns: the icon rail at its own derived width, the sessions track, and the screen view taking the rest. The middle track is nothing wide — a zero-width track and not a stub — while it is closed, and while it holds the all-sessions list or the notification list it takes the width the person has dragged it to, and until they have, the width a session row's own furniture needs at the current text size. The grid itself animates, so the screen view slides over rather than being covered, and the track pins its children to its full width while it animates so nothing reflows mid-motion. The one width at which the grid changes shape is the fold to one column, and the content sets that point — the transcript's own floor against what the track and an open pane need at the current text size — never a device class, a build or a list of devices, so a desktop window dragged narrow folds exactly as a phone-sized window does. The vertical floor is the shortest window in which the header, the transcript and the composer still stack.

The screen view is seven tracks — the session column, the inspector, the review surface, the sidekicks pane, the terminal, the browser pane and the file view — and exactly one of the six beside the session column stands open at a time, or none of them, the terminal able to stack beneath whichever one is up. The column is two rows: the session column spans both, a main pane takes the upper row of its own track, and a stacked terminal opens the lower row and spans the pane tracks, so it is exactly as wide as the pane above it. Inside the session track the column is the session header, then the surface, then the docked composer. Every pane carries an attribution-colored focus ring and an entity breadcrumb with a kind glyph. The inspector is a pane kind, not a fixed third column.

The command palette opens over the session column on a light scrim, with its search field focused and empty and the transcript and the composer still running under it; Enter runs the selected row and closes it, and Escape, a press on the scrim and the chord that opened it all close it without running anything. It has categories — each unbroken run of rows under a small heading reading that run's own category, the best-placed row's category leading and the categories not reshuffling between keystrokes, the headings labels the arrows walk straight past so the list stays one flat set of rows — recents, where an act run lately carries a recent mark and an empty field lists those acts first, newest first and up to eight; a scoped-context row naming what the command acts on; a footer naming the keys it answers with the result count beside it; a ceiling on how many rows one query renders; and one matcher shared with settings search, whose matched letters are marked inside a row's name by weight and brightness on the hue the name already wears rather than by a colour of their own. That one matcher is the Settings screen's search as well: the field above the page list searches three strings per page — the page's own name, its entry's label, and the words that page declares itself findable by — the best of the three winning and ties falling in the list's own order, and it searches the controls inside every page, ranking a hit on the control's own label above the heading it sits under and that above the control's one-line hint, with ties again in page order. Each hit reads the control's label over the page and heading it lives on, the arrows move the highlight, Enter opens the page and lands on the control, and a term nothing matches draws one line naming what was searched for. The list holds every act the open session has, each under the name the screen already gives it — the pane chips, the session's own menu rows, and the composer's level, mode, model, effort and speed controls, a control's row opening that control's own list rather than standing in for it. Five acts live only here: `Copy conversation`, which puts the whole open session on the clipboard in the form the screen reads; `Clear the terminal`, which empties the active shell's own view and its scrollback on screen without sending anything to the shell, so it is safe while a command is running; `Fold every run` and `Unfold every run`, which close and open every run group in the session at once; and `Search all sessions`, which opens the box that searches every session (§The session workspace). `Copy conversation` and a message's own copy are the only ways a transcript leaves this screen: nothing anywhere exports one to a file, prints one, or reads one aloud. An act this session cannot take is absent from the list rather than greyed; an act that exists but is closed right now stays listed with the one sentence its owner wrote for why, and refuses in place when it is pressed.

Iconography is a single-stroke set (Tabler glyphs compiled at build time) plus our own signature glyphs for actors, runs, and provenance kinds in the same collection, drawn at 14 × 14 with a 2.25 px stroke and round caps and joins; the rail's glyphs are the one place they run larger. Borders are hairlines and there are no heavy rules; the radii are exactly 3, 6 and 10 px; elevation is the composer card's glass surface rather than a stack of shadows; each surface carries one filled control and no more; and hover is one derived colour that reveals only affordances whose space is already reserved.

#### Rules every console surface obeys

- **Absent, not disabled.** A control whose capability the bound driver does not declare is not rendered — a disabled control would assert the capability exists and is momentarily unavailable, which would be false. A control the daemon may refuse is rendered and its refusal is shown; eligibility is never projected by the renderer — the `I-003-24` fail-closed-projection discipline, generalized. The rule reaches the menus, the composer's command list and its controls, the pickers, the session strip and the rail, and it has three named sets of exceptions, each greyed in place with its reason in its own hover title and each answering no press. **Three single controls** whose subject is a choice the person can change beside them: the review surface's approve verdict on a pull request the user authored, the composer's output-speed control on a session whose chosen model has no fast output, and the `browser` pane's back and forward, whose subject the person changes by following a link. **The session header's three pane chips**, which stand at every moment on either session shape and grey when they cannot apply, because the header is the one place a person's eye returns to and a chip that comes and goes cannot be learned. **And two rows that stand inside a list their neighbours explain**: the shell tab menu's close-others and close-to-the-right where there is no such tab, and the worktree create form's base list on a branch another worktree already has checked out.
- **Offer, then render the refusal.** The renderer never pre-denies; it calls, and renders the typed refusal code with the daemon's message text and the operator's next move.
- **One accountant.** Every cost figure comes from the committed-spend read surface ([Spec-014 §Cost Figure Display Consistency](014-multi-agent-channels-and-orchestration.md#cost-figure-display-consistency)); the renderer never sums visible rows, never labels a figure a lower bound, never totals across sessions. A figure the daemon has not supplied is not counted in the renderer at all, and a gate whose input is missing refuses rather than guessing.
- **Write-only credentials.** A credential-bearing input appears on no reply and is never echoed ([ADR-028 §Decision](../decisions/028-provider-credential-custody-posture.md#decision); the Provider Accounts and MCP Servers sketches below).
- **Fail-closed projection.** An unknown enum member renders as the explicit unrecognized row or badge, never as a guess.
- **Wire figures are verbatim; wire quantities are formatted from the exact value.** Two classes, decided by the value's kind and never by the surface. **Byte-for-byte strings** — roots and paths, SHAs and digests, identifiers, versions, model names, commands, diffs, terminal output, provider-supplied labels and message text, and any cost or count the wire supplies as a string — render exactly as received, in mono, never re-parsed. A wire **name** is not a figure and is not in this class: an error code, a refusal reason, a status, an event kind, a tool name, a policy action or a node kind reaches the eye as words, under item 6 of §Meridian, the design language. **Formatted quantities** — a numeric duration, byte count, token count, percentage, rate, or timestamp, and a cost the wire supplies as a number beside its currency — render through `Intl` (`DurationFormat`, `NumberFormat` in unit, currency, and compact styles, `RelativeTimeFormat`) in the renderer locale, in mono, from the exact wire value with no client-side arithmetic beyond the format itself — the one exception being byte quantities, which the console's single shared formatter scales by powers of 1024 and labels from the closed set `B / KiB / MiB / GiB / TiB` (`Intl` has no binary unit), the scaled number still passing through `NumberFormat`, `apps/desktop/AGENTS.md` §Chokepoints holding that formatter as the only site that scales, with the `wire-figure-formatting` runtime tripwire firing on a figure rendered outside the two classes: currency at the wire's own precision and never fewer than two fractional digits, bytes in binary units, durations digital at one minute and above and `1.2 s`-style below, relative times always paired with the absolute timestamp on hover, and compact numbers only where the exact figure is one hover away — the exact wire value is exposed in the element's `title` and in the inspector, so no formatted figure hides the number the daemon sent.
- **No interval polling.** Reads happen on subscribe, on window focus, on reconnect, and on the terminal events the owning spec names — through one refresh scheduler firing at `min(lastEvent + delay, firstEvent + maxWait)`, serialized, so a trailing debounce cannot starve under a stream.
- **Reserved, not stubbed.** A capability-gated feature the corpus reserves (realtime voice channels, [ADR-015](../decisions/015-v1-feature-scope-definition.md) feature 23) gets no surface, no placeholder, and no setting until the capability exists.
- **No control ships inert.** Every control the console draws is real and must work, and a fix lands on every surface, session and state that shares the part it repaired, confirmed on each rather than assumed.
- **One way in, one way out.** A menu or pop-over anchors to the control that opened it, flips to the other side when it would leave the window and clamps to the window's edge so every row of it stays reachable by the pointer, and closes on an outside press and on Escape and on nothing else; closing it returns focus to the control that opened it, however it closed. Every menu and list answers the same keys — the arrows move one cursor, home and end jump to the first and last row, Enter presses the row the cursor is on, Escape closes, rows that cannot act are skipped, the first row takes the cursor when the list opens, and no letter key moves the cursor, because the field keeps the caret. Transient statuses — an opening line, a copied line, the copy flash — all clear on one fixed duration.
- **Nothing forces a state by hand.** No switch, flag or hidden control anywhere in the console stages an attachment, opens a picker, slows a list or forces a state. The demo dataset and the developer tools are build-only and are compiled out of a release build (§The fixture bridge), so nothing in a shipped console can make scripted data look like real work.

#### The surface set

##### The icon rail

Five destinations and two controls. The destinations are Sessions, Sidekicks, Skills, Workflows and Settings, in that order ([ADR-031 §Decision](../decisions/031-five-rail-destinations.md#decision)), each a glyph with a text label on hover and in its accessible name; the rail runs the first four, then a spacer, then the attention control and the colour-scheme control, then Settings. It is a bare sunken column of square hit areas, each as wide as its glyph plus the press target a pointer or a fingertip needs around it and growing with the root text size, with no cards, no chips and no panels. The destination the person is on draws a 2 px accent spine in the rail's own padding, outside the button. Rail glyphs are the one place a glyph runs larger than the console's own size, and they come from the console's own collection. Every rail button carries a hover title naming its destination; an icon-only control carries one string that is both its hover title and its spoken name, with no second tooltip surface and no delay before it shows. The Sessions button's hint ends in the chord that opens the list, read from that act's own row on the Keyboard page each time it is drawn, so a rebinding changes the hint and the suffix is absent while the act holds no chord.

**Each destination owns its own addresses**, so a deep link and a press on the rail are one act and the back control works. Sessions addresses the all-sessions list and one session's workspace. Sidekicks addresses the definition library, one definition, and a new definition; that screen is [Spec-027](./027-agent-definitions-and-peer-invocation.md)'s and this spec restates none of it. Skills addresses the list, the new-skill form, a folder opened at its entry file, and that folder with one other file open; that screen is [Spec-030 §The destination, the rail and the addresses](./030-skills.md#the-destination-the-rail-and-the-addresses)'s. Workflows addresses the authoring canvas and one run being watched — the `workflow-builder` and `workflow-run` pane kinds [Spec-015](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md) owns. Settings addresses the screen, one page, and a page with a selection. The command palette lists all five in one category of its own, so every destination is reachable from the keyboard as well as from the rail.

The attention control toggles the notification list and reads as expanded while that list is open. It carries the console's one badge: an amber pip, hidden from assistive technology with the count carried in the button's accessible name, absent rather than a zero when nothing waits, taken from the daemon's attention projection and never counted in the renderer. It counts the sessions and workflow runs waiting on a person right now. The colour-scheme control cycles follow-the-system, dark, light. Settings carries one small quiet dot while an update is staged and waiting for the restart — no count, because there is only ever one update, and not the attention colour, because nothing is needed from the person — hidden from assistive technology with the fact in the button's accessible name and gone when the update is applied; what the update is and the press that applies it live on the General settings page. That dot is the one exception to the one-badge rule.

Nothing else is in the rail: no session list inside it, no status text, no branding, no avatar, no second settings entry, and no mark on any other button. The Sessions button carries no mark of its own — a session row's status dot is that row's state, not a notification. Of the seven surface families §Renderer Responsibilities enumerates, four (repo, diff, approval, orchestration) are session-scoped and are reached inside a session through the deck and the sidebar; the rail names the top-level destinations and drops no family. Assistive technology reads the rail as the console's sections.

##### The all-sessions list

One row per session in the sessions track, grouped by shape and by nothing else: chats above every project group, each project one group under a foldable header carrying the daemon's running / waiting / done roll-up, and an archived group at the foot of the scroll under its own header with its count, folded by default and opening the way a project header opens its list. Grouping is the only discriminator — no badge and no second row layout — and a row names neither the provider that runs it nor the machine it runs on; what a session is bound to is read in the inspector. Each attached project is one group and its rows are that project's own sessions: no group stands for two checkouts and no tally is merged across them. The per-project tally, and the chats header's own count, are the daemon's and are never counted in the renderer.

A row is a button of two stacked lines, the second padded to align under the title rather than under the dot: the status dot and the title on the first, then the branch in mono on a project session or the document count on a chat, with the age right-aligned. Neither of those second-line facts sits behind a switch and neither costs a repository read at draw time. An age reads `now` under a minute and then one count in one unit with no "ago", advancing on the console's one shared beat. A status dot has five states the daemon derives, and no surface invents a sixth: running in the accent, waiting on the person in amber, done a hollow ring, failed a red mark, idle a faint grey circle. Its size derives from the row's own text and is a step larger on a project roll-up; each carries a hover title naming its state in one word — working, waiting on you, done, failed, idle — which is also how the row's spoken name ends; and the running and waiting dots step through an opacity ramp rather than blinking. A session that finished while the person was away draws its done dot filled until they open it and hollow from the moment they do, read from the one seen-or-unseen fact the daemon keeps for the notification list, so the row and that list can never disagree. A running or waiting claim the daemon has stopped renewing stops reading as live once it is older than a bound derived from how often the daemon reports: the row falls back to idle and its timer stands still where it froze. A failed reading is never aged out, because it is a fact about the run rather than a claim about right now.

A session with no name renders by its identifier and its attached agents, never by an invented title, and an untitled row's title is faint italic. A session names itself from its first completed exchange — a short throwaway pass reads that exchange and writes a few words, falling back to the first words of the first message where the pass returns nothing — and keeps that name until it is renamed. The name is written only while the session is still unnamed, and a rename made while the pass is running is re-read before anything is written, so a name the person typed always wins; naming a session renames neither its branch nor its worktree. Renaming is an edit in place from the row or the header, saving on Enter and cancelling on Escape, an empty name restoring the first-message title, and the row and the header updating together. A row the person minted keeps its place until they archive or close it: an emptied draft, an unnamed session or a session that never sent anything removes nothing, because the list loses a row only on an act of theirs.

There is one new-session control, dashed at rest and solid on hover or while open, and its picker is the whole of the choice a session is started from: a chat with no repository, then the recent repositories, then the platform's own folder chooser. It offers no machine, because one machine executes, and the console browses no directories of its own.
