Minting a session prepends its row with a faint italic placeholder title and the right second line, an age of now, bumps its group's own count or forces that project's list open, closes the picker, switches the screen to the session and puts the caret in the draft. Opening the sessions track focuses the search field without scrolling the rows, and closing the track closes the picker with it and returns focus to the rail's Sessions button. The repository a session works in is chosen there and only there; a chat that later needs one gains it through the inspector's attach action. Search filters the list in place across every group and never hides a group header, matching a row on its title, its second line and its folder path, ranked so titles that start with the query come first, then titles that contain it, then second lines and paths, with a clear control at the field's right end that restores the whole list and returns focus to the field. A folded project holding a match unfolds while the field has text and folds again when it is cleared, and only the projects the search itself unfolded fold back. A group whose own name matches keeps every session under it. Searching the text inside sessions is a different surface — the box the palette opens — and the track's own field stays a search over the rows in the list.

A press on a row switches the whole screen for real: title, chips, counts, transcript, composer state and panes. The session on screen is marked by one selected wash on its row and by that row reading as the current one to assistive technology, and by nothing else; a hovered row takes the one derived hover colour and no second mark. A right-click — or, where the pointer is coarse, a touch held still — raises that row's own actions at the pointer: the same list the session header's menu holds, built from one list so the two can never disagree, acting on the row it was raised on whichever session is on screen, and switching nothing, because switching stays the row's own press. Where the pointer is coarse the row draws its menu control at rest rather than on hover; editable text is left to the platform; and no gesture over a row archives, closes or deletes anything — a swipe across a row scrolls the list. While a row is hovered or holds focus its age is replaced in place by one word — archive, or unarchive on an archived row — in the slot the age holds, so no row changes height or width, and that is the row's only hover action. An archived row is the ordinary row at a dimmed ink; unarchiving returns it to its own project's list and drops the group's count, and the group and its count go when the last row leaves it. A session can be pinned to the top of its own group from its row's actions and unpinned the same way: one pin and no second tier, pinned rows sitting above the daemon's order inside their group in the order they were pinned.

The list draws only the rows in view, plus a little either side, once the number of sessions runs into the hundreds, and stays flat below that; the threshold is read off the row count and never off a screen size. A group draws its first rows and puts the rest behind one row saying how many more there are, carrying the roll-up dot of the rows it hides; the session on screen is never behind that cut, and never hidden by a fold either — its row is still drawn under a folded project's header at that project's own indent. What a project header remembers is the folds and not the opens, so a project attached tomorrow arrives open and a fold survives a relaunch. When the order actually changes every surviving row glides from where it was to where it now is, a new row fades in and a removed row simply goes; a change of height alone starts no motion and the first fill never animates. Holding the platform's own modifier stamps a small key-cap chip on the first nine rows in the slot the age holds and those chords open them; the chips are there only while the key is held, never at rest, and never while a menu or an overlay owns the keyboard.

Long names truncate and carry the full value in a hover title, and nothing in the list overflows the track's own width. The list fades at whichever end is hiding rows, each end gated on its own, over a ramp derived from a row's own height. Where two attached projects share a name each header grows a dim parent-folder prefix, enough of it to tell them apart, with the full path in its hover title; a prefix never truncates and wraps above the name where the pair will not fit. A newly attached project's header appears at once; a project the daemon cannot attach takes its header away again and says why in one line in the picker that asked, with nothing else in the list redrawn. A refresh the daemon does not answer keeps every row the list already had, says in amber on the list's own header that it could not be refreshed, and counts no figure it could not read until the next answer lands. With no sessions at all the list says so and names the one control that starts one; with a query that matches nothing it says so and keeps its group headers drawn. The list carries a foot line naming the keys it answers, in words. A lifecycle control whose verb is not registered is not drawn; Plan-021's console growth slate names the missing verbs.

##### The session workspace

**The session header** names the session and carries, in order: the title, the pull-request word where a request is open on the branch, the review chip with its added and deleted counts, the preview chip, the terminal control, and the session's own menu. It is as tall as its own controls and the type beside them, identical on every platform, and nothing about the title bar moves it. All three pane chips are drawn at every moment on either session shape, each carrying one fact and never growing a second; one that cannot act reads as unavailable to assistive technology, takes the faint ink, carries its reason in its own hover title, answers no press, and leaves its chord doing nothing. Each pane has exactly one chord, a row on the Keyboard page like every other: the inspector opens on the platform modifier with `I`, the review pane on that modifier and shift with `G`, and the sidekicks pane, the terminal and the browser pane on the same pair with `S`, `T` and `B`; while the review pane's diff holds focus two bare keys step its files, the next on `J` and the previous on `K`, answering neither inside a text field nor with a modifier held. Every one of them is rebindable, and what a chord reads anywhere in the console is the binding in force rather than a written string. On a project session the review chip greys with its counts at zero while the tree is clean and goes live from the first change, the preview chip greys while the session has neither a page nor a discovered server, and the terminal chip greys once every shell has ended; each goes live again the moment its subject comes back. On a chat session all three grey at every moment whatever the session has written, each naming in its own words what converting the session to a project would light, because a chat keeps files rather than changes and has no repository for a shell. The command palette lists each of those acts either way, carrying the chip's own sentence while it is greyed.

The pull-request word is a clickable word in the header's own colour and font — no box, no border, no glyph, underlined on hover, answering focus and Enter as it answers a press — drawn only while a request is open on the branch and absent the moment none is, never greyed, because absence is the rule everywhere outside the three pane chips. A press opens the review pane on its pull-request tab; where the branch carries more than one request the word reads the newest. Diff counts render with the minus sign and never a hyphen, and an untitled session's title reads `New chat` or `New session`, faint and italic.

The header carries no location chip, attach chip, status dot, goal item, model chip, run-status text, held-note count, port, agent chip or sidekicks badge: the held notes live in the composer's attachment row and the sidekicks badge in the composer's footer strip. It carries no sessions control either, except the one back control the one-column layout puts at the head of the line, which shows the sessions list and is absent side by side. The session's menu is the session's own actions, built from one list with the row's right-click menu: rename, open the inspector with its chord, pin to top or unpin, a rule, archive and close, with convert-to-project above the rule on a chat session. The inspector item reads as opening the inspector and never as worktree details.

**The deck** holds the session column and the pane tracks. The right column holds exactly one main pane — the inspector, the review surface, the sidekicks pane, the browser pane or the file view — and, under it, optionally the terminal. Opening a second main pane replaces the first. The terminal replaces nothing and nothing replaces it: opened while a main pane is up it stacks beneath that pane, opened with nothing up it fills the column, and a main pane opened while it is alone goes above it and leaves it where it is. Closing the main pane leaves the terminal alone in the column; closing the terminal leaves the pane. There are never two main panes, and the column carries no tab strip. One entity opens one pane, structurally: a single mount door re-focuses the pane an address already has rather than opening a second. A pane opens by expanding its grid track in flow, never by overlaying, and panes are separated by a one-pixel inset line rather than a border. Opening the review pane, the terminal or the browser pane collapses the sessions track, and nothing restores it.

Review, the terminal and the browser pane can each take the full width, collapsing every other track; while one holds it its control reads as pressed and its hover title flips to the wording that gives the width back, and a second press or Escape returns it without also closing the pane. Changing which pane the slot holds gives up full width first. Every pane carries a drag edge of its own, and the sessions track carries the same seam: the pointer drags it, one arrow grows and the other shrinks it by one nudge of the current text size and by five with the shift key, and home or a double-click resets it; a separator reports its current size and its two limits on every set. The terminal stacked under a pane carries a second, horizontal edge with the same shape and the same keys turned on their side, and it opens on the lower third of the column, each half clamping where it stops being readable. The grab zone is wider than the line it draws — inside it the pointer becomes the resize cursor and a hairline brightens under it — while the drawn line itself never moves or changes width, so nothing on screen shifts. A drag suspends transitions and holds the transcript's follow until the pointer is released, and the width persists only on release. Every pane clamps at the narrowest its own content still reads at, and the session column keeps the floor its header must hold; a pane narrows itself to keep that floor when the sessions track or the notification list opens, when a pane opens and when full width comes off, not only on a drag. The column has one width and the stack shares it: the pane on top sets it, and the terminal beneath takes it, its own minimum applying only while it is alone in the column.

Every pane clips its own overflow, scrolls in one named inner scroller and never overflows horizontally. Every scroller but the transcript draws its scrollbar over its content and takes no layout width for it in any state: the bar appears while the region is scrolled or hovered, fades when it stops, and its thumb drags. The transcript draws none, its right edge being the ask rail's. A wheel or a drag inside a box that scrolls scrolls that box, and only once the box is at its own end does the gesture reach the transcript behind it; the transcript keeps its own overscroll, never bouncing the window and never passing the scroll outward, with the platform's own scroll anchoring off so a picture typesetting three screens up cannot rock the column under the reader. Each pane loads its body the first time it is opened and stays loaded, so reopening rebuilds nothing, and a body that fails to load reads as one line naming that pane with a retry beside it. Every one-shot read a pane makes takes a cancel signal, and a pane cancels its own reads when it closes or when a newer read replaces them, so a diff nobody is looking at stops being decoded and drawn.

Each half of the stack reads every standing pane rule on its own. A shell that ends while the terminal is stacked closes the terminal half with its one line and leaves the pane above it where it was; a main pane whose subject goes closes itself and leaves the terminal alone in the column. The terminal control is the one control that opens and closes that half, each pane's own close closes its own, and nothing joins the header for the stack.

**The session sidebar** shows the session's other work as independently loaded sections — goal, channels, runs, sidekicks, repos and worktrees, approvals, artifacts — each a composition of its own read; a section carrying an amber or red item is open and every other section is collapsed. A section opens the surface its subject is read on: a repository, workspace or worktree the `inspector` pane, its changes the `diff` pane, a dispatched agent the `agent-console` pane, a file the `diff` pane and a plan its own reader. The goal, runs and approvals sections open no pane of their own, because the goal sits on the session, a run is paused and interrupted from the working line, and a waiting request is answered on the composer's card.

**Banners sit under the session header**, above the pane note and the surface, in the shape §Meridian, the design language item 9 fixes: each carries its own dismissal, blocks nothing, and enters no attention count. Four conditions draw one. A session whose provider started without part of its settings names the file and the line or the key the provider itself reported — never a guess — and offers to open that file in the editor the General settings page names; it goes on its dismissal for this start of the provider, or the next time the file loads cleanly, and a later start still on defaults draws it again. A provider process that ended on its own under a running session names the provider and the exit code the daemon read, and offers a restart, which starts the provider again on the same session and lets the waiting messages go as the next turn. A provider build this console cannot work with names both versions and offers the Providers settings page. A provider build that changed under a running session names the old and the new version and offers a restart, leaving the running turn untouched until the session is restarted.

**Finding inside the open session** is one chord and one box, drawn on whichever surface has focus — the transcript, or the shell where the shell holds it — on that surface's top-right corner over the content, with no scrim and nothing under it moving, while the transcript and the composer stay live. The box holds its field, focused on open with its text selected and kept for as long as the session stays; the count of matches; the previous and next controls; and its close control. There is no scope note, because the count speaks for the whole session. What is searched is the visible text of every row — the person's messages, the agent's replies, the tool rows and the act rows. Each match is marked where it sits, the one being read is marked as current and scrolled into view, and Enter or the step chord moves forward, shift-Enter back, all three wrapping at the ends. The count counts the whole session rather than the rows on screen, so stepping past the last loaded hit loads the history that hit sits in and moves the transcript to it, on the person's own press and never by itself; the count and the reach are the daemon's search over the session's own rows, never a renderer-side walk of what it does not hold. Stepping to a match opens the run group holding it first, so the marked line is never inside a fold. Escape closes the box, removes every mark and returns focus where it was.

**Searching every session** is a second surface, opened by the palette's `Search all sessions` and drawn the way the palette is: one field over the session column, and under it the hits grouped by session — the session's name, then its own matching messages, each hit carrying enough of its words to be recognised. Choosing a hit switches the screen to that session and lands on that message, loading the stretch of history it sits in on the way. This is the only surface that searches the text inside sessions; the sessions track's own field searches the rows in the list (§The all-sessions list). Both reads are the daemon's ([Spec-001 §Interfaces And Contracts](./001-session-core.md#interfaces-and-contracts)), and neither box walks rows the console does not already hold.

A region of the workspace that has nothing to show, has lost its subject or failed while drawing says so in the shapes §Meridian, the design language item 8 fixes.

##### The composer

The composer is the shell chrome every session view carries, sketched under §Signature Feature Composition Sketches. It is a floating card at the transcript's own content width, docked one gutter's gap off the bottom of the session column, and it holds — top to bottom — the hairline, the worktree setup card while one runs, the working line, the approvals card while one waits, the composer proper with its command list, its attachment row, its draft field, its send control and its toolbar, and the session strip. The jump control sits above the card inside the dock while the follow is released. It addresses the session's own agent and carries no second addressee and no machine picker; what its parts show, offer and refuse is in that sketch.

##### Pane kinds

A closed set. The session's own flow is the `timeline` kind, session- or channel-scoped. Beside it the right column holds exactly one main pane at a time or none — `inspector`; `diff`, which is both the review surface and the read-only file view; `agent-console`, the sidekicks pane; and `browser`, the previewed page — with `terminal` able to stack under whichever one is up. Opening a second main pane replaces the first, and which pane a session had open is remembered per session. The workflows rail destination opens `workflow-builder`, and one workflow run being watched opens `workflow-run`. Nothing else is a pane. There is no approvals pane, no runs pane and no artifact pane: the one waiting approval, an agent's question and a plan waiting for a verdict are each the single card in the composer, one at a time; pausing and interrupting a run are the working line's own pressable words and a message sent mid-turn is the transcript's last row until the agent takes it; and a plan opens in its reader over the session column, a picture or a document in the quick look, and a file a session wrote in the `diff` kind — scoped to that one file in the review surface on a project session, and as its own lines in the file view on a chat. A repo, workspace, or worktree entity is a card in its sidebar section and opens as an `inspector` pane keyed by its entity kind, its changes opening the `diff` pane — no dedicated pane kind exists for those families and the set is not widened for them. A layout snapshot of an unknown version is discarded whole, an unknown pane kind is dropped and reported, and an entity id that fails validation is rejected.

- **`timeline`** — the transcript surface, session- or channel-scoped. One flow per session, all but the one on screen hidden, capped at the single width every row of it reads with a gutter either side, its rows one derived gap apart whatever they are. It is the one scrolling surface in the conversation: no run group, tool body, diff or output scrolls inside itself, nothing in it is cut at a height of its own beyond the cuts a long diff and a long output take, and nothing scrolls sideways — code wraps, a table wraps its cells and a diff line wraps. The flow fades into the header at its top edge over one line height and nowhere else. Older history comes in as the reader nears the top, one stretch per gesture so a fling cannot pull in a whole day, with a line at the top of what is loaded for the case the automatic one cannot see; newer rows page in as the reader nears the bottom without the reading position moving. Only the rows near the window are drawn, with a band above and below kept ready so a fast scroll never shows blank space, and old rows leave memory in cuts that never take a row on the screen or the row under the reader. A session long enough to reflow is held blank while its row heights settle and shown in one piece, with a failsafe so it can never stay blank; a session opened before mounts at the heights its rows really had last time. It has two position aids and no third: the ask rail at its right edge, and the jump control above the composer. A run group is drawn open, as the transcript draws it, and stays open until a person closes it — by its own header, or by the palette's fold-every-run act; nothing chooses the state a run arrives in, and no setting bounds how many stand open at once. Every way of being sent to one row takes the same path — the row is loaded if it is not on the page, the run group holding it is opened first, the flow lands on it, and it washes faintly in the accent for one duration and then fades; a row that is no longer there says so in one line instead of moving the page. The row kinds themselves, and their states, are [Spec-011](./011-live-timeline-visibility-and-reasoning-surfaces.md)'s; §Timeline View composes them.
- **`inspector`** — one entity at a time, as wide as its widest label-and-value pair reads at the current text size, with a header carrying a back control, the entity's kind and name, and a close control, over its own scrolling body. The chord that toggles it gives the slot back to whichever pane was open before when it closes it, while the pane's own back and close controls empty the slot. Its kind word is the worktree on a project session and the workspace on a chat, and wherever it is the open pane it re-keys to the session on screen. On a chat the repository fact reads none beside the attach action that promotes the session in place, keeping its history and its managed workspace, and that session's inspector carries no branch, no base and no snapshots. Its sections are identity (repository, path, branch, base, mount, created, provider and account, the account fact ending in the switch that lists the provider's saved accounts and applies at the run boundary), memory, hooks, cost, context, changes, artifacts, runs here, snapshots on a project session, sidekicks, and actions. The only controls in it that change anything are that account switch, the memory switch, a snapshot restore and, in its Sidekicks section, the session's `Sidekicks may call each other` switch, which reads `On` or `Off` and starts off, each one press rather than a form; everything else it offers opens something elsewhere — a path in the editor, a file in the review pane, a plan in its reader. It offers no settings form, no model switcher, no token graph and no raw event dump, and nothing in it overflows its own width: every long value truncates and carries a hover title. **The console's own caches are bounded from this machine's memory rather than by a count.** The files it has cached, the colour spans the daemon computed and the pictures it has loaded each carry a budget worked out from the machine's own memory, the least recently used clean entry going first when one is reached; the trim runs only while nothing is happening on screen and no turn is live, it never takes the rows a child's view or its parent is showing, and nothing about any of it is shown — no figure, no control, no notice. That run-time budget is a different thing from the fixed targets in §Budgets, which are the bars every console change is measured against.
- **`diff`** — the one pane kind both the review surface and the read-only file view use: a repository's, workspace's or worktree's changes open it from the inspector or from a row in the transcript, and a file a chat session wrote opens it read-only on that file's own lines with its version stepper. What it shows is [Spec-009](./009-gitflow-pr-and-diff-attribution.md)'s and [Spec-012](./012-artifacts-files-and-attachments.md)'s; this spec fixes that both are the one pane kind, that the review surface is that pane with the ship strip and the pull-request tab composed on top rather than a modal, a route or a pane kind of its own, and that the review surface opens on no chat session, because a chat keeps files rather than changes. The ship strip is one state machine: a state line read off the daemon's facts, one next act — commit, push or open the pull request — and one alternate, a pull while the branch is behind standing in place of whatever the state would otherwise offer. The review surface's diff is one list at line granularity — every file header, every notice, every gap and every code line is a row of it — and only the rows on screen are built, a bounded number of them each frame however large the diff is; a folded file contributes no rows at all, so folding every file empties the list at once, and a line's colouring happens off the path that builds its row.
- **`workflow-run`** — one workflow run being watched, the view [Spec-015](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md) owns; this spec restates none of its behaviour.
- **`workflow-builder`** — the authoring canvas [Spec-015](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md) owns, which the workflows rail destination opens; this spec restates none of its behaviour.
- **`browser`** — the session's own previewed page. Two page hosts and one endpoint: on the machine that executes the session the page is real — a native `WebContentsView` in its own persistent partition on this window, or the daemon's headless Chromium where no desktop is open there — and on another device it is a live picture of that page and nothing else, with the address line naming the machine it runs on. Both hosts expose one Chrome-debug endpoint, one resolver in the daemon returns the active one, and nothing above it branches on which host is live ([ADR-036](../decisions/036-embedded-browser-for-preview.md)). The pane's header carries the full-width and close controls; under it an address line runs back, forward, reload, the address control, the live width while the pane's edge is under the pointer, the machine line on another device, the three marking tools with the stroke-hue slider under the pen, and last the hand-off that opens the same address in the system browser. Back and forward are always drawn and greyed in place from the page's real history rather than from a count the pane keeps. The address control edits in place, navigates on Enter, keeps typed text while the page updates under it, and takes an http or https address, a bare host, a loopback host and port, a bare port, an address literal or a path against the current page; anything else is refused in the pane's own one-line refusal naming the exact cause, and it never searches. Its page tab strip appears only at two or more pages, one tab each with the page's icon, its title falling back to the host, its port on a loopback page and its own close control, drawn like the terminal's strip and activating the same way, reordering by drag, and closing on a middle-click as on the control; a typed address navigates in place rather than minting a tab, and a page's own pop-up request opens as another tab rather than an operating-system window. The body holds exactly one of four states: the empty rectangle the desktop positions the real page over, the live picture on another device, the discovered development servers as rows naming each server, its framework where the owning process's command line names one, its loopback address, and a mark on a server this session started — or the one line saying none is running in this project — and the load failure with its retry. With a tool pressed the body shows the page frozen into a picture under the marks layer: the capture is taken first and the view hidden second, never the other way round, and the picture's scale is read from the returned image rather than from the platform's reported scale factors. Comments drop a numbered marker with a note, boxes are dragged with a note, the pen draws freehand in the colour on the slider and keeps it; comments and boxes take the accent and never the waiting colour; a mark is removed from its note and a stroke from its own close control; clearing and sending the marks exist only while a mark does; and releasing the tool returns the live page with the marks kept. Sending them stages one attachment chip in the composer's attachment row and focuses the draft, the attachment carrying the picture with the marks drawn in, and per mark its number, note, element reference and box — a stroke its points and its colour — with the address and the width. A mark's element reference is taken at the moment it is committed and is valid only for the snapshot that minted it. The pane reports its own screen rectangle, and the part of it still visible against everything that clips it, on every frame the pane moves or resizes, so the page never lags the frame it sits in; an overlay that covers the visible part hides the page, because that is the one place an embedded page is visibly not a document element, and while a pane edge is under the pointer the page lets pointer events through so the drag never sticks on it. Zoom lives on the keys alone, per page, stepping the platform's own presets while focus is inside the pane and resetting to the default for a new page; every chord the console has bound is still the console's while the page holds the keyboard, and everything else the person types reaches the page. A right-click on the page raises a small menu the console owns rather than the page engine's own, whose rows are back, forward and reload, the text acts, copying a link where the press landed on one, and opening the page in the system browser; a row whose act is not available where the press landed is absent rather than drawn dead. One saved site-data set per machine serves the pane, the agent's tools and a scheduled workflow alike, so a login in the pane persists across restarts; per-site forget, clearing everything, and signing in and out per site are on the Browser settings page, which also holds whether site data is remembered at all, whether the browser tools are handed to a provider in the first place — which is what that switch decides, rather than whether one call is allowed — and which browser is in use with its version. A download is refused with one line in the pane. **Caps.** One browser per machine, one tool server per daemon, one renderer process per open page, and as many pages per session as the machine's own memory allows — no count stands between the person and another page; the oldest page nobody is looking at is released first when the machine's ceiling is reached, with its address kept, a page unseen for ten minutes is released and reloads on demand, the headless browser closes after ten idle minutes, the screencast runs only while another device has the pane open, and discovery ticks only while a project session is live. The pane is never a web browser: no bookmarks, no downloads, no developer-tools control, no search, no picture standing in for a real page on the machine that executes the session, no second pane open beside it, and no dialog, overlay, backdrop or scrim anywhere in it. Preview is no source of attention either: a page loading, a mark drawn or an agent's browser call is none of the things that wait on a person. **The agent's browser tools** act on that same page through one tool server, identically on both providers, and each call is a row in the transcript in words with its screenshot inline, gated by the session's permission level like any other tool; a call driven from another device names the machine in its row.
- **`terminal`** — the session's own shells, lease-gated over [Spec-002](./002-runtime-node-attach.md)'s terminal write lease. The pane opens wide enough for a command line and its output to wrap rarely and never narrows past the point one still reads at, with its own drag edge and a full-width control, and it is drawn on no chat session. Under the pane header sits a tab strip: one tab per shell the session has open, each carrying the shell's own title — its basename until the shell sets one — and its own close control, with a control at the end of the strip that opens another shell and makes it active. A tab's shell is the person's own login shell started in the session's own tree, so their profile, aliases, prompt and version managers are all there; where their shell names nothing that can be started the daemon falls back to the platform's default and says so in one line above the prompt. A middle-click closes a tab as its control does, tabs reorder by drag, and a right-click raises a small menu with close, close-others and close-to-the-right, the last two greyed with their reason where there is no such tab — there is no close-everything row, because one press would end every shell in the session, a running server among them. The sub-line reads the active tab's project and tree, and the body — a fixed monospace grid one step of the type scale in size, with glyph shaping off and a line height that tiles so box-drawing characters meet with no seam — shows the active tab's shell. Each shell keeps its own control lease, so this device can hold one shell while another of the person's devices, or an agent's running command, holds another. The lease line sits between the strip and the body and speaks for the active tab's shell alone: where another of the person's devices holds it, the line names that device and offers to take the shell, asking once in place, and the body is read-only and says so to assistive technology; where an agent's running command holds it, the line names the agent and offers to stop that run, and there is nothing to take. Switching tabs re-reads the line for the shell that tab shows. Closing the pane hides its shells and ends none of them — a build started in a tab keeps running and the pane comes back to it — and quitting the console ends none either; a shell ends when its own tab closes, and a shell that ended keeps its tab, its last line saying it exited and with which code, until that tab is cleared. Output a shell prints while its tab is not drawn is held for it under a bound and shown when the tab is drawn again; a tab whose command ended off screen carries a small quiet mark until the person looks at it, and nothing rings for it. A session opens as many shells as the machine allows, and the only refusal is the daemon's own, written in place under the strip in plain words with the open control left where it is. A selection in the body raises a control that lands those lines in the draft as a fenced code block and moves focus there without sending, which touches neither the scrollback nor the lease, so a shell someone else holds can still be read and quoted. While the body has focus every platform-modifier chord is still the console's and every other key is written to the shell, Escape and Enter included; a key pressed while the view is scrolled back snaps it to the live bottom first. An address the shell prints is a live link in the body's own text: a loopback address opens the `browser` pane at that address and any other opens the system browser through `native.openExternal`, and the press moves neither the shell's focus nor its lease. The pane carries no repaint control — a program whose frame has gone wrong is redrawn by moving an edge a step, which reaches it as a real resize — and no export: what a person wants out of it leaves as a selection.
- **`agent-console`** — the sidekicks pane and the child view inside it, composed in §Multi-Agent Channels View.

##### Auxiliary windows

`timeline` and `agent-console` panes can be moved into their own window — the two windows §Main Process Responsibilities names, each a `BaseWindow` hardened per §Security Hardening Baseline — opened from the platform menu bar. An auxiliary window loads the same renderer bundle at a window route, carries its own preload and bridge instance, subscribes to the daemon itself, and shares no in-memory store and no auth material with the main window; the main window shows the moved pane's slot as a placeholder with a focus control, and a crashed auxiliary window returns the pane to the deck with the crash noted in the pane's error slot. A renderer-initiated detach from a pane rides the bridge's `window` namespace, which is on Plan-021's console growth slate; the menu-bar path ships first because it needs nothing new. The chord reserved for detaching a pane is bound to nothing and is refused as a binding on the Keyboard page, so nothing else can take it.

##### Settings and operator pages

Settings is where the machine is configured — the accounts that pay for the work, the tool servers, the projects, the browser, the keys, the look, the notifications and the local runtime — and nothing on it makes or runs work; a rail destination does that. Every page is one projection of what the daemon and the shell already hold: the screen recomputes nothing they report and keeps no second copy of a fact, so a page whose write is refused renders that refusal and keeps the value it had rather than pretending to save, and a reading the daemon cannot settle is drawn as unsettled — an account it cannot decide about reads as undecided and never as signed in. The console carries no identity of its own on this screen or any other — no sign-in gate in front of the first session, no organisation or workspace step, no avatar, no security section and no passkey row, and nothing that offers to carry an earlier workspace across — and the only sign-in any page offers is a provider account's, on the Providers page. Nothing on the screen authors an agent, sets how a diff reads, or holds a preference for an editor inside the console: an agent's instructions are written on the Sidekicks screen, the review surface keeps its own reading controls, and the editor preference on General names an outside editor.

Two panes, one page at a time, sharing the palette's matcher for search. The left pane is the page list with its search field above it and the nine entries below in a fixed order; the right pane draws exactly one page. A page that splits its own pane in two — the server list and the selected server's detail on MCP servers — is that page's own business and not a third pane of the screen. The nine are a closed set, rendered always and in order with the open one marked as the current page to assistive technology, so a page whose service is unreachable keeps its entry and says on the page what is missing — a list that shrinks teaches a person the setting does not exist. The list is a roving-cursor list whose cursor opens the page it lands on: an arrow, home or end moves the cursor and draws that page at once with no second press, and the cursor wraps from the last entry to the first and back, so the keyboard and the eye walk the same order. Which page is open lives in the route, in three arms — the screen, a page, and a page with a selection — so a deep link and a click on the list are the same act and the back control works; the selection is handed to the page untouched and narrowed fail-closed against that page's own vocabulary, so an unrecognised selection opens the page for nothing rather than refusing to open. Three surfaces deep-link in: the worktree setup card's edit control opens Projects on that project, the composer's tool-servers control opens MCP servers through its manage link, and a run refused on the account plane opens Providers on the account the refusal names. Arriving from any of them finishes the journey rather than landing at the top: the page opens, a folded section holding the named control is opened first, the control is scrolled into the middle of the view and lit briefly and then quiet again, and a control the page is not drawing leaves the person on the page rather than refusing the link. Where the window is too narrow to hold both panes at once — the break being the width the two panes' own content needs at the current text size, never a device class — they become one screen at a time, the list alone and a page with a back control above its title that returns to the list. Reopening Settings opens the page that was last open. The rail and its attention control are the same here as on any other screen, and the sessions track sits collapsed.

Every page opens with one line saying what it is and where its values are kept, and a control commits as it changes: the screen's only save-and-revert pair belongs to the Projects page's worktree draft, which is a real draft, so closing Settings commits the field being edited and never that draft. A control whose value differs from its default is visibly marked. A refused write rolls the control back to the value it had and renders the daemon's own refusal in place, in the refusal grammar's inline shape, and an act that settles without changing what is drawn says so in one line on the page, in flow; there is no toast anywhere on this screen. Every switch carries its role, its accessible name and its state, answers Enter and Space, shows the console's own focus ring, and sits in a press target derived from the text size; a switch greyed by one of the named exceptions keeps all three and says why in its own hover title. A sign-in that does not finish — a provider account's or a tool server's — says so where it was started, leaves what it was signing in to exactly as it was, and keeps the control that starts it again. Credential material reaches this screen only as a write-only field. What this install looks like stays on this install: the theme, the scheme, the text size, the transcript width, the page last open and the keyboard map are kept for this machine and no page offers to carry a preference to another one, nothing exports or imports the screen's own values, and nothing resets the screen in one press — a reset is a page's own, and the Keyboard page's is the only one.

The nine pages, in that order:

- **General.** What this install is, how it updates itself, and what a new session starts from, all kept on this machine. The page opens with the running version and, beside it, the platform, the architecture and the language, each as the app reports it. Then the update block, which draws the whole path as five numbered steps — checked, downloading, checking the signature, ready to install, restart to finish — with the step it is on marked and the ones behind it ticked: idle names the newest version this machine has seen and offers a check; found names the version and the date it was released and offers the download; downloading draws its percentage over a bar under one line saying what a restart would stop and in how many sessions an agent is working; ready says so and asks for the restart, and the restart confirm names that same count before it acts. Under the line naming the version found sits one fold carrying that version's notes in plain sentences, shut until it is pressed and drawn in every arm that names a version and in none that does not. A verification failure says the update could not be verified and was not installed, with try-again as a faint clickable word at the right end of that line. An update a previous run staged and never applied is reported in the same block, naming the staged version beside the version this install is on, and stays there until an update lands. An install that cannot update itself draws no update button at all: it says so in place and names the command that gets the newest version. Below the block: `Check for updates automatically`, on; `Send crash reports`, on, under one line saying personal data is stripped before a report leaves this machine, and the one control for it anywhere in the console; a switch that starts a new session on the last model and effort used, on, carrying those two values and no third, under one line saying the permission level is never carried over because a new session always starts at its project's default level, and one line naming what would be carried — the automatic-compaction bound is not remembered here either, staying the provider's own on Providers and the session's own behind its context ring; a switch that keeps this machine awake while an agent is working, off, with nothing added to the rail for it — it reads `Keep this Mac awake while a sidekick is working` on macOS and `Keep this computer awake while a sidekick is working` on Windows and Linux; the editor that opens files, one preference every open-in-the-editor act in the console honours, listing every editor the console looks for with the system default leading the list and holding the value until someone changes it, an editor this machine does not have keeping its place dimmed and unpickable and reading as not installed, and an editor chosen here that later goes missing saying so on its row, keeping its place, and letting the open land in the system default rather than failing; and the default checkout for a new project session, applied when a project session is created and never overriding a choice made at creation.
- **Providers.** One section per provider, each in two halves: the settings that provider exposes at the provider level above the accounts registered against it. The registry itself — what an account is, how it is signed in, how its billing mode and its per-limit quota are kept, and which account a run starts on — is [Spec-026](./026-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md)'s; §Provider Accounts And Cost View composes both halves and states what the page shows and refuses.
- **MCP servers.** The settings-level governance page for this machine's tool servers, and the only place in the console where one is added, removed, trusted or governed: a server list on the left of the page pane and the selected server's detail on the right. The governance rules it renders — the inventory, trust, per-tool overrides, the authorization it never re-derives, and the events it reads — are [Spec-025](./025-mcp-server-configuration-and-governance.md)'s; §MCP Servers View composes the page and states what it shows, collects and refuses.
- **Projects.** Every project attached to this machine, one row each, opening in place. An `Every project` section sits above the list holding the environment rows every process the console starts is given. An opened row carries, first, the steps that run after a worktree is created — the files to copy, the commands to run in order, and the time limit they are given — behind the screen's one save-and-revert pair, then that project's own environment rows directly beneath those steps, a name set there winning over the row of the same name above it and saying so on itself. Beside them sit four actions on the attachment: rename, which edits the display name and moves nothing — not the folder on disk and not the project segment of any worktree path, so a renamed project's worktrees and the sessions standing in them are untouched; open in the editor General names; archive, which takes the row out of the sessions list's grouping and moves it to an archived group at the foot of the page, its sessions kept and one unarchive action on it; and delete, greyed while an agent is running anywhere in the project with its hover title naming that agent — the pattern a worktree's own removal control uses — and otherwise behind a confirm in place that says its sessions stay, the folder on disk stays, and the act cannot be undone. What the steps do, what an environment row reaches, and what attaching and detaching a project mean are [Spec-007 §Required Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#required-behavior)'s and [Spec-008 §Required Behavior](./008-worktree-lifecycle-and-execution-modes.md#required-behavior)'s; this page is where they are written.
- **Browser.** What the browser inside the console remembers, and whether agents may drive it. One saved site-data set serves this machine — the cookies and local storage, and the console's own visit list — shared by the `browser` pane, an agent's browser tools and a workflow's browser steps alike, with no per-session partition, so a login survives a restart. The page holds: whether site data is remembered at all, on; the sites that have data, one row each reading the host, whether it is signed in, and when it was last used, carrying the one act that row's state allows — signing in, which opens that site in the `browser` pane so the person signs in there, or signing out, which signs out and forgets that site's login — beside a forget that is per site and never per session, and nothing offered in a state where it could only refuse; clearing all of it, confirming in place and naming how many sites go with it; whether the browser tools are handed to a provider at all, which is what that switch decides rather than whether one call is allowed, under one line saying every call an agent makes still goes through that session's own permission level and nothing here loosens it; which browser is in use, with its version and, where it was fetched rather than found, when it was fetched, and a control that fetches it again; and the self-hosted git hosts, one validated host per row with its own removal beside a field that adds another, so a self-hosted forge lights the pull-request half of the review surface, a host that does not parse refused in place with nothing written. With no site data saved the page says so rather than drawing an empty table.
- **Keyboard.** Every chord the console answers is a row on this page, and nothing on any other screen changes for it. The page carries its own search box above the rows, filtering them as it is typed and saying so where nothing matches, and the rows sit in four groups in one order — Panes, Sessions, Find, App — one row to an act, thirty-six rows over thirty-seven keys. They are the five pane chords and the next-and-previous file in a diff; the sessions list, the search over that list and the search across every session, the new-session chord, next-and-previous session, the nine session rows, the previous and next ask, sending the message, interrupt and interrupt-everything; the find box's own four keys; and the palette, open-Settings, open-Workflows, next-waiting and answer-this-run chords. **No chord carries two acts**, with one shape of exception: two rows may share one key where they are disjoint by state — Escape, where closing the find box answers only while that box is open and interrupting only when nothing is open to close, and Enter, where stepping to the next match answers only while that box is open and sending the message in the message box. A row carries the act's on-screen name, a one-line description of what it does — never a command identifier and never a raw when-clause — and the chord it holds now drawn as key caps; the rebind and reset controls are drawn only while that row is under the pointer or holds focus, and the reset only while the row is overridden, so a page of thirty-six rows reads as names and chords rather than as a hundred controls. The page carries one whole-page reset naming how many chords are changed, in the singular where exactly one is. Rebind records the next press on the control itself while the rest of the keyboard is suspended, so a chord the console already uses can still be pressed; during a recording Escape leaves the chord alone and Backspace or Delete leaves that act with no chord at all, and one line under the search box says both. A rebind that settles says so on the row in one line, naming the act and the chord it now answers to, or that the act now answers to no chord at all. A key another act already answers to is refused on the row, naming the act that holds it, and so is the chord reserved for detaching a pane, which is bound to nothing. **A chord is modifiers plus exactly one key**, held in one spelling so two chords written differently are known to be the same and the refusal on a taken one can be trusted, and it answers only while the modifiers held are exactly its own — a finger too many defeats it rather than firing the shorter chord. Where the platform reports a different character than the key that was pressed, the letter under the option modifier and the punctuation under the platform-and-shift pair are recovered from the physical key, so those chords still answer. Sending the message is a row like any other, holding Enter by default: while it holds Enter, Enter sends and shift-Enter starts a new line; while it holds the platform modifier with Enter, Enter starts a new line and that pair sends. The message box gains no control for it either way. **The grammar is when-scoped.** Each act declares once whether its chord reaches into a text field: inside a field only the chords so marked fire and every other key falls through to the text editing, and while a shell body or a previewed page holds the keyboard every platform-modifier chord is still the console's while everything else reaches the shell or the page. A chord answers only where its own act can act: a chord whose chip is greyed does nothing, and a chord whose surface is not open does nothing. One line above the rows says that in the terminal the chords carrying the platform modifier act in the console and every other key goes to the program running there, and one line under them says that Claude Code and Codex keep their own keys and names the command each of them answers to for changing them — the console neither reads nor writes either program's key file, because a session runs over the wire where no key exists and a command a person runs in the terminal pane reads its own file itself. The page closes by naming the keys it does not hold: the window-closing chord, which keeps its system meaning, and the reserved detach chord, which runs nothing. There are no two-stroke sequences, no keymap presets and no import or export of the map: every chord is one press, every row is changed on its own row, and the map arrives from nowhere but the person's own rebinds. Every chord drawn anywhere in the console reads the live binding rather than a fixed string. The map is local to this install and is never written to a wire: it holds only the rows that differ from the chord they ship with, keyed by the act rather than by a position, so a row put back to its default drops out of it and a default improved in a later release reaches anyone who never touched that row, and it is written whole and atomically and readable and writable by this person alone, so a crash mid-save cannot leave the keyboard half-written. A development build's reload chords are compiled out of the shipped app and are no rows here, because in a shipped console there is nothing there to rebind.
- **Appearance.** How the console looks on this machine, kept for this install. Its first control is the theme, two swatches with the default marked and no third, each previewing live on press. Under it the colour scheme's three choices side by side, then `Text size`'s four steps, then the switch that draws the console flat for a screen reader, then the transcript-width slider, which sits directly under `Text size` and returns to its default on a double-click of its knob. What each of them changes, and what each of them must not change, is fixed in §Meridian, the design language; this page is where they are chosen.
- **Notifications.** Two switches, both on by default — `Show a count on the app icon` and `Notify me outside the app` — and nothing else on the page: no per-kind switch, no per-session mute, nothing that makes a sound, and no rendering of the daemon's own stored preference records as further switches. Both switches, and that they are the whole of the preference surface, are [Spec-017 §Default Behavior](./017-notifications-and-attention-model.md#default-behavior)'s; the mute is global in V1, so there is no per-session entry here.
- **Runtime.** The local runtime that runs the agents, the folders it can reach, what it keeps, and the port it listens on. The page shows what the supervisor reports about the daemon and edits none of it — whether it is running and since when, its version, and how much of the machine's processor and memory it is using, each reading stamped with the time it was taken — with a control that takes those readings again, and beside them the stop, the restart and the separate update of the daemon itself. Below them: the folders this machine can reach, each with what is using it and its own removal; how long diagnostic logs, finished sessions and a workflow run's step data are kept; the one act that deletes old data; the port a workflow started by a web request listens on; the time limit after which a run is ended; the memory cap for tool processes; and the two diagnostic switches, recording traces and recording an event-replay log, the second naming the file it writes. What each of those controls does, what each refuses, and that each value is set in one place and no second one are [Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)'s, and this page is the surface that shows them. Nothing on it makes a deletion automatic: past a bound a finished session becomes eligible for deletion, eligible is not deleted, and the delete control is the only thing on the page that removes anything. A daemon that is not answering says so here, and the page keeps its controls drawn rather than emptying. Diagnostics is not a page of its own — those two switches and the retention bounds are the whole of it — and nothing on this page reads the event log back: there is no recent-activity fold, no credential-attempt list and no audit view anywhere in the console. Because there is no such page, the reported status above is the only health the console reads: the one-shot node health read behind it — the overall status and each component's own state with the time it was last checked — taken again when the control asks and never on a subscription of this page's own. Nothing in the console inspects a stalled run, reads a failure's classification, requests a recovery action or reads the redaction policy; those reads had no home but a diagnostics page, and no surface here claims them ([Spec-018](./018-observability-and-failure-recovery.md) still owns the operations themselves).

##### Notifications

The notification surface composes the daemon's attention projection — items grouped by session, actionable split from informational — under [Spec-017](./017-notifications-and-attention-model.md). The list shares the sessions track at the same derived width and is shown in that track's place: the two lists are never open together, opening one closes the other, and only the open one's rail button reads as expanded. Its top row is its heading and its close control; its scroll holds a waiting group under its own header with its count, then an earlier group. An entry is one line — a dot in the row's own state, the title, the state word and the relative time, newest first inside each group — and the whole line is the control: a session entry switches the screen for real, a workflow-run entry opens that run in Workflows, and neither closes the list. Opening the list focuses its first entry and closing it returns focus to the rail's attention control; the chords that open the sessions track, the review pane and the terminal treat this list exactly as they treat that one. Only waiting entries are counted, and they are pinned above the rest; finished and failed entries are read and never counted, and an entry leaves the waiting group the moment its moment resolves — the daemon's projection, never the renderer's arithmetic. There is no dismiss (an item clears when the daemon resolves it), no per-session mute (preferences are global in V1), no re-filtering by preference in the renderer, no count on a finished or failed entry, no second badge, no overlay, and nothing said inside a session. Outside the window the operating system's notification is the only other channel, and it is the shell's (§Main Process Responsibilities), which also honours the system's do-not-disturb.

#### The fixture bridge

- A bridge provider in the renderer is typed from `packages/contracts`' desktop-bridge types and has two implementations: a **live** bridge wrapping `window.sidekicks`, and a **fixture** bridge serving deterministic scripted scenarios over async generators with time-scaled playback and a frozen clock. Both are shape-identical to §Preload Bridge Contract, namespace for namespace, on the registered surface, so a surface cannot tell which it is running on; the unregistered surface is the growth port below.
- **The growth port.** A wire the console needs and the corpus has not registered (every `§Console growth slate` row in Plan-021) is reached through the **console growth port** — a renderer-local typed seam carrying one typed entry per eventual bridge **operation** (method or subscription) the row's namespace will expose, each entry naming the slate row it serves — a row is not a method: a row that bundles a namespace, settings, and a pane-kind declaration maps to several entries, and its non-callable prerequisites (types, settings keys, pane-kind declarations) live in the scenario manifest's `prerequisites` ledger and never become port methods (Codex closing round) — typed by the console and implemented by the fixture bridge alone. The live bridge implements no growth-port method: its port resolves every method to the typed refusal that renders the row's surface in its _not checked_ absence shape (rule 8 of §Meridian, the design language). The port never crosses the preload boundary, so it mints no wire method and the shape-identity rule above stays exact on the registered surface. When a slate row lands, its consumer moves from the port to the registered namespace in the same PR and the port method is deleted; Plan-021 I-021-13 holds the two ledgers to each other — every open slate row maps to its operation entries and/or prerequisite entries, no entry exists without a row, and a landed row has none.
- Fixture code — every scenario and the switcher — sits behind the same compile-time `define`-substituted identifier as the smoke probes (§Pitfalls To Avoid), so a release bundle carries none of it; a runtime `process.env` gate leaves the code in the bundle and is rejected.
- **The demo dataset is a build-only switch behind that same gate.** One launch argument boots the console on scripted sessions, projects and transcripts with one reply that streams, no daemon behind it and nothing running, and the argument can name which session to open. Both the dataset and the argument are compiled out of a release build, so scripted data can never be mistaken for real work. Developer tools exist in a development build only and are compiled out of the shipped app too — the same discipline the `browser` pane keeps in offering none of its own — and neither is a row on the Keyboard page, because in a shipped console there is nothing there to rebind.
- A scenario manifest names every scenario, its frozen ticks, and, per bridge method, that method's live status at the corpus revision the console was built against, so a surface's live-status claim is checked against the manifest by a test rather than asserted. Scenarios double as demo reels and renderer test data; one — the flagship frame — is the README hero image and the headline surface of the capture aid §Console Test Tiers describes.
- The renderer runs in the Electron shell and under plain `vite dev` in a browser, since the fixture seam needs no daemon.

#### Budgets

Named targets, measured from the first console commit, recorded in a dated endurance ledger, and gating every console PR. **Where they are measured (Codex round 2).** The deterministic budgets — bundle size, heap at rest, steady heap, and first-ledger-row latency against the fixture's frozen clock — gate on any runner. The hardware-dependent budgets — frame time and CPU — are measured under one protocol: the built bundle in the Electron shell against the fixture bridge's flagship scenario with the frozen clock, ten warm-up seconds discarded, the metric taken as the median of three runs, on the **pinned CI runner class** the desktop workflow names by label (the reference machine for gating purposes), with thresholds calibrated on that class at first landing with 25 % headroom, re-baselined only by amendment when the label changes, and one automatic re-run before a failure counts. The figures in the table are the product targets on a laptop-class reference profile — eight cores, integrated GPU, one 60 Hz display — which the endurance ledger records beside the runner-calibrated thresholds, so a runner regression and a product regression stay distinguishable. A budget that cannot be measured on the pinned class at all is a spec amendment, never a skipped gate.

| Budget | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Renderer initial bundle | ≤ 450 kB gzip, excluding lazy chunks (terminal, node graph, math, diagrams, browser tools) |
| Frame time, four lanes streaming | p95 ≤ 16.7 ms on the reference machine |
| Renderer heap, one session open at rest | ≤ 120 MB |
| Steady heap, flagship live stream | flat over thirty minutes; ≤ 250 MB renderer heap |
| Idle CPU, session open, nothing streaming | ≤ 0.5 % of one core averaged over sixty seconds; no timer fires except the refresh scheduler's deadline and the presence heartbeat |
| Streaming CPU, one lane | ≤ 15 % of one core on the reference machine; the reveal engine yields when the frame budget is spent |
| Terminal instance | ≤ 20 MiB at the default scrollback |
| Time to first ledger row on launch | ≤ 800 ms from window show, fixture mode |

#### Persistence on the renderer scheme

Layout, scroll position, selection, pins, and expansion sets persist per install; **drafts do not** — composer text, form values, paths, and code a user has typed and not sent are user-authored content, and the only durable homes the corpus gives such content are the daemon's encrypted, PII-mapped stores ([Spec-020](./020-data-retention-and-gdpr.md)). A draft therefore lives in exactly one of those and never in window storage: it is daemon-held and session-scoped, saved a moment after typing pauses, restored on a session switch, on a restart and on the person's other devices, and cleared by sending it. The files staged beside it are copied to the daemon at staging time and held with the session outside the checkout, and a review's held notes live in the same store, so a half-written message or review reaches the person's other devices and sending needs no upload step.

What the renderer's own store holds is device-local convenience and nothing else: pane widths, the sessions track's own width, the stacked terminal's height, which pane each session had open and which child the sidekicks pane was on, the window's place, size, display and maximised-or-fullscreen state, the review pane's scope and base branch per session, the reader's place in a comparison and in a session, the projects the person folded, which files in the review pane they folded, the expansion sets, the theme, the colour scheme, the text size and the transcript width, and which settings page was last open. The theme, the scheme, the text size and the transcript width are what the Appearance page writes here, the width as the one root token every session's transcript reads its own column width from (§Meridian, the design language item 4). Every read and write is wrapped defensively and the screen renders correctly when the store is empty or throws. A remembered pane is put back only where its subject is still there — a shell to draw, changes to read, a page or a discovered server to open, the sidekicks list and the inspector always having one — and the memory is held until the daemon has answered about that subject: an answer that has not arrived, or one that fails, reopens nothing and erases nothing and is tried again when the daemon reports itself reachable, and an answer landing after the person has opened a pane themselves is dropped rather than replacing what they opened. Nothing is announced when a pane does not come back; the session simply opens with an empty slot. A saved window rectangle that is off every display now attached is pulled back on screen, and the window is restored without first appearing at another size. Projections never persist and are re-derived on reconnect (§Pitfalls To Avoid). The durable store therefore holds **UI state only** — a closed value-class enumeration the persistence layer's schema encodes, and a write outside it (message text, form values, paths, code, names, anything user- or machine-authored) is a tripwire failure at the store's write chokepoint. That persistence lives in IndexedDB under an own per-session partition with LRU trim and a quota gauge — which exists on the renderer's custom scheme only when the scheme is registered privileged (`standard: true`) before `app.ready` (§Renderer Bundle; Plan-021 I-021-11, shipped by its Tier-1 supplement Phase 1B). Until that registration ships the persistence layer runs on an in-memory adapter and says so.

### WebAuthn Credential Flow

Per ADR-010, WebAuthn PRF is the primary desktop credential path. The main process drives the ceremony through a per-platform native binding, not through `navigator.credentials.*` in the renderer — the renderer's origin is the `sidekicks-renderer://` custom scheme, which is a secure context and still not on Chromium's origin allowlist for public-key credentials, so that API rejects there regardless of scheme privilege; and §Trust Stance keeps the ceremony and its PRF output out of the untrusted renderer independently of that (re-derived 2026-09-01; see [Implementation Notes §WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module](#webauthn-platform-authenticator-native-module), which carries the measurement and corrects the prior claim that Electron provides no platform-authenticator flows at all).

End-to-end flow:

1. Renderer requests sign-in → bridge `webAuthn.signIn()`, which takes no argument. Main fetches the server-issued options — `rpId`, origin, challenge, and the ceremony transaction id — from the control plane over its own channel and validates them against the paired control-plane origin below; a mismatch refuses before any native binding is invoked (I-021-16). **That fetch is deliberately uncredentialed on the sign-in path**, because a user signing in on a cold install holds nothing to authenticate with — the ceremony is what mints the credential, so requiring one to start it would make the whole flow unreachable after a reinstall ([Spec-016 §Required Behavior](./016-identity-and-user-state.md#required-behavior); the enrolment path, which runs for an already-signed-in user, is authenticated). A renderer-supplied option set is not merely ignored: the method accepts no parameter, so passing one is a compile error, and the main-side handler refuses an IPC frame that carries a payload
2. Main process invokes that host's binding against whatever authenticator the host's own capability probe reported — the macOS platform authenticator (Touch ID / iCloud Keychain) or an attached security key through `AuthenticationServices`; Windows Hello or a roaming FIDO2 key through the OS `webauthn.dll` dialog; a roaming FIDO2 key through `libfido2` on Linux, which has no platform authenticator at all. The binding module is selected by `process.platform`; whether a usable authenticator and PRF exist is **probed at ceremony time and never inferred from the platform name** (§WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module)
3. Authenticator produces the assertion + PRF output, evaluated at the contract-fixed input every leg uses (guard 3 of §WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module) rather than at a value the reply supplied. **The PRF bytes are provisional here and are used for nothing** — they sit in main-process memory, unconsumed, until step 4 answers
4. Main submits the assertion to the control plane's authentication-verify procedure, which consumes the challenge, resolves the credential row from the returned credential id, checks signature, origin, `rpId`, the UV bit, and the signature counter, and answers with a verdict plus **the stored user-verification mode of the credential that actually signed** ([Spec-016 §Required Behavior](./016-identity-and-user-state.md#required-behavior)). Only a positive verdict makes the provisional bytes usable. On any refusal — unknown credential, bad signature, RP or origin mismatch, UV mismatch, counter regression — main **zeroizes the provisional PRF bytes before anything is cached, derived, or unwrapped** and takes §Fallback Behavior. The order is verification then derivation and never the reverse: a leg that derives first has already produced key material from an assertion the relying party went on to reject, and the wrong key is indistinguishable from the right one until something fails to open
5. Main compares the response's own UV bit against the mode that verdict returned (guard 4 of §WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module), and only then derives the wrapping key, which lives in its own address space and is never exposed to the renderer
6. **The verification reply carries a freshly issued PASETO access/refresh pair (2026-09-01, Codex round 6)**, sender-bound per [ADR-010](../decisions/010-paseto-webauthn-mls-auth.md) to the DPoP key main proved possession of on the verify request — a proof of key possession, which is not authentication and does not make the pre-authentication pair a credentialed one. **Main supplies that key itself and holds it outside the custody root (2026-09-01, Codex round 7):** it generates the DPoP key pair on first use and persists it in the OS keystore as its own entry under the [ADR-021](../decisions/021-cli-identity-key-storage-custody.md) ladder, **not** wrapped under the custody root, because the proof has to be signed before the ceremony returns and the root does not exist yet on a fresh install. The proof carried here is the token-request form — it covers the verify request's own method and URI and carries **no** `ath`, there being no access token yet to hash — and the relying party computes the issued pair's `cnf.jkt` from the proof's public key ([Spec-016 §Required Behavior](./016-identity-and-user-state.md#required-behavior)). Main unwraps this installation's custody root key with the ceremony-derived wrapping key — or, when this installation holds **no** root yet, mints one and wraps it under that same key first, which is the ordinary path on a fresh install signing in with an already-enrolled synced credential, where no local enrolment ever runs — and wraps the newly issued refresh token under that root before persisting it. **Unwrapping an existing token is the path for an installation that already holds one**, and only that: a cold install holds nothing to unwrap, so a flow that started there would be unreachable exactly after a reinstall, which is the case sign-in exists to serve. Main then resolves the bridge call with a `WebAuthnCeremonyOutcome` — a closed union of an authenticated arm carrying the user identity claims, a fallback-required arm naming the probe result that produced it, and a refused arm carrying a typed reason. No arm carries an assertion, a PRF output, a derived key, or a handle to one
7. Main process returns only the ceremony success signal + user identity claims to the renderer
8. Subsequent control-plane calls from the renderer flow through the bridge, where the main process attaches the unwrapped access token + DPoP proof

If the host's probe reports no usable authenticator, or the authenticator that answered does not support the PRF extension, the flow falls back to the Device Authorization Grant path per §Fallback Behavior. That fallback is a **per-host degradation**, not a per-platform assignment: a Windows or Linux host with a working authenticator runs the native ceremony, and a macOS host without one does not.

**A user cancellation is terminal, and is not a capability result (2026-09-01, Codex round 5).** The fallback above is reached by _the host having nothing that works_ — no authenticator, no PRF, a binding that will not load. A user who dismisses the OS dialog has answered the question, and the answer is no. The two outcomes are therefore distinct arms and never collapsed: a cancellation ends the whole ceremony, does not try a second provider arm on a host that offers one (the macOS platform and security-key providers are the case that makes this concrete), and does **not** open the DAG loopback — which would present a browser sign-in to a user who just declined to sign in, and would turn a refusal into a different credential path they never asked for. `WebAuthnCeremonyOutcome`'s refused arm carries it; the fallback-required arm does not, and no leg may map a cancellation into a probe result.

**The relying-party identifier comes from the trusted control-plane origin, not from a build constant (2026-09-01, Codex round 5).** A self-hosted control plane serves a different origin from the hosted one, and an `rpId` compiled into the shell would make every self-host fail the ceremony's origin check. Main derives the accepted `rpId` from the control-plane origin it already holds — pinned when the install was paired to its control plane, held in main-process configuration, and **never chosen by the renderer or carried on a bridge call** — and validates the server-issued options against that, which is what I-021-16 has always required and what "build-pinned" understated. The three legs do not degrade equally under a self-host, and this spec says which rather than implying parity: the Windows, Linux, and macOS **security-key** arms are relying-party-agnostic and work against any `rpId` the operator serves, while the macOS **platform** provider is bound by the `webcredentials:` associated-domain entitlement compiled into the signed application and matched against an `apple-app-site-association` file the relying party serves — so on a self-hosted deployment that arm is unavailable to a stock build, and reaching it means the operator ships a signed build of their own carrying their own domain. That is an [ADR-020](../decisions/020-v1-deployment-model-and-oss-license.md) self-hosting parity residual, named here rather than discovered at deployment: a self-host on macOS degrades to the security-key arm, and to the DAG loopback if no key is attached, with the reason surfaced rather than presented as a failed ceremony.

### Auto-Update Flow

1. Main process schedules update checks on a configurable cadence (default: every four hours, additionally on app startup, additionally on user request)
2. On available update: download to a temp location and verify the signature against the bundled update-signing public key
3. Post-verification: stage the update artifact in the platform-appropriate location (`CSIDL_LOCAL_APPDATA` on Windows; `~/Library/Application Support` on macOS; `$XDG_DATA_HOME` on Linux)
4. Notify the renderer via `update.subscribe`; renderer surfaces the "Update ready — restart to apply" UX
