5. On restart (user-initiated via bridge `update.requestRestart()` or next natural launch): apply the staged update
6. Post-apply: perform the daemon handshake. If the daemon fails to start or reports an incompatible protocol version, roll back to the previous version and re-surface the update to try again later
7. If signature verification ever fails, discard the artifact, log the event, and re-attempt the download next cycle

Update signature verification must use an Ed25519 or ECDSA-P256 signing key pinned at build time. The public key is embedded in the shell binary and immutable for the lifetime of that binary.

### Daemon Supervision Lifecycle

1. Shell startup: read daemon config (socket path, expected version); probe the existing socket
2. If daemon not running: spawn via `utilityProcess.fork(daemonEntryPath)` (see [Implementation Notes](#utility-process-vs-child-process)); wait for `DaemonHello` readiness signal up to a 10-second timeout
3. If daemon running but version incompatible: surface to renderer; block mutating operations; permit read-only subscriptions
4. Live mode: heartbeat via `DaemonStatusRead` on 30-second cadence; missed heartbeats trigger reconnect probe
5. Daemon crash: detect via `utilityProcess` exit event; restart with backoff; after five failed attempts surface persistent error state
6. Shell shutdown: send `DaemonStop` with a 10-second grace window, then force-terminate

The Runtime settings page is the one console surface that drives this lifecycle by hand, and the one that shows what the supervisor above reports. What its stop, its restart and its separate daemon update do, what each of them refuses, and how the daemon's machine-wide values are set — one place per value and no second one — are [Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)'s and are not restated here; the page reads those facts and renders them, and edits none of them anywhere else.

## Default Behavior

- The shell auto-connects to the local daemon at shell startup (starting the daemon if needed, per [Spec-006 §Default Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#default-behavior)) and exposes renderer-accessible capabilities via the preload bridge per §Trust Stance; the renderer is not a direct daemon client
- Auto-update is **enabled** by default; user may disable it via settings
- Crash reporting is **enabled** by default with PII-stripping; user may opt out via settings
- Notifications are **enabled** by default; the mute is **global only** — [Spec-017 §Resolved Questions and V1 Scope Decisions](./017-notifications-and-attention-model.md#resolved-questions-and-v1-scope-decisions) keeps notification preferences global in V1 with per-session preferences deferred, so the console offers no per-session mute (corrected 2026-09-01; the prior sentence offered one that spec does not define)
- Deep-link protocol handler registration is performed on first launch

## Fallback Behavior

- If OS-local transport to the daemon is unavailable: fall back to loopback per [Spec-006 §Fallback Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#fallback-behavior); surface the fallback clearly in the UI
- If the daemon fails to start after five backoff attempts: enter offline read-only mode; surface the error; expose a manual retry
- If the auto-updater cannot reach the feed: skip this cycle; retry on schedule; do not block normal operation
- If a write from a settings page is refused: the control rolls back to the value it had, and the page renders the daemon's own refusal in place as an inline strip carrying try-again as a faint clickable word at the right end of its text line — no box, no icon, and no toast anywhere on that screen. Nothing ever draws as saved
- If the machine's settings file is malformed: the shell falls back to the schema's defaults, repairs the file, and the page that reads it says both happened
- If this install cannot update itself: the General settings page draws no update button at all, says so in place, and names the command that gets the newest version instead
- If a chord a rebind records is already held by another act: the Keyboard page refuses it on the row and names the act that holds it, and the chord reserved for detaching a pane is refused the same way; the row keeps the chord it had
- If the daemon is unreachable while Settings is open: every page keeps its entry in the list, each page says in place what it could not read and that nothing on it can be saved right now, and every control keeps the value it had rather than emptying or disappearing
- If WebAuthn is unavailable on **this host** (the capability probe reports no usable authenticator, or the authenticator that answered does not support the PRF extension): fall back to the CLI-equivalent Device Authorization Grant flow per Security Architecture §Control-Plane Authentication, surfaced as a `localhost:<port>/callback` browser capture. **The DAG settles authentication and not custody.** It reauthenticates the user and cannot reproduce the key-encryption key that [ADR-010 §Consequences](../decisions/010-paseto-webauthn-mls-auth.md#consequences) has the desktop derive or wrap from a PRF ceremony, so a host on this path takes that ADR's own already-decided disposition — a **password-derived KEK**, built on `ADR-021`'s ratified Argon2id + XChaCha20-Poly1305 primitives rather than a second scheme — to wrap the long-term Ed25519 identity key. The passphrase is collected in the **secret-entry window** of §Preload Bridge Contract — main has no text-entry surface of its own, and Electron's native dialogs offer none (added 2026-09-01, Codex round 2) — delivered to main once over that window's single write-only operation, used to derive the KEK, and zeroized; it is never persisted, never logged, never echoed back to any renderer, and never reachable from a session window. No E2EE feature is withheld from such a host: it is a full user whose KEK has a different provenance, and that provenance is surfaced rather than hidden. The condition is a probe result, never a platform name — this is the fallback for a host with no usable authenticator or no PRF, on any of the three platforms, and it is not "the Windows / Linux path" (re-derived 2026-09-01; every platform has a native ceremony per §WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module)
- If the OS keystore is unavailable: refuse to persist long-lived auth material; session is memory-only; surface the degradation

## Interfaces And Contracts

### Renderer → Shell → Daemon (via Preload Bridge)

- Wire format between renderer and shell: Electron IPC over `contextBridge`-exposed functions; serialization via structured clone
- Wire format between shell and daemon: JSON-RPC 2.0 with Content-Length framing per Spec-006 and ADR-009
- The shell is a transparent forwarder for the daemon contract — no method rewriting, only auth-header attachment and response-payload sanitization

### Renderer → Shell → Control Plane (via Preload Bridge)

- Wire format between shell and control plane: tRPC v11 over HTTPS for request/response (incl. relay negotiation); WebSocket (JSON-RPC 2.0) for device-presence events, and the relay WSS connection speaking the relay's binary wire frames — per ADR-014 and [Spec-028](./028-remote-control.md)
- Shell attaches `Authorization: DPoP <PASETO v4.public>` + `DPoP: <signed-proof>` headers (the RFC 9449 §7.1 scheme for a DPoP-bound token, never `Bearer`) to the tRPC/HTTPS and presence-WebSocket requests; renderer never sees them. **The relay WSS connection is the exception**: it carries no `Authorization`/`DPoP` headers — browsers cannot set custom headers on a WebSocket handshake — and authenticates solely via the R3-issued `connectionToken` presented over two `Sec-WebSocket-Protocol` subprotocol values (`paseto-v4, <base64url(connectionToken)>`), per the relay connection lifecycle ([Spec-028](./028-remote-control.md)) and [Security Architecture](../architecture/security-architecture.md) (§WebSocket authentication to relay). This `connectionToken` is auth material confined to the main process: relay negotiation (`negotiateRelay` → `RelayNegotiationResponse.connectionToken`) runs **main-process-owned** — it is not exposed on the renderer-facing generic `controlPlane.call` forwarder, and the main process consumes the token to open the relay WSS connection, so it never crosses the preload bridge to the untrusted renderer (§Preload Bridge Contract forbidden-surface list; renderer-untrusted §Trust Stance). **A second exception is the anonymous-procedure arm**: header attachment is conditioned on the shell holding a control-plane credential, so a procedure the control plane defines as anonymous is issuable with none at all — the WebAuthn sign-in options and verification pair of §WebAuthn Credential Flow are the V1 instances, since the ceremony is what mints the credential and a cold install holds none. Anonymous means authentication is not _required_, not that a held credential must be stripped: such a procedure is rate-limited per transaction rather than per principal, so a present credential changes neither admission nor throttling

### Shell ↔ OS Keystore

- macOS: Keychain Services via a native-binding library (see [Implementation Notes §Native Keystore](#native-keystore))
- Windows: Credential Manager via the same abstraction
- Linux: Secret Service (libsecret) with KWallet fallback; if neither available, surface the OS-keystore-unavailable degradation from §Fallback Behavior
- Custody-root entries (added 2026-09-01, Codex round 6): one wrapped copy of the installation's custody root key per enrolled WebAuthn credential, keyed by credential id, plus one wrapped under the §Fallback Behavior passphrase-derived KEK on a host that takes that path. These are the only keystore entries whose count grows with the user's enrolled-device count — the refresh-token and identity-key envelopes stay single entries, wrapped under the root rather than under any credential (§WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module; Plan-021 I-021-19)
- DPoP proof key (added 2026-09-01, Codex round 7): the key pair main proves possession of when it requests a token pair from the ceremony's verification procedure, generated on first use and held as its own keystore entry under the [ADR-021](../decisions/021-cli-identity-key-storage-custody.md) ladder. It is deliberately **not** wrapped under the custody root: the proof is signed before the verification reply arrives, and on a fresh install no root exists at that moment, so a root-wrapped proof key would be unreachable exactly when it is first needed

### Shell ↔ Auto-Update Feed

- Transport: HTTPS
- Artifact format: platform-appropriate (`.dmg` / `.zip` for macOS, `.exe` NSIS or MSI for Windows, `.AppImage` / `.deb` / `.rpm` for Linux) plus a manifest carrying the artifact hash and signature
- Signature algorithm: Ed25519 (preferred) or ECDSA-P256
- What the General settings page reads is this channel and nothing else: one subscription and one read over the shell's own update state, beside the facts the app reports about itself — its version, its platform, its architecture and its locale. The preferences on that page that carry no wire at all — the editor that opens files, the default checkout for a new project session, the new-session switch and the keep-awake switch — live in the machine's settings file beside the crash-report opt-out and the auto-update preference (§State And Data Implications)

## State And Data Implications

- Where the window was, how big it was, which display it was on and whether it was maximised or fullscreen are remembered across restarts in the renderer's own device-local store, beside the pane widths and read and written just as defensively (§Console Design (Meridian) §Persistence on the renderer scheme) — one home and no second copy, so the machine's settings file below holds none of it; not sensitive
- The machine's own settings file, written by the shell, holds five settings and no more: the auto-update preference, the two notification preferences, the mounted workspace folders, the crash-report opt-out, and the two node-wide browser switches. Every write is an atomic rename over a schema-checked parse with fail-closed defaults, so a half-written file is never read back and a malformed one falls back to the defaults, is repaired, and is reported on the page that reads it; a small value may additionally be sealed through the platform's own one-blob encryption. Everything else a settings page shows — the accounts, the tool servers, the projects, the browser's site data and the daemon's own facts — is the daemon's, read and written over its wire and never kept as a second copy in the shell or the renderer. Credential material is never in it
- Console layout, scroll position, selection, pins, and expansion sets — UI state only, never user-authored content: a draft, its staged files and a review's held notes are daemon-held and session-scoped and never enter this store, so they are covered by the daemon's own [Spec-020](./020-data-retention-and-gdpr.md) selectors rather than by a renderer one — persisted **per install** in IndexedDB on the privileged renderer scheme (§Console Design (Meridian) §Persistence on the renderer scheme) — never a projection, which is re-derived on reconnect; this is the one renderer-storage tier the shell has, it holds no auth material, and it exists only once the scheme is registered `standard: true` before `app.ready` (Plan-021 I-021-11), the persistence layer running on an in-memory adapter and saying so until then
- Auth material (tokens, keys) persisted to OS keystore; never shell config, never renderer storage — the renderer-scheme tier above is the only renderer storage and is out of bounds for it
- Daemon session token cached in shell memory for the lifetime of the session; rotated on daemon restart per Security Architecture §Session Token
- Supervisor state (daemon PID, last-heartbeat timestamp, restart attempt count) persisted in shell-local state for crash-recovery diagnostic only
- Update artifact cache: shell-owned; cleared after successful apply or after a configurable retention window

## Example Flows

- `Example: First-run onboarding.` The shell starts with no daemon running. The shell launches the daemon as a utility process, waits for `DaemonHello` readiness, and loads the renderer. The renderer displays the first-run three-way-choice onboarding surface (relay selection) per Spec-023 (BL-081). User selects "free public relay". The shell writes the choice to daemon config and forwards the daemon-configured relay URL to the renderer. The renderer displays the sign-in surface.

- `Example: Passkey sign-in.` Renderer calls `bridge.webAuthn.signIn()` with no arguments. Shell fetches the server-issued challenge and relying-party options from the control plane, validates them against the **paired** control-plane origin (I-021-16 — not a build constant, which would refuse every self-hosted deployment), and invokes this host's per-platform binding (§WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module); the authenticator its probe found prompts — Touch ID on macOS, the Windows Hello / security-key dialog on Windows, an attached FIDO2 key on Linux. Authenticator returns the assertion plus PRF output, which stays **provisional** until the relying party admits it. Shell submits the assertion, and the verification reply carries the verdict, the signing credential's user-verification mode, and a freshly issued DPoP-bound access/refresh pair. Only then does the shell derive its wrapping key, unwrap the installation's custody root with it — or mint one, on a first enrolment — wrap the new refresh token under that root, cache the access token + DPoP key in main-process memory, and return only the user identity claims to the renderer. (Re-derived 2026-09-01 across Codex rounds 5 and 6; the earlier form validated against a build-pinned RP, derived before verifying, and unwrapped a stored token a cold install does not have.)

- `Example: Auto-update applied.` Shell hits the update feed on schedule; finds a new version; downloads and verifies the signature; stages the artifact; emits an `update:ready` event. Renderer surfaces "Update ready — restart to apply." User clicks; shell calls `app.relaunch()` after a graceful daemon shutdown; the new shell launches; the daemon handshake succeeds; the user sees the new version.

- `Example: Daemon crash mid-session.` The session engine process exits abnormally; shell's `utilityProcess` exit handler fires. Shell surfaces "Local runtime disconnected — reconnecting…" to the renderer. Shell restarts the daemon with exponential backoff; on recovery the daemon replays its event log and the renderer resubscribes. User sees the timeline catch up and the session resume.

## Signature Feature Composition Sketches

Each V1 Signature Feature view must compose daemon and control-plane state via the preload bridge. The owning plan listed in parentheses is the canonical source of feature behavior; the renderer is a read-and-steer projection of that behavior, not the source of truth. The section also carries operator-plane views that compose under the identical rule without themselves being Signature Features; each such view says so in its own `Note`, so the Signature Feature enumerations elsewhere in this spec continue to name exactly the features they always did.

### Timeline View — the "everything happens here" surface (→ Plan-011 Live Timeline And Reasoning Surfaces)

- Data sources: daemon event-log subscription (`bridge.daemon.subscribe('session.events', …)` per Spec-011); control-plane presence subscription for the linked-device liveness badge
- Renders: chronological event stream (messages, tool calls, approvals, diffs, agent reasoning, interventions, state transitions) per Spec-011
- Interactions: scroll-to-tail, jump-to-event-by-ID, filter-by-actor / event-type, "replay from here" via Spec-013 replay contract, and the per-row edit-and-resend entry point on user `user.message` rows (placement bullet below)
- Edit-and-resend placement: the entry point for Plan-003's atomic edit-and-resend composite is a hover-revealed pencil affordance in the user `user.message` row's footer, opening an inline editor with a Cancel action and a confirm action. It is an **entry point to the existing `rollback` control, not a new run control** — the V1 control set stays closed as [Spec-003 §Resolved Questions and V1 Scope Decisions](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#resolved-questions-and-v1-scope-decisions) states it. Its eligibility predicate, three-state visibility (hidden while the activation arm is dormant, disabled without the declared provider capability, disabled-with-stated-reason on transient guard failure), and dim-not-remove in-flight treatment are owned by Plan-003; this view renders that projection and never re-derives it. Confirm-action copy states that the replacement is **queued and sends on the next Resume** — the replacement send is admitted run-bound and is not dispatched, so the run lands `paused`. Plan-003 authors the affordance in its `run-controls/` subtree and Plan-011 mounts it at the row-footer composition point (CP-003-15); neither plan edits the other's subtree
- Input-ask card: an input-kind `driver_ask` is not a row of this surface. It is the question card in the composer, in the place the approval card takes, so exactly one card ever waits on a person at a time: the eyebrow reads question with the agent's short header beside it, the ask is the title, its structured options are rows carrying a mark, a label, a description and a number key on the first nine, the row the agent marked recommended stays where the agent put it and carries a recommended mark with the provider's own suffix stripped from its label, and a dashed something-else row with a text field stands last, so the free-text answer arm is **unconditional** ([Spec-011 §Timeline Entry Types](011-live-timeline-visibility-and-reasoning-surfaces.md#timeline-entry-types)). Several asks page one at a time with the count beside the eyebrow, picks and typed text surviving the paging both ways and every answer going back together on the last page. A **permission**-kind ask is not drawn as a question at all — it normalizes into the approval model and takes the same place under §Approvals View, and the `kind` discriminator is what keeps one card rendering any given ask. Answers dispatch through the already-registered `driver.respondToRequest`, and the card settles its resolved and cancelled states from the corresponding `driver_ask.*` rows. The daemon holds the request open with no timer, so the card carries no countdown, no expiry and no expired state: it waits until it is answered, is rebuilt on reload and on every linked device with the first answer settling it everywhere, and is cancelled with an interrupted turn. Answering lands one turn of the person's own carrying, per ask, the question as a faint quoted line with the answer under it, and that turn is the record — it cannot be answered again. Plan-011 owns the card and its states; this view renders that projection
- The surface the rows sit on is the `timeline` pane kind (§Pane kinds), which fixes the flow's single width, its derived row gap, its history paging, its drawn window, and the one path every jump to a row takes. Four surfaces may cover it and no others: the plan reader, the command palette and the quick look over a picture or a document, each on a light scrim with the transcript and the composer still running underneath, and the find box, drawn on the surface's own corner with no scrim and nothing beneath it moving. Nothing else is drawn over the transcript.
- Copying out: the palette's copy-conversation act puts the whole open session on the clipboard in the form the screen reads — the same markdown a reply copies and the same plain text a message copies, in the order they are drawn — and the per-message and per-reply copy controls stay. Nothing exports, prints, publishes or reads a transcript aloud; those two are the only ways a transcript leaves the screen.
- State handling: live-tailing mode vs historical-browse mode; local projection cache invalidation on daemon reconnect
- Owning plan: Plan-011

### Session Composer — the shell chrome every session view carries (→ Plan-021 Desktop Shell And Renderer)

- Data sources: the driver capability read and the provider command-and-skill enumeration through `bridge.daemon.call(…)`, both node-local and both scoped to the session's own provider, account and model binding ([Spec-004 §The provider command and skill surface](004-provider-driver-contract-and-capabilities.md#the-provider-command-and-skill-surface)); the context figure and the rate-limit reading the Plan-011 surfaces this spec mounts already carry; and the draft, its staged files and the review notes being held, all three daemon-held and session-scoped rather than kept in window storage.
- **One agent, one composer.** The card addresses the session's own agent and nothing else. There is no target picker, no second addressee, no machine picker and no posture control anywhere on it, and nothing on it attaches an agent, a provider or a model to a running session: another agent takes part only when the session's own agent delegates to it, or when the person names one from the Sidekicks group of the command list, which inserts the provider's own form for addressing it. A change to the provider, the model, the reasoning effort or the output speed is one configuration mutation on that agent (`agent.configUpdate`) which applies at a run boundary, so it is acknowledged as pending rather than drawn as already in effect, and its `pending` / `applied` / `degraded` / `failed` disposition is rendered from the daemon's answer ([Spec-014 §Same-Agent Provider Switch](014-multi-agent-channels-and-orchestration.md#same-agent-provider-switch)); the account a session runs on is changed in the inspector and lands at that same boundary. What a run may do on its own is the permission level on the toolbar below, which applies from the next turn, and the composer carries no second reading of it.
- **Every capability gate is the daemon's own report.** The composer derives none of them. A control the session's provider, account or model cannot run is absent rather than greyed — the levels the level control lists, the rungs the effort control offers, the compaction control behind the context ring — on the fail-closed-projection discipline `I-003-24` states, since a disabled control would assert the capability exists and is momentarily unavailable, which would be false. The one named exception is the output-speed control, which is drawn on every session and greys in place with its reason in its own hover title where the chosen model has no fast output (§Rules every console surface obeys). Where the provider refuses a speed a turn asked for, the refusal is one act row in the transcript in words and the control reads its value alone: no suffix, bracket or mark is ever added to a chip.
- State handling: the command-and-skill enumeration is a **live read held for the session's current binding and nothing longer** — not persisted, not cached across sessions, and re-read rather than patched when the provider announces that its own set changed, which is what keeps the list from offering a definition the running provider no longer has. The compaction control's completed state renders only on the daemon's own compaction row rather than on the call returning.
- **The card.** A floating card at the transcript's own content width, read from the same root token the transcript column reads so the two move together at every width and every text size, docked one gutter's gap off the bottom with the gutters beside it still clickable. Its children run top to bottom: a hairline that takes the strong edge colour while the session is stopped; the worktree setup card while one runs; the working line; the approvals card while a request waits; the composer proper — the command list, the attachment row, the draft line with send at its end, and the toolbar; and the session strip, which carries the project chip, the worktree switcher, the attach control on a chat session, and, right-aligned, the sidekicks badge with its count. A press on the card's own empty space puts the caret in the message box, unless one of the card's menus is open, in which case the press closes that menu and the closing hands the caret back. Focusing the draft strengthens the card's border; the message box has no focus ring of its own. The draft grows from one line to the smaller of about eight lines of the current text size and about a third of the transcript's visible height, and scrolls inside itself past that; the transcript's bottom padding is republished to the card's measured height plus its gap whenever the card resizes, so the newest row stays readable above it, and a growing approvals strip, command list or setup card pushes the transcript up rather than covering it.
- **The working line** reads, left to right: the activity indicator; the action words; then, from the moment the turn starts, the elapsed clock and the token figure, each behind its own separator; then, each with its own separator and only while it has something to say, the state slot, the tasks word and the count of unanswered questions; then the pause word and the interrupt word. There is no "working" word — the indicator and the action words carry it. The line sits at the head of the card above the draft and is drawn whenever it has something to say, absent on an idle session and while a plan waits; the card grows upward for it, so the draft never moves when a run starts or ends, and it is never an empty reserved strip. Nothing in the row wraps, and the action words are the only part that truncates, with their full text in their own hover title. The action words say what the agent is doing now, hold the last step's words while reasoning streams between steps, read as thinking before the turn's first step has landed, stay faint after a pause or an interrupt, and read as sending from the press of send until the turn starts, with no clock and no token figure beside them then. While any command runs they are pressable and open the running-commands list in place above the line: one entry per command in the order they started, each with a live timer, its own stop word — the same word that command's transcript row carries — and a live tail of its output that follows the bottom and pauses while it is scrolled up, with a last row that stops every running command in the session and touches no agent. The tail is a window onto the command's own transcript row and never a second copy of its output, and the line itself never counts commands. The token figure reads a down arrow and the tokens received this turn, starting at zero when the turn starts, stepping up once per model round rather than per word, holding across a pause, muting with the clock after a stop, and hidden while the line is the connection line; no other live token figure stands anywhere on the screen. The state slot holds one reading at a time and is absent with its separator when there is none — thinking while reasoning streams, pausing, paused, or interrupted with what is still running after it. Because the slot sits after the clock, nothing before the clock changes on a pause or an interrupt: the indicator freezes, the clock and the token figure mute where they stand, the action words fade, and the clock never moves again. The tasks word is drawn only while the turn has a task list and opens that list in place above the line, one list open at a time with the running-commands list. A lost connection turns the indicator amber, swaps the action words for the connection state, puts the reconnecting or not-connected wording after the clock, hides the token figure, the state slot, the tasks word and the controls, and puts the retry control at the right — all at the row's own height and type, so nothing grows.
- **Commands.** A leading slash or currency character opens the command list, which assistive technology reads as a list of choices and which stays open only while the draft holds no newline and a row still matches what is typed; a word no row matches closes the list rather than refusing the word, because that word is going to the provider as typed. Its order is the console's own words first, each with one line of help and its caught spellings matched but never drawn as rows, then on a Claude Code session every other word that provider's frame offers, marked as the provider's and sent as typed, then the skills both providers carry with their origin marks, then a sidekicks group under its own heading whose row inserts the provider's own form for addressing one, then one group per working tool server under the server's own name holding the prompts it publishes. The console's own words are intercepted in the client and never forwarded as text: most press a console control, and the four bridges do their work through the daemon on the provider's own verb. A word on no list is sent as typed and the provider answers it as it would in its own terminal; there is no escape for a literal slash and no unknown-command row. A word that does work of its own lands one small quiet act row in the transcript; a word that presses a control lands exactly what that control's own press lands, which for most is no row at all. The arrows move and wrap, tab completes, Enter runs or completes, and Escape closes the list for the word it was open on and leaves the draft as it is. The draft draws its text plainly — no word in it is tinted, underlined or marked — because the open list is where a recognised word is confirmed. A file mention opens a ranked search of the working folder's files, run on the machine that holds them rather than in this window, waiting a beat after the last keystroke instead of searching per keystroke, retrying once when that machine could not be reached, dropping an answer a newer search has overtaken, and keeping the rows already shown while the next answer lands; it says which state it is in rather than letting an empty list stand in for a failure. An accepted mention is plain text in the draft and renders as a mono chip on the sent turn where it matched a real file.
- **Interrupt and pause.** Interrupt is the working line's last word and Escape is its key once nothing else is open to close; the composer carries no stop control, send never becomes anything else, and there is no cancel-turn anywhere. It interrupts the lead and only the lead: the turn ends, the command it was running is ended, its running agents are spared, every unfinished live timer in the transcript settles, and one act row lands. Anything pending goes at once as the next turn, and with nothing pending the state slot says the lead is interrupted and waiting. While agents the session dispatched are still live the word gives way to interrupt-everything, which stops every one of them at every depth from the daemon's own tree, one stop per identity with partial failure carried on the row it failed for and nothing atomic, and returns any still-pending text to the draft one line per message in send order. Pause sits before interrupt while a turn runs, on the session and in every child's view, and is a toggle with no key: the word turns amber, the state slot reads pausing while the step in flight finishes and paused once nothing runs, the clock freezes where it stands, and pressing the word again or sending anything continues from exactly there with nothing repeated. There is no pause-now, no resume word standing in pause's place and no pause row in the transcript; pending rows stay pending while paused and go as the continue; and interrupt stays available throughout.
- **The attachment row and send.** The attachment row is drawn only while something is staged, and holds one chip per staged thing with its own removal control: one chip per file the attach control picked, one chip for the marks a `browser` pane sent, and one chip for the review notes the person is holding. Attach opens the platform's own picker through `native.showOpenDialog` — files only, several at once, no type filter, never a folder — and staging writes nothing into the draft: the chip is the whole record of what will go with the message, and a folder reaches an agent only as a path typed or dropped in. Staging is bounded by what the daemon and the provider accept, and those figures are read from them rather than written on the screen: a file that does not fit is refused in the row's own one-line refusal naming that file and the limit it hit, and where several were picked at once the line says how many were taken — a file is never dropped in silence. A file still being copied says so on its chip and send waits for the copy, so a drop followed straight by Enter never loses it. Send is the one filled control on the card, at the right end of the draft line, sharing one diameter and one centre line with the context ring below it; it is dimmed only while the message carries no text, no staged file and no held notes, and it carries no other state — a message that is only a file goes as it is, with no sentence written for it. Enter sends and shift-Enter inserts a newline. A message lands in the transcript the moment send is pressed, whether or not a turn is running, drawn as an outline with the time it was sent and settling in place the moment the daemon has it, so no row re-mounts under the reader; several stack in send order as the transcript's last rows while the agent's new rows are inserted above them. A send that fails takes its row back out of the transcript and puts everything it carried back into the composer — the words, the staged files and the held notes — with the caret where it was, and lands one act row naming the cause. From an empty draft the up and down arrows walk back through the messages this session has sent and forward again, returning whatever was typed before the walk; they recall only from an empty draft, so they never collide with moving the caret through text.
- **The toolbar's controls.** Six value controls, drawn on every session: the model control, reading the model with its context window on its own rows; the effort control, reading the reasoning effort as one word and opening the one ladder — minimal, low, medium, high, extra high, max — with the rungs the model lacks absent, each rung mapped one to one onto the provider's own name and applied from the next turn; the output-speed control, reading fast or standard; the mode control, which swaps its glyph with its word between build and plan; the permission-level control, reading the session's level and opening the five levels in one fixed order from the most careful to the least — read-only, which reads on its own and asks before any change; ask, which edits files on its own and asks before running commands; reviewed, where the provider's own automatic check approves routine work and asks only on the rest; sandboxed, which never asks and writes only inside the worktree; and YOLO, which never asks and has no limits at all — with a one-line note on each; and the tool-servers control, reading how many of the session's tool servers are switched on and opening them as a grouped list with a session-level switch on every row under one link into the MCP servers settings page. Every one of them reads its value as a word and never carries a pressed tint, no value chip ever looks selected beside its neighbours, and each opens its list in place above the chip, hung off the dock rather than inside the card, which clips its overflow — a list that is present but half clipped is a defect. A control whose value differs from what the session started on carries one small mark saying so and loses it the moment the value matches again; that mark is the only sign of a departure. The level list never shows a provider's own mode name and never shows plan, which is the mode control's, and a level the session's provider, account or model cannot run is absent rather than greyed. Beside the six, the right-hand cluster holds the attach control and then the context ring at the far right: one figure and one meter, grey on grey, never coloured, with a faint notch on its arc at the compaction bound, opening the compaction control in place above it. Nothing on this screen estimates what a draft will cost — the ring's figure is the one the provider reported, and no local count of the text being typed is drawn. Once the card is measured too narrow to hold them — the chips' own width at the current text size against what is left beside the ring and send, measured and never a figure — every value chip gives way to one overflow chip whose menu carries a row per control reading that control's name and its current value, and picking a row opens that control's own list exactly as its chip would; the right-hand cluster never folds, nothing else in the card reflows, and the chips are back the moment they fit again. The composer carries no standing attachment tray, no emoji picker, no formatting toolbar, no clear control, no coloured context meter, no abort control, no second send variant, no send-later or schedule, and no count of the draft; it proposes no text of its own, and the draft is the platform's own text field, so the operating system's dictation, spelling, substitutions and input methods work in it and the console adds no dictation control, microphone or speech path of its own.
- **The ask rail** rides the transcript's own right edge inside its scroller and is pointer-only: invisible at rest, fading in once the pointer has rested inside the rail's own edge zone and out again a moment after it leaves, hidden from assistive technology, carrying no focusable control, not drawn while the session holds fewer than two asks, and not drawn at all while the transcript column is at its floor, so a narrow window or a pane taking the width leaves the transcript its whole measure. It carries one hairline tick per message the person sent and none for an agent turn or a tool row; the tick of the ask nearest the middle of the visible band is filled in the accent; hovering a tick shows a one-line label with the first words of that ask, and pressing it scrolls the transcript there. There is no preview card, no widening and no jump arrow, and two chords give the keyboard the same two jumps without adding anything to the screen.
- **The jump control** sits above the card inside the dock, riding the card's height, and is drawn only while the transcript's follow is released; nothing is drawn over the transcript for it and nothing else moves. One press lands on the newest row and pins the follow again, scrolling to the tail pins it too, and a session opens pinned with nothing drawn.
- Owning plan: Plan-021
- Note: shell chrome, not a V1 Signature Feature — it is the composition point the Signature Feature views mount their composer-area affordances into, and its addition leaves the Signature Feature enumerations in §Scope and §Acceptance Criteria naming exactly the features they already named.

### Approvals View (→ Plan-010 Approvals, Permissions, Trust Boundaries)

- Data sources: the daemon's approval subscription and its approval projection, both read through the bridge; the remembered rules the daemon holds and evaluates. What an approval is, when one is raised, and how a remembered rule is scoped and evaluated are [Spec-010 §Required Behavior](./010-approvals-permissions-and-trust-boundaries.md#required-behavior)'s and [Spec-010 §Default Behavior](./010-approvals-permissions-and-trust-boundaries.md#default-behavior)'s; this view is the one surface that draws them, and it re-derives no rule of its own.
- **One card, in the composer.** A waiting request renders inside the composer above the draft, so a person answers without leaving the transcript. It is never a modal, a toast, a free-standing transcript card or a pane of its own, and the console carries no approvals list and no approval history anywhere. Exactly one is shown at a time and a queue is worked one item at a time: with more than one waiting the card reads its place in the queue beside its eyebrow — `1 of 3` — and with one waiting it carries no count at all.
- **What the card holds.** A two-pixel accent inset top rule rather than a fill; an eyebrow reading approval; the title over its summary line; one reason line under the summary in the provider's own words saying why this particular act stopped, stripped of control characters and terminal escapes before it is drawn and absent rather than empty where the provider sent none; and, in a fixed-width block, the exact thing it is asking about, whole — the command for a command, the path for a read or a write, the text for an input — wrapping rather than cutting and scrolling inside itself past a height derived from the card, which pushes the transcript up rather than covering it and keeps its actions in reach. That one line is editable in place before the card is answered, in its own type, and the changed text is what goes back with the answer and what the row then records as having run; there is no raw form of the call and no fourth action.
- **Three actions, in one fixed order:** decline, then a standing allow for this session on the subject the daemon derived from the ask — a command's program and first subcommand, a network request's host, a written file's name — then approve once, the hero last. The middle action carries an arrow offering the project as a second scope, a project rule being written where the session's own provider writes its own durable rules; it is absent rather than greyed on an ask the provider marks as one that must not carry a standing allow, and nothing takes its place. On a network ask decline carries the same arrow offering a block on that host, so the remembered set is allow and block rather than allow alone, and an allow and a block on one subject cannot both stand. Pressing decline opens one optional line under the actions for why not, which goes back to the agent through the provider's own refusal field and sends a bare decline when it is left empty.
- **The card exists only at the three levels that ask.** The two that answer for themselves never raise one: moving the level to one of them answers an open card and its blocked row runs, a later ask raises nothing, and moving back to a level that asks asks again.
- **It waits until it is answered.** The daemon holds the request open with no timer, so the card carries no countdown, no expiry and no expired state. It is rebuilt on reload and on every device the person has linked, the first answer settles it everywhere, and a device that was showing it closes it with a line saying it was answered elsewhere rather than with anything that reads as an error. The closers are the answer itself, an interrupt of the run — which cancels the open request and records the cancellation — a move to a level that never asks, and the provider process ending. An agent's own question and a plan waiting for a verdict take the same place on the same terms.
- **Answering.** Approving resolves the call in place: the card hides, the blocked timer stops waiting and keeps running, the working line becomes the running verb, the result lands when it settles, and the session's review data is marked stale — there is no approved banner and no extra row. Declining resolves in place the same way, the blocked call's row reading declined where its result would sit, and the session leaves the attention list. An approval a child raised is the parent's approval, answered in the parent's composer through one approval pipeline and one trust boundary; the child's own inline card answers by pressing the composer's matching action by index, so there is still exactly one answer surface, and walking to an approval from a child scrolls it into view and tints it faintly in the accent for a moment while the pane renders no second approval control. The card takes focus when it arrives while the draft has focus, landing on approve once, and that focus move never scrolls the transcript; its actions are walked with the left and right arrows and pressed with Enter, the walk stopping at each end. No single key answers it: there is no per-action keyboard accelerator, no approve-all, no per-approval notification and no cancel-turn action.
- Owning plan: Plan-010

### Runs View (→ Plan-003 Queue, Steer, Pause, Resume)

- Data sources: the daemon's run-state and queue subscriptions ([Spec-003 §Interfaces And Contracts](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#interfaces-and-contracts)). A run's state, its queue and its intervention history are the daemon's facts; the renderer derives none of them.
- **There is no runs pane.** A run is controlled where it is watched. Pause and interrupt are the last two pressable words of the working line at the head of the composer card — pause a toggle with no key, interrupt answering Escape once nothing else is open to close — and while agents the session dispatched are still live interrupt gives way to interrupt-everything. The composer carries no stop control, send never becomes anything else, and there is no cancel-turn anywhere in the console.
- **What is waiting is the transcript's own last rows.** A message sent while a turn runs lands at once as the person's own row, drawn as an outline with the time it was sent, and stays there until the agent takes it at its next step, when it settles in place without re-mounting; several stack in send order as the transcript's last rows while the agent's new rows are inserted above them. On hover or keyboard focus such a row offers four acts, the same on both providers: a grip that drags it, or the arrows with the row focused, to move it within that stack; an edit that puts its text back in the draft and holds its place until it is sent again; a removal, after which nothing reaches the agent; and a send-now that interrupts the turn, delivers that row first and lets the rest follow in order. Moving a row here changes the send order of messages the person has already sent; it is not a priority override on the daemon's admission queue, which [Spec-003 §Resolved Questions and V1 Scope Decisions](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#resolved-questions-and-v1-scope-decisions) defers for V1, so that queue's only removal path stays cancel-before-admission.
- Interactions: pause and resume on an active run; steer, interrupt and rollback through the generic intervention dispatch [Spec-003 §Interfaces And Contracts](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#interfaces-and-contracts) registers; enqueue and cancel-before-admission on the queue. All are forwarded to the daemon's run engine per the run-state domain model, and the edit-and-resend entry point on a sent message is an entry point to the rollback control rather than a run control of its own (§Timeline View).
- Owning plan: Plan-003

### Multi-Agent Channels View (→ Plan-014 Multi-Agent Channels And Orchestration)

- Data sources: daemon channel roster read via `bridge.daemon.call('channel.rosterRead')` (per-channel state + typed config + arbitration facet) and child-run links via `bridge.daemon.call('orchestration.childRunLinkRead')`, with channel/arbitration lifecycle events consumed as opaque re-read signals through the daemon event subscription — per the Tier-5 Plan-014 method-string registry (api-payload-contracts.md §Plan-014; supersedes this view's pre-audit "daemon channel subscription" gloss, D-014-18)
- Renders: channel roster with per-channel turn-policy + moderation badges from the typed `ChannelConfig` rendered wire-verbatim; round-robin turn-order indicator from the arbitration facet (`state`, `turnPolicy`, unreachable agent/node when paused); child-run links with `linkType`, `internalHelper` differentiation (visually de-emphasized, never ejected), and `visibility: reachable | unreachable`; budget state via `orchestration.budgetRead`. Run-level stop-condition outcomes (`turn_limit`, `budget_exhausted`, `idle_timeout` triggers) render in the Runs View / timeline, not here (Plan-003/Plan-011 surfaces)
- Interactions: create channel (name + create-time `ChannelConfig`); mute / unmute / archive channel via the `channel.*` lifecycle methods (one wire mutation per explicit user action). The pre-audit "mute agent" and "pause channel" interactions are struck — no such wire surface exists in V1 (agent-level mute has no method; arbitration pause is daemon-initiated, not user-initiated); "intervene" on runs belongs to the Plan-003 run-controls surface. The V1 desktop ships **no** `OrchestrationRunCreate` affordance — child runs are created via SDK/CLI and (at Tier 7) Plan-015 workflows; the desktop renders linkage and refusal records (D-014-18)
- **The sidekicks pane in the session workspace.** The `agent-console` pane kind draws the session's dispatched agents, as wide as its widest row reads at the current text size and not resizable, with a header carrying the title, its sub-line, an all-control, a divider, previous, next and close. One list view and one child view switch in place. **The list view** carries a bar with a stop-all control while anything is live and a hide-finished toggle, off by default, over one tree of every agent in the order the work happened, finished rows dimmed; a row is a button in the tab order carrying the agent's swatch on its own hue, its name and model on one line, the line saying how it was reached, its act line in its own state colour, its result once finished, an amber waiting control while it waits on a person, and a foot with tools, tokens, spend and duration. Depth indents by one step of the current text size per level and draws a hairline connector to the row's left. **The child view** holds the child's head — its swatch, its name, how it was reached, the one-line ask clamped to one line with the whole ask in its hover title, and its meta line carrying model, reasoning effort as one word, how it was reached, tokens, spend, start, and the chain walking every ancestor as pressable links — then that child's own flow in the transcript's own rendering at pane scale, with the pane's foot holding the child's own working line, any approval card inline, and the steer box with its send. Its controls are words at the end of that view's own working line rather than buttons on a card: pause and interrupt, on the same terms as the session's, neither drawn once the child is done while the steer box stays, so a send starts the child again; a child stopped for good draws that word and no box. Interrupt is the last word in the line, so every interrupt on the screen sits in one place. The pane carries no pause or stop control on a card, no resume word, no kind label, no relay note, no mechanism tag, no overflow menu, no open control, no steer button, no second approval control and no dependency graph.
- Owning plan: Plan-014
- Note: this view binds to the finalized Spec-014 surface as registered in api-payload-contracts.md §Plan-014.

### Provider Accounts And Cost View (→ Plan-026 Provider Accounts And Credential Homes)

- Data sources: the node-local `providerAccount` wire namespace through `bridge.daemon.call(…)` for the registry read, default selection, and per-account authentication probe result, and the session cost receipt read surface for spend — concrete method names for both are registered in api-payload-contracts.md per [Spec-026 §Interfaces And Contracts](026-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#interfaces-and-contracts) and [Spec-014 §Session Cost Receipt](014-multi-agent-channels-and-orchestration.md#session-cost-receipt)
- Renders: a **provider page of one section per provider**, each section in two halves — that provider's own settings above the accounts registered against it. **The provider's half** carries: the provider's own line, reading as installed and signed in with its version, as not installed on this machine, or as undecided where the check could not settle, and never as a guess; the path to that provider's command in one field, reading as auto-detected while it is empty, re-checked where it now points whenever it changes and re-reading that provider's model catalogue with it so the model control never offers a model the new command does not have, and refusing in place where nothing runnable sits there; a switch that makes the provider available for new sessions, which the last provider still on keeps live rather than dead and says on itself why it cannot go off, and which is greyed with its reason where that provider's command is not installed; a bound on how many helper processes the provider may run at one time, zero meaning the provider decides for itself and a figure outside the range the provider accepts settling back to the nearest one it does; the standing rules that provider holds on this machine, read from the provider's own files, one row per rule reading what it allows or refuses with a revoke beside it that confirms in place, a provider holding none saying so rather than drawing an empty list; the provider-level automatic-compaction bound, which moves between 50 and 95 per cent in steps of five and is mapped onto that provider's own control, drawn in both providers' sections because both expose it — a different control from a session's own bound, which sits behind that session's context ring ([Spec-011 §Context Window and Usage Meters](011-live-timeline-visibility-and-reasoning-surfaces.md#context-window-and-usage-meters)), so the row's own line says the bound is mapped onto the provider's and that the session's stays where it is; Claude Code's output style, one choice reading the style the installed build is on with that build's other styles beside it, one line describing the style it is on and one saying the choice applies to sessions started later, no style typed and no such row under Codex; and one action that imports that provider's own existing conversations, reporting into one progress row on the page — no pop-up, no wizard, no screen of its own — carrying a stop while it runs, skipping every session this console already holds and saying how many it skipped, counting the files it could not read and unfolding their paths on a press, saying which reason it is where there was nothing to bring in, and confirming in place before it runs where it would attach projects this console does not have. **The accounts half** opens with one line saying that new sessions run on the account marked default and that an agent or a workflow step can be set to a specific account instead, and then draws one row per account. **An account is named only by the identity its provider reports at sign-in** — the email address the provider returns, then the plan in the provider's own word, then the organisation where the plan carries one — with no invented name and no typed label, because one address can hold two accounts on two plans; there is no operator-authored label anywhere on this page. Beside the identity a row carries its billing-mode chip, the default mark on the one account per provider that holds it, its state in words with when it was last checked, the one remedy that applies to that state and no other, the ages the background observation writes — last checked, last refreshed, and the re-login horizon rendered as an approximation, a value the daemon reports as unknown **reading as not known rather than being fabricated** or read as though the account were fine — a freshness switch that is on by default and turns that observation off for this account alone without signing it out, and its quota windows per `(account, limit)`, each labelled with its own limit rather than merged, each a bar with its figure and its reset time in words beside it, a window that has reset reading as reset and a reading taken before that account's credential changed reading as being from before the change. Under the windows sits that account's lifetime spend carried with its billing mode, so a plan seat's usage never reads as billed dollars and an account whose billing mode nobody has declared reads as not set rather than being folded unlabelled into a currency total, and then the total across accounts, which states when it spans more than one mode ([Spec-026 §Billing mode](026-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#billing-mode)). **Never rendered here:** credential-home contents, which the renderer neither reads nor receives, and a token value, which no reply carries. The list draws its loading, empty and failure states rather than an empty list — ghost rows in the shape of the real rows while it is being read, a provider with no accounts naming the way to get one, a failure carrying try-again as one faint word, a provider whose accounts carry no default mark saying that new work on it will not start until one is marked, an account whose usage cannot be read drawing one quiet line in place of its meters rather than an empty bar, and a warning the daemon reports about one provider drawn as its own strip in that provider's section. Neither spend by period nor spend by model is drawn, because no durable table backs either, and no usage screen stands beside this page.
- Interactions: register an account, remove one, set a provider's default, correct its billing mode, start a brokered sign-in, cancel an in-flight sign-in, supply a non-interactive token, turn the background observation off for one account or back on, check one account now, reset a credential home, and run the one-time copy of the person's own provider memories into an account's own home — each forwarded to the daemon registry as one wire mutation per explicit user action, and each act on the provider's half the same: the command path, the availability switch, the helper bound, a standing rule's revoke, the compaction bound, the output style and the conversation import. **Signing out and removing are two acts and never one:** signing out ends that account's login and leaves the row exactly where it is, so the same account signs in again without being registered twice, while removing forgets the registry row itself, leaves the credential folder on the machine untouched because that folder is the person's own material, confirms first because the account's spend and history go with the row, and is refused in place while a run bound to that account is live, naming the sessions. Checking one account checks that account alone and settles in place with one line saying what it did and did not do, since the observation never signs in, never asks for a credential refresh, never reaches a first-party sign-in endpoint and never spends a model turn. There is no refresh control at all: a refresh the provider asks for is the daemon's to broker, and it shows on screen only as the last-refreshed age moving. A brokered sign-in renders the provider's verification URL and, on a device-code arm, its user code with how long it has left and then that it has expired, the URL always drawn and selectable so a browser that did not open is not a dead end, with cancel available while the attempt is in flight; **completion arrives on the registry subscription and is never inferred from a timer**, a completion reported successful updates the row only after the daemon's own observation lands ([Spec-026 §Brokered interactive sign-in](026-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#brokered-interactive-sign-in)), and a sign-in that does not finish says so where it was started, leaves the account as it was, and keeps the control that starts it again. The token field is **write-only in the renderer as well as on the wire**: masked on entry, never read back, never held in serializable renderer state a devtools inspection or crash report would capture, and cleared on submit — the wire rule protects the transport, this protects the screen. A token the provider does not accept is refused under the field it was pasted into with the field left empty and the controls still there to try another; re-supplying one targets the account already there and keeps its identity, its spend and its history; and a token account's own remedy is to mint a fresh token and paste it rather than to retry, because it carries no refresh. Moving the default mark moves which account new sessions run on and rewrites no credential, each account keeping its own home; this page sends no per-run account override at all — a session already running moves to another saved account of its provider from the session inspector's account fact, applied at the run boundary and nowhere else ([Spec-026 §Selection at run start](026-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#selection-at-run-start)). Two refusals on this page are its own rather than the daemon's: the last provider still available for new sessions keeps its switch live and says on the control why it cannot go off, and a provider that exposes no provider-level setting at all reads that the provider decides it itself — the form that section takes, drawn only where that is true, which is neither provider today because both expose the compaction bound
- State handling: every rendered figure is wire-verbatim — no client-side derivation, re-summing, or parallel per-account tally. An account's lifetime spend and the total across accounts are each read as the daemon serves them from its committed-spend fold, one figure per account and one for the set, and the renderer assembles neither of them from the other and neither from per-session receipts, which is the client-side re-aggregation [Spec-014 §Cost Figure Display Consistency](014-multi-agent-channels-and-orchestration.md#cost-figure-display-consistency) forbids. No durable spend table exists ([Spec-026 §State And Data Implications](026-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#state-and-data-implications)), which is why spend by period and spend by model are drawn on no surface at all. Account-scoped state (authentication probe result, provider quota) is held per account rather than once per provider — and quota per `(account, limit)` rather than per account, since one account may stand against several limits at once — and a reading observed under a superseded credential generation is re-read rather than rendered as current. **The page derives no eligibility of its own**: health, readiness, expiry, and which controls are available are all rendered from what the daemon reports and disabled where it reports nothing, the fail-closed-projection discipline `I-003-24` states for the edit affordance. A page that recomputed any of them would be the surface nothing enforces
- Owning plan: Plan-026
- Note: an operator-plane view, not a V1 Signature Feature — it composes through the same preload bridge under the same read-and-steer rule, and its addition leaves the Signature Feature enumerations in §Scope and §Acceptance Criteria naming exactly the features they already named.

### MCP Servers View — the settings-level governance page (→ Plan-025 MCP Server Configuration And Governance)

- Data sources: the node-local `mcp.*` wire namespace through `bridge.daemon.call(…)` for the server inventory and per-server detail, and `mcp.subscribe` — the live governance stream — as the update channel, so the page refreshes on the daemon's own transitions rather than by polling, which [Spec-025 §Status Observation and Events](025-mcp-server-configuration-and-governance.md#status-observation-and-events) forbids the daemon to do on the providers and which this page must not reintroduce from above. The page subscribes to that stream **before** it reads the inventory, so nothing that changes between the two is lost, and status arriving on it is applied to the row it names rather than triggering a whole-inventory re-read. All eleven operations are already registered by Plan-025; this view mints none — no operation, no event kind and no error — so a control that would need a new one is not a control this page draws
- Renders: a settings-level page in two panes — the server list on the left, the selected server's detail on the right. Each list row carries the server name, its provider and configuration scope, the normalized connection status, an enabled marker, and a **trust chip** whose revoked state names the daemon's own reason, including a config-drift auto-revoke, so an operator sees that trust lapsed and why rather than only that it is gone. **The connection status is one of five words and no others** — connected, starting, needs sign-in, failed, and not known yet — and this page is where they are stated: every other surface in the console that says what a tool server is doing, the composer's tool-servers chip among them, draws one of those same five from this one inventory. A server name held in two places is shown as held in two places rather than resolved away, and a server the daemon has only just observed starts untrusted, so the first act taken on it is taken against an untrusted server. With nothing selected the detail pane says so in one line and asks for a pick. The detail pane adds the server's tool inventory — per tool, whether it is on, whether it asks before running, and what happens if a call is interrupted, each carrying its effective value beside whether that value is the server's own or set here — the effective idempotency class among them, and the base-config hash identity the trust grant is bound to. Configuration content splits three ways on this page rather than being uniformly withheld, because a page that could not show or collect any would have no way to register a server at all. **(1) Operator-authored input the declaration form collects**: transport, command, arguments, URL, timeouts, the required-server marker, and environment-variable and header **names**. **(2) Read-back**, which is exactly the daemon's redacted config view and nothing beyond it — the same non-secret fields, with secret **values** structurally absent — so the page can support an edit workflow without the daemon ever serving credential material ([Spec-025 §Unified Inventory](025-mcp-server-configuration-and-governance.md#unified-inventory)). **(3) Never rendered**: any value the daemon does not serve — environment-variable values, header values, tokens, URL query values — and the page reads no provider config file, which remains the source of truth and the daemon's to read. Credential-bearing values the form does collect are **write-only in the renderer as well as on the wire**, on the Provider Accounts token-field discipline above: masked on entry, never read back, never held in serializable renderer state a devtools inspection or crash report would capture, and cleared on submit. The five governance **event** payloads remain credential-free and content-free by construction, and the page adds nothing to them
- Interactions: add or update a server declaration, remove one, enable or disable one, grant or revoke trust, set or clear a per-tool idempotency-class override, start an OAuth login, and reconnect — each forwarded to the daemon as **one wire mutation per explicit user action**, the same discipline the Multi-Agent Channels and Provider Accounts views state: nothing batches, nothing retries on the person's behalf, and nothing hides an act the daemon would refuse. Reconnect is offered on the selected server whatever its state and drawn first on one reading failed; it asks the provider to open the connection again, writes nothing and changes no setting, says so, and where it moves no state it says that nothing changed rather than reading as success. An OAuth login renders the daemon-returned authorization URL and settles on the daemon's own completion event rather than on a timer or on the browser returning — the same shape a brokered provider sign-in already takes on the Provider Accounts page. That URL is **transient render state and nothing else**: it arrives on the login reply alone, is held only for the life of the attempt, is written to no renderer storage and no log, and is dropped at settlement — the write-only discipline this page already applies to a credential-bearing form value, applied to a value that is a one-shot launch handle rather than a credential. It is admitted to the renderer as the daemon's own reply value rather than as renderer-composed input, which is the distinction the external-open path exists to police. The prohibition it does not touch is the one that matters: tokens, authorization codes, and PKCE material reach this page on no surface at all. A refusal renders the daemon's typed reason verbatim — a safety-weakening override on an untrusted server is refused before any write, and the page shows that refusal rather than pre-filtering the control, so the operator learns the rule from the system that enforces it
- State handling: every rendered value is wire-verbatim, and **the page derives no state of its own** — connection status, trust state (including a config-drift auto-revoke and its reason), enablement, tool overrides, and effective idempotency class are all rendered from what the daemon reports, the fail-closed-projection discipline `I-003-24` states for the edit affordance. A page that recomputed trust or effective class would be a second source of truth for exactly the decisions [Spec-013](013-persistence-recovery-and-replay.md) recovery depends on. **Eligibility is a separate question and this page does not project it at all**: no inventory field reports whether an operation would be permitted, so every control is offered, the daemon adjudicates, and its typed refusal renders — which is the same rule as the no-pre-filtering interaction above rather than a second one. The one non-offer is a control whose required input is **structurally absent** from the entry the daemon served — on the degraded arm the daemon serves when the trust store is unreachable, the trust state, config-hash identity, and per-tool overrides are absent outright and the enabled marker survives only where the provider itself declares it, so the trust and per-tool controls have nothing to act on while every control whose input did arrive is offered as usual — and that is absence of data, not a judgment the renderer formed. Status arriving on the subscription is applied to the row it names rather than triggering a whole-inventory re-read
- Owning plan: Plan-025
- Note: an operator-plane view, not a V1 Signature Feature — it composes through the same preload bridge under the same read-and-steer rule, and its addition leaves the Signature Feature enumerations in §Scope and §Acceptance Criteria naming exactly the features they already named.

## Implementation Notes

_This section captures architecture-relevant, non-normative implementation guidance. It will be expanded with primary-source citations from the current-state Electron ecosystem research once the research pass completes; see References §Research Conducted._

### Electron Version And Support Window

**Updated 2026-09-01.** Electron 41 reached end-of-support on 2026-08-25 with the release of Electron 44.0.0 (Chromium 152.0.7977.54, Node 24.18.1); the supported majors are 44 / 43 / 42. V1 builds target **Electron 44.x** — 44.1.1 at authoring; the pin shipped at Plan-021 T-021p-1B-4 is the exact 44.1.0, the newest 44.x this workspace's `minimumReleaseAge` guard admitted that day — and [ADR-016 §Decision](../decisions/016-electron-desktop-shell.md#decision) now carries the 42 / 43 / 44 supported-branch subset of the GHSA-3c8v-cfp5-9885 floors with **44.x the only admissible V1 runtime**. Two facts of that line the console depends on: the detached-`WebContentsView` bounds fix (electron/electron PR #53031, closing #43257) landed on 44 and was backported to 42 / 43 late in their lives, so earlier point releases on those branches clear the CVE floor and still lack the fix — rather than carry a per-branch fix floor the release feed cannot verify, V1 admits 44.x alone, and the `browser` pane kind is buildable on nothing below it; and the Electron 40 renderer-clipboard deprecation stays enforced through §Renderer Bundle. One dependency rides the floor: Electron 44 is Node ABI 149, which no `better-sqlite3` 12.x release ships a prebuild for, so the daemon's binding moves to the 13.x Node-API line before or with the pin ([ADR-022 §Decision Log](../decisions/022-v1-toolchain-selection.md#decision-log), 2026-09-01). The paragraphs below are the 2026-04-17 record, kept as provenance for the floor derivation.

V1 builds must target **Electron 41.x** (stable release 2026-03-10, Chromium 146.0.7680.65, V8 14.6, Node v24.14.0) at minimum patch version **41.1.0** to pick up the Q1 2026 high-severity CVE batch (CVE-2026-34769, -34770, -34771, -34772, -34774, -34764, all fixed 2026-04-02 in 39.8.5 / 40.8.5 / 41.1.0). 41.1.0 also subsumes the earlier [CVE-2026-34776](https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-34776) fix ([GHSA-3c8v-cfp5-9885](https://github.com/electron/electron/security/advisories/GHSA-3c8v-cfp5-9885), out-of-bounds heap read in `requestSingleInstanceLock()` second-instance IPC parser on macOS and Linux; shipped in 41.0.0), whose per-branch floors are the source-of-truth in [ADR-016 §Decision](../decisions/016-electron-desktop-shell.md#decision). Electron 42 is scheduled for 2026-05-05; by V1 GA the target is expected to be Electron 42.x.

Electron has **no LTS lane**. The project team supports the latest three stable majors on an 8-week release cadence aligned with Chromium's 4-week stable channel. Support windows as of 2026-04-17:

- Electron 37: EOL 2026-01-13
- Electron 38: EOL 2026-03-10
- Electron 39: EOL 2026-05-05
- Electron 40: EOL 2026-06-30
- Electron 41: EOL 2026-08-25

**Consequence:** Plan-021 must budget two forced major-version upgrades in V1's first year after ship. Same-week patch adoption is required on security-advisory drops. This is the single largest recurring operational cost of choosing Electron and is accepted per ADR-016; Plan-021 must include release-engineering capacity for the cadence.

Sources:

- [Electron release timeline](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/tutorial/electron-timelines)
- [Release schedule](https://releases.electronjs.org/schedule)
- [Electron 41.0 release notes](https://www.electronjs.org/blog/electron-41-0)
- [GitHub Security Advisories, electron/electron](https://github.com/electron/electron/security/advisories)

### Electron Fuses

Electron Fuses are build-time toggles on the shipped binary that harden the binary against misuse as a generic Node runtime or against injection vectors. The posture declared above (RunAsNode disabled, NodeOptions disabled, CLI inspect disabled, asar integrity enabled, OnlyLoadAppFromAsar enabled, cookie encryption enabled) matches the current Electron-documented production hardening recommendation and the posture VS Code, Slack, and 1Password ship with.

### Utility Process Vs Child Process

The shell supervises the daemon via `utilityProcess.fork()` (introduced in Electron 22; production-ready since Electron 24) rather than raw `child_process.fork()`. `utilityProcess` is Chromium-Services-backed (not Node's native `child_process`), which gives the daemon:

- an isolated V8 instance
- MessagePort-based IPC with `MessagePortMain` transfer via `postMessage(msg, [transfer])` — survives across process boundaries
- exit-event propagation the shell can hook for crash recovery
- integration with Electron's Crashpad reporting pipeline
- participation in the shell's structured-clone IPC

The daemon does not inherit the shell's Chromium command-line flags or memory pressure. `utilityProcess.fork()` must be called after `app.ready`.

Exit-reason handling: as of Electron 40, `utilityProcess` exits may carry the reason `"memory-eviction"` — this is the OS reclaiming memory from a backgrounded process, not a crash. Supervisor logic must treat `"memory-eviction"` distinctly from crash reasons (restart with the standard backoff, but without incrementing the failure counter that feeds the "five-attempts-then-surface-error" rule in §Daemon Supervision Lifecycle).

Gotcha: `utilityProcess` has no direct network-interception API equivalent to a renderer's `session` object. If the daemon needs Electron's network stack (for cert pinning, for example), requests must proxy back through the shell main process. If the daemon is content with Node's native `https`, this is moot.

Sources:

- [Electron utilityProcess API](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/utility-process)
- [Electron 40.0 release notes](https://www.electronjs.org/blog/electron-40-0) — `"memory-eviction"` exit reason
- [Electron Message Ports tutorial](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/tutorial/message-ports)

### Native Keystore

`node-keytar` was archived by its maintainers on 2022-12-15 (last release v7.9.0, 2022-02-17) and is **not** a supported dependency for new projects. The 2026 replacement adopted by this project is **`@napi-rs/keyring`** 2.1.0 (MIT, 47 kB plus one native package per platform — a napi-rs Rust binding to the `keyring-rs` crate, self-described as a "100% compatible node-keytar alternative"). It supports macOS Keychain, Windows Credential Manager, and Linux Secret Service. It does **not** require libsecret on Linux (it uses `secret-service-rs`), which matters in headless CI, WSL, and Codespaces environments. It is also the replacement path used by the Microsoft Authentication Library for JS and the Azure Identity SDK.

For simple encrypt-one-blob use cases (e.g., small settings values), Electron's main-process `safeStorage` API is acceptable. `safeStorage` uses Keychain on macOS, DPAPI on Windows, and Secret Service / kwallet / libsecret on Linux. Exposed methods: `isEncryptionAvailable`, `encryptString`, `decryptString`, `getSelectedStorageBackend` (Linux only).
