**Critical Linux gotcha for V1:** When no OS keystore is available, `safeStorage` silently falls back to a **hardcoded plaintext password** — i.e., secrets are unprotected. This is explicit in the Electron docs. For AI Sidekicks, which holds PASETO refresh tokens, DPoP keys, and WebAuthn PRF-derived wrapping material, the shell must:

1. Call `safeStorage.isEncryptionAvailable()` at startup
2. On Linux, additionally call `safeStorage.getSelectedStorageBackend()` and reject the values `'basic_text'` and `'unknown'`
3. On a non-protective backend, **refuse to persist long-lived auth material** — degrade to memory-only session per §Fallback Behavior, and surface the degradation prominently

The same rule applies to `@napi-rs/keyring` on headless Linux without Secret Service: the abstraction layer must detect the no-keystore case and refuse to persist auth material rather than silently falling back.

Sources:

- [`node-keytar` archive notice](https://github.com/atom/node-keytar) — "archived by the owner on Dec 15, 2022"
- [`@napi-rs/keyring` 2.1.0](https://www.npmjs.com/package/@napi-rs/keyring)
- [MSAL JS keytar migration issue](https://github.com/AzureAD/microsoft-authentication-library-for-js/issues/7170) — corroborating MSAL's move off keytar
- [Electron `safeStorage` API](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/safe-storage) — explicit Linux plaintext-fallback behavior

### Build And Packaging

`electron-builder` v26.9.0 (released 2026-04-14) is the packaging tool for V1 — re-pinned 2026-09-01 to the **26.15.x** line (26.15.3 is the registry `latest`, 2026-06-09; 26.15.7, 2026-07-18, sits under the `v26` dist-tag). Multi-platform coverage: macOS `.dmg` and `.zip`, Windows NSIS and MSI, Linux `.AppImage` / `.deb` / `.rpm` / Snap / Flatpak. Reasoning: cross-platform installer-format coverage is broader than Electron Forge's, production-proven at the scale of VS Code / 1Password / Slack, and integrates cleanly with `electron-updater` for the update path on all three platforms.

**Alternative considered:** Electron Forge. Forge v7.11.1 (2026-01-12) is the Electron team's officially-maintained packaging tool, and the electronjs.org docs recommend it as the default. Forge v8.0.0 (the ESM release) is still in alpha as of 2026-04-10; the Forge README explicitly directs production users to v7.x. Forge wins on "closer to Electron's official cadence and less third-party surface." It loses on auto-update: Forge requires wiring `update.electronjs.org` or a custom updater, neither of which covers Linux at all.

Decision: `electron-builder` wins for this spec because cross-platform auto-update on macOS + Windows + Linux is a V1 requirement (see §Auto-Updater below). Plan-021 must pin to `electron-builder` v26.15.x minor versions (re-derived 2026-09-01) and review the changelog on every bump (electron-builder is outside "official Electron support," per the Electron docs). **Electron binary materialization (landed 2026-09-01, Plan-021 T-021p-1B-4).** The 44.x line publishes no install script, and the change it inherits is **Electron 42.0's**, not 44's: `docs/breaking-changes.md` §Breaking API Changes (42.0) records both that `electron` "no longer downloads itself via `postinstall` script" and that `ELECTRON_SKIP_BINARY_DOWNLOAD` "is no longer supported, as its primary purpose was to prevent the `postinstall` script from running" — 41.6.1 published `postinstall: node install.js` and every manifest from 42 through 45 carries no `scripts` field at all — binary acquisition having moved into a lazy download at module scope in `npm/index.js`, which every `require('electron')` reaches. A plain `pnpm install` therefore plants the launcher shim and no `dist/`, and the first process to require Electron pays a **120–160 MB** platform download wherever it happens to run (130 MB for `darwin-arm64` at 44.1.0; 158 MB for `win32-x64`), including inside a test's own timeout. The binary is materialized at INSTALL time instead: `apps/desktop` carries an idempotent `postinstall` that runs Electron's own install entry point when the binary is absent, skips when it is present, fails closed if it is still absent afterwards, and honours `ELECTRON_SKIP_BINARY_DOWNLOAD` — the escape 42.0 dropped, reinstated by this wrapper on its own terms rather than by Electron's — so a CI leg that never launches Electron stays fast. Every consumer calls that one script rather than a second acquisition path, and the smoke harness refuses before spawning, naming the remedy, rather than timing out.

**Reproducibility limit:** Neither `electron-builder` nor Electron Forge guarantees bit-reproducible binaries out of the box. No primary source found in the 2026-04 research pass claims Electron apps can be bit-reproducibly built today. If Spec-021 needs reproducibility (supply-chain verification, for example), Plan-021 must architect supporting infrastructure (SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH, deterministic filesystem ordering, stripped metadata). V1 does **not** claim reproducible builds; this is an accepted scope gap to revisit in V1.1.

Sources:

- [electron-builder releases — v26.15.x line (re-pinned 2026-09-01 from v26.9.0)](https://github.com/electron-userland/electron-builder/releases)
- [Electron Forge v7.11.1 / v8.0.0-alpha](https://github.com/electron/forge)
- [Electron Forge overview](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/tutorial/forge-overview)
- [electron.build](https://www.electron.build)

### Auto-Updater

`electron-updater` v6.8.4 (released 2026-04-14, part of the `electron-builder` project) drives the auto-update flow — re-pinned 2026-09-01 to **6.8.9** (2026-06-05), the registry `latest`; the manifest schema (`sha512` per-file entries) Plan-021's trust chain binds to is unchanged across the line. Code-signature validation on **both macOS and Windows**. Staged rollouts (0–100% gradual) supported. Squirrel.Windows is explicitly unsupported and must not be used.

Update feed hosting: project-operated static artifact store (S3-backed) with an Ed25519-signed manifest. Update signing key is distinct from the code-signing certificate and is rotated on a documented schedule.

**Delta update posture:** `electron-updater` ships block-map-based differential updates on Windows/NSIS only. macOS DMGs and Linux AppImages download full payloads. Plan-021 must budget bandwidth accordingly (this is a material difference from browser-style delta updates and should not be assumed).

**Rollback absence:** Neither `electron-updater` nor Electron's built-in `autoUpdater` module ships automatic rollback on failed update. The "Update Flow" requirement of this spec (roll back on signature-verification failure or post-install daemon-handshake failure) is **architecture we must build**, not an out-of-box feature. Plan-021 must include the rollback state machine (prior-version artifact retention, launcher-based version selection, or a dual-slot approach) as a first-class component.

**MSIX (Windows Store) path:** Electron 41 added MSIX auto-updating via the same JSON response format as Squirrel.Mac (per Electron RFC #21). Not required for V1 (V1 ships NSIS / MSI installers direct-download). Re-evaluate MSIX for V1.1 if Windows Store distribution becomes a target.

**Alternative (not chosen):** Electron's built-in `autoUpdater` module uses Squirrel.Mac on macOS and Squirrel.Windows (or MSIX updater, auto-detected) on Windows; it has **no built-in Linux support**. The hosted `update.electronjs.org` service is restricted to public GitHub repos, macOS (DMG) + Windows (NSIS) only, and requires signed macOS builds. Neither covers V1's Linux requirement.

Sources:

- [electron-updater v6.8.9 release (re-pinned 2026-09-01 from v6.8.4)](https://github.com/electron-userland/electron-builder/releases)
- [electron-updater documentation](https://www.electron.build/auto-update)
- [Electron built-in autoUpdater API](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/auto-updater)
- [update.electronjs.org](https://github.com/electron/update.electronjs.org)
- [Electron 41 MSIX auto-updating](https://www.electronjs.org/blog/electron-41-0)

### Code Signing And Notarization

**Major 2026 change — CA/Browser Forum Ballot CSC-31 (effective 2026-03-01, per [CSCBR v3.10.0 §6.3.2](https://cabforum.org/uploads/CA-Browser-Forum-CSCBR-3.10.0.pdf)):** All publicly-trusted code-signing certificates issued on or after 2026-03-01 have a maximum validity of **460 days** (≈14 months), down from the prior 39-month cap. EV and non-EV certs are both affected uniformly. Hardware-token holders must physically rekey every ≤14 months (≤460 days) rather than every 3 years (literal "15 months" can equal 456–465 days depending on month lengths and may exceed the cap). This reshapes the vendor-cost calculus and makes per-month subscription signing services competitive with EV-cert purchases for small/mid-tier publishers.

#### macOS

- **Identity:** Apple Developer ID Application certificate, issued free under the $99/year Apple Developer Program membership.
- **Process:** Hardened runtime enabled + Apple notarization via `xcrun notarytool` (the `altool` command has been deprecated since 2023-11-01 and must not be used) + `xcrun stapler` to attach the notarization ticket to the artifact so it works offline.
- **Entitlements:** The hardened runtime must declare only what the shell needs. V8/JIT commonly requires `com.apple.security.cs.allow-unsigned-executable-memory` and `com.apple.security.cs.allow-jit`; dynamic loading of native modules may require `com.apple.security.cs.allow-dyld-environment-variables`; keystore access uses `keychain-access-groups`.
- **Operational risk (Jan 2026+):** Apple's notarization queues have been experiencing delays of 24–120+ hours per active developer-forum threads. Plan-021's release pipeline must include **timeout + retry** logic rather than synchronous blocking on notarization.

#### Windows

- **Primary path:** **Azure Artifact Signing** (renamed from "Azure Trusted Signing" when it went **GA on 2026-01-12**), Basic SKU $9.99/month (5,000 signatures, 1 certificate profile). FIPS 140-2 Level 3 HSM-backed, zero-touch cert lifecycle, no hardware token, no EV-cert purchase. Chains to a CA in the Microsoft Trusted Root Program — recommended path for Smart App Control friendliness.
  - **Eligibility gate:** Public Trust is available to organizations in **USA, Canada, EU, UK** and to **individual developers in USA and Canada only**. Outside those regions, Artifact Signing is not available for public-trust signing and this project must use a traditional EV cert. Per-deployment decision; confirm eligibility before locking in.
  - **Does not issue EV certificates.** If distribution requires EV (e.g., for instant SmartScreen reputation), Artifact Signing is not sufficient and a traditional EV cert from DigiCert / Sectigo / SSL.com is required in addition.
- **Alternative $0 OSS path:** [SignPath Foundation](https://signpath.org) sponsorship issues a Standard OV signing cert under publisher attribution "SignPath Foundation" (UX trade-off against own-branding — end users see "SignPath Foundation" not "AI Sidekicks" in the publisher field). Per [Microsoft Learn code-signing-options 2026-04-20](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/seccrypto/cryptography-tools), SignPath is the sole Microsoft-recommended OSS-sponsored path (OSSign.org intake is suspended). Eligibility: OSI license + active maintenance + MFA on maintainer GitHub account + published code-signing-policy page on the project site. Turnaround SLA not published by Microsoft or SignPath; third-party reports indicate 2–4 weeks at procurement time.
- **Fallback path:** EV code-signing cert from DigiCert / Sectigo / SSL.com. Typical OV pricing $300–$700/year; EV pricing $400–$1,200/year. Under [CSCBR v3.10.0 §6.3.2](https://cabforum.org/uploads/CA-Browser-Forum-CSCBR-3.10.0.pdf) (CSC-31, effective 2026-03-01), renewal is now every ≤14 months (≤460 days) rather than 3 years — factor this into vendor comparison vs. Artifact Signing's monthly subscription.
- **Smart App Control reality:** Valid code signing is **not sufficient** for Smart App Control to allow a binary. SAC evaluates cert trust chain _and_ cloud reputation (Intelligent Security Graph). New binaries with low distribution get blocked until reputation builds, even with a valid EV signature. First-launch UX on Windows 11 will be rough until reputation accumulates; mitigation is Artifact Signing's Public Trust chain + clear user-facing "Run anyway" instructions.
- **Windows 10 EOL:** Windows 10 reached end-of-support on 2025-10-14. Per [Spec-021 §Scope](./021-desktop-shell-and-renderer.md#scope), V1 supports Windows 10 + 11 (x64). Because Windows 10 is EOL, this spec accepts the security-posture implication of shipping to an EOL OS for V1. Plan-021 must surface this as a known-state item; a V1.1 decision may tighten to Windows 11 only.

#### Linux

There is no universal code-signing model for Linux. Three distinct formats, three distinct signing mechanisms:

- **`.AppImage`** — optional embedded GPG signature (rarely verified by users); this project additionally publishes the artifact hash alongside the download.
- **`.deb` / `.rpm`** — GPG-sign the package with the project's release key; publish the public key for repositories.
- **Snap / Flatpak** — Canonical signs Snap artifacts on your behalf at the Snap Store; Flathub signs Flatpak artifacts at publication.

`electron-builder` supports all of the above. No automatic signing beyond what each format specifies.

Sources:

- [CA/Browser Forum CSC-31 ballot](https://cabforum.org/working-groups/code-signing/requirements/)
- [Microsoft — Azure Artifact Signing GA announcement (2026-01-12)](https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/microsoft-security-blog/simplifying-code-signing-for-windows-apps-artifact-signing-ga/4482789)
- [Azure Artifact Signing FAQ — eligibility & EV scope](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/artifact-signing/faq)
- [Apple Developer ID](https://developer.apple.com/developer-id/)
- [Apple Developer forum — notarization queue delays](https://developer.apple.com/forums/thread/813441)
- [Windows 10 lifecycle](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/lifecycle/products/windows-10-home-and-pro)

### Crash Reporting

Electron's built-in `crashReporter` module uses **Crashpad** (not Breakpad as older docs suggest). It automatically covers main, renderer, Node `child_process`, and `utilityProcess` crashes. Payloads upload as `multipart/form-data` POST (minidump + metadata — version, platform, process type, custom params with key ≤39 bytes, value ≤127 bytes). Compression has been enabled by default since Electron 12.

**V1 sink choice — deferred to a follow-up decision:**

- **Option A — Sentry Electron SDK.** Production-grade symbolication, issue grouping, release tracking, per-process initialization (`@sentry/electron/main`, `/renderer`, `/utility`). Lower operational burden. Cost: third-party dependency holding minidumps.
- **Option B — Self-hosted symbolication.** Project-operated minidump sink. Higher operational burden. Benefit: no third party sees crash payloads.

**Critical PII rule (applies to both options):** Minidumps contain process heap memory, which for this shell contains PASETO tokens, DPoP keys, WebAuthn PRF output, OAuth bearer headers, and daemon session tokens. Uploading unfiltered minidumps to a third party is a data-exfiltration vector. V1 must:

1. Configure `crashReporter.start({ uploadToServer: true, ... })` only after initializing `beforeSend`-equivalent scrubbers (Sentry's `beforeSend` / `denyUrls` / `ignoreErrors` hooks, or equivalent pre-upload scrub for the self-hosted path).
2. Strip session IDs → stable hashes; file paths → extension-only; user messages and agent reasoning payloads → elided.
3. For the self-hosted path, keep the sink inside the same security boundary as the control plane.

Final sink decision is a BL-to-be-filed and owned by Plan-021; this spec declares the requirements the choice must satisfy.

Sources:

- [Electron `crashReporter` API (Crashpad)](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/crash-reporter)
- [Sentry Electron SDK documentation](https://docs.sentry.io/platforms/javascript/guides/electron/)

### WebAuthn Platform-Authenticator Native Module

_Re-derived 2026-09-01. The 2026-04 pass could not confirm platform coverage for any cross-platform module and left V1's posture as an accepted scope degradation. A first-party pass has now measured all three platforms and settled it; every claim below carries its source in §References §WebAuthn Platform-Coverage Pass (2026-09-01), which also names the four 2026-04 hypotheses it falsified._

**What Electron does and does not provide.** The renderer's `navigator.credentials.*` is not the path here, and the reason is neither CSP nor the absence of a secure context. Measured on Electron 44.1.1 / Chromium 152.0.7977.65 (macOS 26.6.1 arm64, 2026-09-01): from a renderer loaded on the `sidekicks-renderer://` scheme registered `standard: true, secure: true`, `isSecureContext` is `true` and `PublicKeyCredential` is defined, yet `navigator.credentials.create()` rejects with `SecurityError: Public-key credentials are only available to HTTPS origins with valid certificates, HTTP origins that fall under 'localhost', or pages served from an extension.` Chromium gates the API on an **origin allowlist**, not on the secure-context test, so no amount of scheme privilege opens it. `app.configureWebAuthn` does exist on Electron 44 and is honoured — an unsigned build logs `Touch ID authenticator unavailable because keychain-access-group entitlement is missing or incorrect` — but it configures only that renderer path, which the allowlist has already closed. The 2026-04 claim that Electron "does not provide native platform-authenticator WebAuthn prompts on macOS, Windows, or Linux" is accordingly **corrected**: its macOS half is false (Chromium's Touch ID authenticator is present and entitlement-gated), and the main-process design stands on the allowlist and on §Trust Stance rather than on that claim. Two capability reads **do** answer from the custom scheme and cost no credential — `PublicKeyCredential.getClientCapabilities()` and `isUserVerifyingPlatformAuthenticatorAvailable()` — but that is a statement about the **renderer**, and the probe below runs in **main**, where neither exists: both are Web IDL members of the DOM `PublicKeyCredential` interface, and Node exposes no `navigator.credentials` at all (checked against the Node API corpus, zero occurrences). The measurement is recorded for what it establishes — that the allowlist closes the ceremony and not the capability read — and the main-process probe is specified from native calls instead, per leg, below.

**Per-platform strategy. All three legs ship in V1, and each is native wherever its OS exposes a native surface.**

| Leg | Mechanism | What it reaches | Shipped runtime dependency |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| macOS | own-built Swift N-API addon over Apple `AuthenticationServices`, in `apps/desktop/native/webauthn-darwin/` | platform authenticator (Touch ID / iCloud Keychain) through `ASAuthorizationPlatformPublicKeyCredentialProvider`, **and** attached security keys through `ASAuthorizationSecurityKeyPublicKeyCredentialProvider` — two providers, probed and attempted independently | none — system framework. **Two PRF floors, not one:** platform-authenticator PRF is macOS **15.0+**, but the `prf` property on the security-key requests and results arrived only at macOS **26.4**, so on 15.0–26.3 a security key on macOS performs sign-in **without** PRF and its wrapping key comes from the §Fallback Behavior custody leg |
| Windows | the OS `webauthn.dll` surface through the FFI (`WebAuthNAuthenticatorMakeCredential` / `WebAuthNAuthenticatorGetAssertion`) | Windows Hello and roaming FIDO2 keys, through the OS's own dialog | none — an in-box OS component. Its own requirements table publishes **no** minimum Windows version, which is why the leg gates on `WebAuthNGetApiVersionNumber()` rather than on an OS build |
| Linux | Yubico `libfido2` through the same FFI | roaming FIDO2 keys over **USB HID** and **NFC**. `fido_dev_info_manifest` runs the HID backend unconditionally and the NFC and PC/SC backends under compile-time flags that both default **ON** for Linux (`NFC_LINUX` for the native backend; the experimental `USE_PCSC` additionally needs `libpcsclite` at build time), so NFC is native on a default build and, since this leg never bundles the library, is ultimately whatever the distribution built. **There is no BLE backend at all**: the library's own scope sentence is "a FIDO device over USB or NFC", and `fido_dev_info_manifest` calls no BLE manifest under any flag — so a BLE-only key on Linux reaches §Fallback Behavior rather than a ceremony, and adding a BLE transport is a material cost this leg does not take (corrected 2026-09-01, Codex round 2 — the row previously read "USB / NFC / BLE"). Linux exposes **no** platform authenticator, and this leg deliberately claims none | the distribution's own `libfido2.so.1`, never bundled — and the device-access rules are **not** in any `libfido2` package. Access is a property of the host's `systemd` / `udev` (≥ **244**), so the packaged build declares that, not a rules package |

The Device Authorization Grant loopback stays exactly what §Fallback Behavior makes it — the degradation for a host whose probe finds no usable authenticator or no PRF, on **any** of the three platforms. It is not "the Windows / Linux path".

**The FFI.** `koffi`, exact-pinned, floor `3.1.6` (MIT). It is chosen over a hand-written N-API addon for the two non-macOS legs because both are calls into a library the host already has, and over `ffi-napi` (last published 2021-03-18) on maintenance. Four properties decide it: the platform binary arrives as one of fifteen `os`/`cpu`-gated `optionalDependencies` (`@koromix/koffi-<platform>-<arch>`), so nothing compiles at install and **no `onlyBuiltDependencies` entry is warranted** — unlike `better-sqlite3`; it declares `napi: 8`, so an Electron bump needs no rebuild; warm load in the Electron 44 main process measures 7.8 ms / 1.70 MiB RSS, and the whole cost sits at module evaluation, so a `process.platform`-guarded dynamic `import()` means macOS never pays it; and the per-platform packages exist for every V1 target only from `3.1.1` (`win32-arm64` was absent in `3.0.0`–`3.1.0`). Two constraints are normative rather than advisory: koffi's zero-copy `koffi.view()` **throws under Electron**, which forbids external buffers, so every OS-allocated out-struct is copied out with `koffi.decode()` before it is freed; and because the binary lives in a _sibling_ package rather than in `koffi` itself, `asarUnpack` must name `**/node_modules/@koromix/**` explicitly rather than rely on `electron-builder`'s smart-unpack heuristic, whose failure mode is a runtime throw in packaged builds and not a build error.

**Why the macOS leg is own-built.** `electron-webauthn-mac` 1.0.0 (MIT, Vault12) is the **reference implementation** and is not adopted, on two disqualifiers measured against the shipped package: it generates the WebAuthn challenge internally (32 random bytes; the caller cannot pass the relying party's challenge), which defeats every server-verified assertion — both passkey sign-in and the step-up re-authentication Security Architecture requires need the control plane's own challenge for freshness; and its prebuilt `.node` is arm64-only and non-fat with no `os` / `cpu` field and no arch guard, so a `darwin-x64` install succeeds and fails at `require`. The own-built addon drives **both** relying-party providers, each on its own arm: `ASAuthorizationPlatformPublicKeyCredentialProvider`, whose `createCredentialAssertionRequest(challenge:)` and `createCredentialRegistrationRequest(challenge:name:userID:)` take the caller's challenge (macOS 12.0+), and `ASAuthorizationSecurityKeyPublicKeyCredentialProvider` for an attached FIDO2 key, whose own `createCredentialAssertionRequest(challenge:)` and `createCredentialRegistrationRequest(challenge:displayName:name:userID:)` are likewise macOS 12.0+ — the registration signatures differ by one argument, which is a porting trap rather than a design choice. PRF attaches as a **directly-named `prf` property** on each request, never through an `extensions` dictionary: Apple models WebAuthn extensions as individually-named request properties, and no request class in the framework declares `extensions` at all (verified by enumerating the framework index, not by absence of memory). Its floors are the asymmetry that matters: `prf` on the **platform** requests is macOS **15.0+**, while `prf` on the **security-key** requests, and on both security-key results, arrived only at macOS **26.4** — so a roaming key on macOS 15.0–26.3 can sign in and cannot produce PRF, and that host takes the custody leg rather than being refused. PRF output arrives as a CryptoKit `SymmetricKey` rather than as raw `Data`, so the addon extracts bytes rather than casting. The addon is compiled universal (arm64 + x64) and prebuilt on the macOS CI runner, which already carries Xcode for signing and notarization. **The toolchain floor is `Xcode 26.4`, which bundles Swift 6.3 and the macOS 26.4 SDK and itself requires macOS Tahoe 26.2 or later** — the floor is set by the SDK rather than by the language, since the security-key `prf` property is a macOS 26.4 symbol and cannot be compiled against an older SDK, while the runtime floor stays macOS 12.0 through availability annotations rather than through a raised deployment target.

**Detection is a per-host probe at ceremony time, never a platform name (I-021-15).** `process.platform` selects which binding module is loaded and settles nothing else. Three rules govern every leg. **First, a platform-authenticator probe gates the platform arm alone, and only where the OS publishes a direct availability call.** Such a call reports on an authenticator _built into the device_ and takes no transport or attachment parameter, so a negative is not evidence that an attached security key is unusable: `WebAuthNIsUserVerifyingPlatformAuthenticatorAvailable` returning `FALSE` (with `S_OK` — a negative is not an error channel) suppresses the Windows Hello arm while the roaming attempt proceeds under `WEBAUTHN_AUTHENTICATOR_ATTACHMENT_CROSS_PLATFORM`. macOS's two providers are likewise independent arms, but **no probe suppresses either one** — macOS publishes no such call, only a proxy, and the second rule says what follows from that. Routing to §Fallback Behavior on a platform-probe negative would strand every desktop whose only authenticator is a key on the end of a USB cable. Fallback is reached when **every** arm this host offers has been attempted and none produced a usable PRF result. **Second, the probe is native per leg, and on macOS it is advisory and gates nothing.** Windows reads `WebAuthNGetApiVersionNumber()` and `WebAuthNIsUserVerifyingPlatformAuthenticatorAvailable()`; Linux reads `fido_dev_info_manifest` plus `fido_dev_get_cbor_info`. macOS has **no** native call that answers the capability question the way those do — `PublicKeyCredential.getClientCapabilities()` is a Web IDL member that does not exist in the main process, and the one framework member that answers directly, `ASAuthorizationWebBrowserPublicKeyCredentialManager.isDeviceConfiguredForPasskeys`, is macOS 26.2+ **and** browser-class API behind an Apple-approval entitlement this app does not hold. The macOS leg therefore **attempts the platform provider on every host** and reads `LAContext.canEvaluatePolicy(.deviceOwnerAuthentication)` only as a diagnostic recorded beside the outcome — it explains a failure after the fact and prevents no attempt. _Corrected 2026-09-01 (Codex round 2)._ This section previously had the macOS probe gate the platform arm on `.deviceOwnerAuthenticationWithBiometrics`, and Apple's own documented semantics make that a wrong gate: that policy is false on a Mac with no Touch ID hardware, false on one with no enrolled biometric, and false system-wide after too many consecutive failed biometric attempts — and in every one of those states a platform passkey ceremony still completes, through iCloud Keychain, a nearby paired device, or the user's password. Gating on it would refuse the platform authenticator on exactly the hosts Apple documents as still able to serve it, and would do so silently. If a probe is kept at all the policy is `.deviceOwnerAuthentication`, which on macOS covers Touch ID, a paired Apple Watch, **or** the user's password; it is still a proxy, and its negative now costs a diagnostic line rather than an arm. The asymmetry with Windows is deliberate and is the rule, not an exception: a **direct** first-party availability answer may gate its own arm, and a proxy that is not that answer may gate nothing. Fallback is reached on macOS only after both providers have actually been attempted and neither produced a usable PRF result; a macOS host with no platform authenticator and no key falls back exactly as a Linux host with no key does.

**Third, a binding that cannot load is a negative capability result and never a crash (2026-09-01, Codex round 3).** The platform-guarded dynamic `import()` and the FFI's own symbol resolution can each fail on a host that is otherwise healthy — `webauthn.dll` absent or exporting none of the entry points the binding declares, `libfido2.so.1` not installed by the distribution, the macOS addon present but missing the slice for this architecture. Each leg catches that at its own load boundary, records a diagnostic naming the library and the unresolved symbol, and takes §Fallback Behavior. It never escapes as an unhandled rejection, and it is never reported as a failed ceremony: the host did not fail a ceremony, it never had a binding to run one with, and the two are different stories about the host. Each leg carries a fixture that makes resolution fail.

**Four silent-failure guards.** Each names a way a ceremony can report success while producing no usable key material, which is worse than a refusal because the caller cannot tell. A fifth was minted at Codex round 5 for a per-credential PRF evaluation input and **retired at round 6** when that input became a contract-fixed constant; the reproducibility obligation it carried survives inside guard 3, which is where a rule about the salt belongs.

1. **Windows API-version floors are per-member, not per-struct, and are read at runtime.** `WebAuthNGetApiVersionNumber()` is the only reliable reading, and struct sub-versions do not track API versions: `pHmacSecretSaltValues` — the _only_ assertion-time PRF route — sits under `WEBAUTHN_AUTHENTICATOR_GET_ASSERTION_OPTIONS_VERSION_6` but ships in the `WEBAUTHN_API_VERSION_4` delta block, so the assertion-time floor is **API ≥ 4**, and gating it on 6 would refuse on builds that can serve it. `bEnablePrf` needs API ≥ 6 and `pPRFGlobalEval` API ≥ 8, both on the _MakeCredential_ options struct rather than on GetAssertion. The raw `WEBAUTHN_EXTENSIONS_IDENTIFIER_HMAC_SECRET` identifier is create-only ("GetAssertion Input Type: Not Supported"), so the two mechanisms are not interchangeable. Structs are grow-only and `dwVersion`-gated, and the only rule for choosing one is the explicit **API version → maximum struct version** table below: the binding reads the API number at runtime and sends the struct version that table assigns to it. Deriving `dwVersion` by comparing the API number against a desired struct number is exactly the mistake the table exists to prevent — the two are unrelated sequences, which is the first fact this guard states. Two naming traps in the same header are worth stating, because either one read as the other produces a wrong gate: `bPrfEnabled` is **not** `bEnablePrf` — it is an _output_ on `WEBAUTHN_CREDENTIAL_ATTESTATION` (sub-version 5) reporting what the authenticator actually did, and reading it as the input would gate a request on its own result; and MakeCredential's `dwFlags` is still commented `Reserved for future Use` while `pPRFGlobalEval`'s own comment in the same struct instructs the caller to set `WEBAUTHN_AUTHENTICATOR_HMAC_SECRET_VALUES_FLAG` in it, an unresolved contradiction inside the current header that this leg never has to settle only because guard 3 leaves that flag unset on both structs.
2. **An assertion that carries no PRF output fails closed.** All three surfaces can return a valid assertion with the PRF / `hmac-secret` output simply absent — most concretely when the credential was not created as discoverable — and deriving a wrapping key from absent bytes is the failure this guard exists to prevent. The binding treats a missing output as "no PRF on this host" and routes to §Fallback Behavior; it never substitutes, zero-fills, or silently retries.
3. **One salt domain and one salt value across all three legs.** The same logical PRF input must produce the same bytes on every platform, or a credential enrolled on one host derives a different key on another. Windows applies the WebAuthn PRF transform `SHA-256("WebAuthn PRF" || 0x00 || value)` itself unless `WEBAUTHN_AUTHENTICATOR_HMAC_SECRET_VALUES_FLAG` is set, and Apple's PRF input types take WebAuthn PRF semantics, while `libfido2`'s `fido_assert_set_hmac_salt` takes the raw 32-byte CTAP2 salt with no transform. The daemon supplies WebAuthn-PRF-semantics input; the Windows leg therefore leaves that flag **unset**, and the Linux leg applies the transform itself before setting the salt. **The value is one contract-fixed constant, not a per-credential or per-ceremony one (2026-09-01, Codex round 6).** PRF output is a function of the credential's secret _and_ the evaluation input, so an input that varies between two ceremonies against one credential derives two different keys — the ceremony reports success, the key is well-formed, and everything wrapped under the previous one stops opening at the next restart. Fixing the input by contract removes that axis entirely rather than defending against it: there is no per-credential value to mint, store, serve, or lose, and nothing for the daemon to pass through a process boundary. It costs no security, because the input contributes none — the secret is the authenticator's per-credential `CredRandom`, the input only selects which evaluation of it to take, and the value was never confidential in the first place (round 5 served it on an uncredentialed reply for exactly that reason). Yubico's PRF guide recommends "a unique, per-credential random salt … generated by the server during registration and stored alongside the credential ID"; that recommendation is **recorded and declined**, because it presumes an `allowCredentials` flow in which the server already knows which credential will sign, and this deployment's are discoverable — guard 2's own requirement — so at options time there is no credential to look one up for, and returning every candidate's input instead would publish the user's enrolled-credential set on an unauthenticated endpoint. The key separation a per-credential salt would have bought is supplied instead by the custody root below, which gives each credential its own wrapping path without giving it its own envelope. Each leg carries a test that two ceremonies against one credential, separated by a restart, derive **byte-identical** keys.
4. **The assertion's user-verification mode is pinned, never left to the authenticator.** CTAP 2.1 has the authenticator mint **two** independent per-credential secrets at registration — `CredRandomWithUV` and `CredRandomWithoutUV` — and select between them at assertion time on the `uv` bit **actually set in the response**, not on what the platform asked for. The same salt against the same credential therefore yields two unrelated keys depending on whether the authenticator happened to verify the user. `libfido2` defaults `fido_cred_set_uv` and `fido_assert_set_uv` to `FIDO_OPT_OMIT`, which delegates exactly that choice, so an unpinned leg has a non-deterministic key derivation that fails only later and only sometimes — the worst shape in this list. Every leg fixes one UV mode, records it beside the credential id at registration, and requests that same mode on every subsequent assertion. **Requesting is not binding, so the response is what gets checked (2026-09-01, Codex round 3).** A WebAuthn `userVerification` preference is a preference: `discouraged` does not forbid an authenticator from verifying and `preferred` does not establish that it did, so on Windows and macOS the request carries no binding force, and on Linux `FIDO_OPT_TRUE` / `FIDO_OPT_FALSE` is still only what the platform asked for. Every leg therefore **reads the UV flag out of the returned authenticator data** — bit 2 of the flags byte, mask `0x04`, from `WEBAUTHN_ASSERTION.pbAuthenticatorData` on Windows, from `fido_assert_authdata_raw_ptr` on Linux — **`_raw_` and never `fido_assert_authdata_ptr`**, which returns the same bytes still wrapped in their CBOR byte-string header, so a leg reading bit 2 at a fixed offset into that form reads into the header and compares a byte the authenticator never set (2026-09-01, Codex round 6) — and from the `ASAuthorizationPublicKeyCredentialAssertion.rawAuthenticatorData` the macOS providers return — and compares it against the **server-held** mode: the control plane records the UV bit observed at registration on the credential row and returns it on the **verification** reply, so the comparison survives a reinstall and is available on a second desktop using a synced credential ([Spec-016 §Required Behavior](./016-identity-and-user-state.md#required-behavior), Plan-016 I-016-16 — a client-remembered mode has neither property). **On the verification reply and not the options reply (2026-09-01, Codex round 5)**, because this leg requires discoverable credentials — guard 2's own reason — and a discoverable sign-in offers no credential list at all: the options leg does not know which credential will answer, so the only reply that can name the signing credential's mode is the one that arrives after it has signed. That is also why the PRF bytes are provisional until then (§WebAuthn Credential Flow step 4): the comparison this guard performs is not available any earlier. On a mismatch the leg derives **nothing**, zeroizes the provisional bytes, and routes to §Fallback Behavior, because the bytes that came back were computed from the other `CredRandom` and are silently the wrong key rather than a detectable error. The rule is deliberately **not** "require user verification everywhere": that would exclude every non-UV-capable key and refuse exactly the hosts the Linux leg exists to serve. Each leg carries a mismatched-bit test.

**Per-credential KEKs wrap a custody root, never the envelopes themselves (2026-09-01, Codex round 6).** A KEK derived from one credential's PRF output belongs to that credential alone, so a user who enrols a second authenticator derives a second, unrelated KEK — and if the refresh-token and identity-key envelopes were wrapped directly under the first, the second would open nothing. Re-wrapping every envelope at each enrolment is not the repair either: it needs a live ceremony on the **old** credential at the moment the new one is added, which is precisely what a user enrolling a backup key does not want to depend on. The installation therefore holds one **custody root key** — 32 random bytes minted the first time a **verified** ceremony on this installation finds no local root, whether that ceremony was an enrolment or a sign-in — and the refresh-token envelope and the long-term identity-key envelope are wrapped under it **once**. **The trigger is the missing root, not the enrolment (2026-09-01, Codex round 7).** Scoping it to enrolment would have left the ordinary fresh-install case with no root at all: a user whose discoverable credential is already synced to this machine — an iCloud Keychain passkey, a roaming key carried over — signs in without ever enrolling locally, so an enrolment-only trigger never fires, and the token pair the verification reply issues would have nothing to be wrapped under. Minting is therefore checked on every verified ceremony and performed at most once, with the new root wrapped under that ceremony's own derived KEK before the token pair is persisted. Each enrolled credential's derived KEK wraps a **copy** of that root, and the wrapped copies live in the OS keystore keyed by credential id (§Shell ↔ OS Keystore). Enrolling an authenticator writes a new copy and touches no envelope; removing one deletes its copy and touches no envelope; the passphrase-derived KEK of §Fallback Behavior wraps a copy under the same rule, which is what makes a host that later gains PRF a re-wrap of one small copy rather than a re-encryption of everything it holds. Two consequences are stated rather than left to be discovered. Losing **every** enrolled credential loses the root and everything under it — there is no server-side escrow, because an escrow the server can open is a key the server holds, and `§Trust Stance` does not put one there. And the root is **per installation**: a reinstall mints a new one and re-enrols its identity key through the ordinary `verified → bound` flow rather than recovering the old one, which is why sign-in after a reinstall rests on the freshly issued token pair of §WebAuthn Credential Flow step 6 and not on unwrapping anything.

**Windows API version → maximum struct version (2026-09-01, Codex round 4).** The floors above are not derivable from the struct definitions, and a leg that computes `dwVersion` by comparing the API number with a desired struct number sends the wrong version on exactly the builds PRF matters on. Two facts from the pinned `webauthn.h` decide it. First, the header carries **no** `added in WEBAUTHN_API_VERSION_N` comment inside any struct body — every in-struct delta comment names the **struct's own** version constant, and the API-to-struct mapping exists in exactly one place, the version-history block at the top of the file (`webauthn.h` lines 36–154). A tool that parses in-struct comments therefore derives the struct version and is wrong wherever the two diverge. Second, each block is a **delta** from its predecessor by the header's own wording (`WEBAUTHN_API_VERSION_N : Delta From WEBAUTHN_API_VERSION_N-1`), so an unlisted struct carries forward. The table below is read from those blocks and carried forward; a cell marked with a caret is stated by the header, the rest are carried:

| API version | `..._MAKE_CREDENTIAL_OPTIONS` | `..._GET_ASSERTION_OPTIONS` | `WEBAUTHN_CREDENTIAL_ATTESTATION` | `WEBAUTHN_ASSERTION` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 3^ | 4^ | 3^ | 1^ |
| 2 | 3 | 4 | 3 | 1 |
| 3 | 4^ | 5^ | 4^ | 2^ |
| 4 | 5^ | **6^** | 4 | **3^** |
| 5 | 5 | 6 | 4 | 3 |
| 6 | **6^** | 6 | **5^** | 4^ |
| 7 | 7^ | 7^ | 6^ | 5^ |
| 8 | **8^** | 8^ | **7^** | 5 |
| 9 | 9^ | 9^ | 8^ | 6^ |

The binding sets `dwVersion` from this table's row for the API version `WebAuthNGetApiVersionNumber()` returned — never from `min(desired, supported)`, which compares numbers drawn from two unrelated sequences. The three PRF floors fall out of it, and each pairs a request member with the response member that returns its result at the **same** API version, which is the check that the reading is right rather than merely plausible: assertion-time PRF is `pHmacSecretSaltValues` on `GET_ASSERTION_OPTIONS` v6 with its result on `WEBAUTHN_ASSERTION.pHmacSecret` v3, **both at API 4**; registration-time enablement is `bEnablePrf` on `MAKE_CREDENTIAL_OPTIONS` v6 with its acknowledgement on `WEBAUTHN_CREDENTIAL_ATTESTATION.bPrfEnabled` v5, **both at API 6**; and registration-time global evaluation is `pPRFGlobalEval` on `MAKE_CREDENTIAL_OPTIONS` v8, **API 8**. Struct versions 1–2 of `MAKE_CREDENTIAL_OPTIONS`, 1–3 of `GET_ASSERTION_OPTIONS`, and 1–2 of `CREDENTIAL_ATTESTATION` appear under **no** API block at all — the API-1 baseline already starts them at 3, 4, and 3 — so the binding never sends them. The count member for the salt values is **not** `cbHmacSecretSaltValues`, which does not exist: the count lives one level down as `WEBAUTHN_HMAC_SECRET_SALT_VALUES.cCredWithHmacSecretSaltList`.

**Linux read-back is `_raw_`, and the attestation object is composed rather than fetched (2026-09-01, Codex round 4).** A credential id is not a server-verifiable registration, and `libfido2` offers no getter for a whole attestation object — `fido_cred_set_attobj` is a **setter only**, and the library's header carries no `fido_cred_attobj_ptr`. The Linux leg therefore composes the WebAuthn `attestationObject` itself as the three-key CBOR map `{fmt, attStmt, authData}` from `fido_cred_fmt()`, `fido_cred_attstmt_ptr()` / `_len` (already a CBOR-encoded map, spliced in as an encoded item), and `fido_cred_authdata_raw_ptr()` / `_raw_len`. The `_raw_` accessor is load-bearing and not interchangeable with its sibling: `fido_cred_authdata_ptr` returns the authenticator data **still wrapped in a CBOR byte-string header**, which the library's own manual states and which a relying party's verifier rejects, while `_raw_` returns the decoded bytes WebAuthn defines. The same rule governs the assertion: `authenticatorData` is `fido_assert_authdata_raw_ptr(assert, idx)`, never `fido_assert_authdata_ptr` — including for the guard-4 flags-byte read, whose bit offset is otherwise wrong by the width of the CBOR header. Signature, credential id, and user handle are `fido_assert_sig_ptr`, `fido_assert_id_ptr`, and `fido_assert_user_id_ptr`, each indexed by `idx` across `fido_assert_count()`, since an assertion is a collection of statements rather than a single one. A full attestation certificate chain needs `fido_cred_x5c_list_count()` with `fido_cred_x5c_list_ptr` / `_len` per index; `fido_cred_x5c_ptr` is the **leaf only** and would truncate a chain silently. Client data goes in through `fido_cred_set_clientdata` / `fido_assert_set_clientdata`, which take the **unhashed** `clientDataJSON` bytes and hash internally — the form Yubico's manual directs applications to prefer over the `_hash` variants — and the leg keeps its own copy of those bytes for the server payload, because the library exposes a getter for the client-data **hash** and none for the client data itself. Every returned pointer is valid only until the next non-`const` call on the same object, so each is copied immediately rather than held.

**Blocking FFI calls run off the main thread, and cancellation is per-platform rather than uniform (2026-09-01, Codex round 4).** `WebAuthNAuthenticatorGetAssertion` and `fido_dev_get_assert` both block until the user touches a key or dismisses a dialog — `libfido2`'s manual states outright that `fido_dev_get_assert` "is synchronous and will block if necessary" — so a synchronous FFI invocation from Electron main freezes every window for the length of a human interaction and leaves no thread to deliver a cancellation on. Both `koffi`-backed legs therefore invoke through the library's **asynchronous call path** — the wrapped function's `async` member, called as `fn.async(...args, (err, result) => …)` and promisified at the call site, since koffi documents no promise form of its own — which runs the native call on a worker thread and settles the callback back on the event loop; the macOS leg needs no equivalent, because `AuthenticationServices` is already asynchronous by construction — the ceremony is started and its result arrives on an `ASAuthorizationController` delegate callback.

Three of koffi's documented async constraints bind this use and are honoured rather than discovered: a variadic function cannot be called asynchronously (none of the declared entry points is variadic); thread safety across the boundary is the caller's responsibility, so no buffer is shared between the worker call and main; and a buffer the native side may write later or from another thread must be `koffi.alloc`-backed rather than a JS `Buffer` or typed array, because only the former is documented not to move — which is why every out-struct is allocated that way and read back with `koffi.decode()`. Cancellation is **not** symmetric across the two legs, and the asymmetry is stated rather than smoothed over. On Windows it is fully documented: `WebAuthNGetCancellationId` yields a GUID before the ceremony, that GUID rides the options struct's `pCancellationId`, and `WebAuthNCancelCurrentOperation` takes it — so main can cancel a call that is still pending and the pending promise settles as a refusal. On Linux there is no sanctioned equivalent. `fido_dev_cancel` exists and is exported, but its entire documentation is one sentence on a page it shares with `fido_dev_open` — "cancels any pending requests on `dev`" — it is omitted from that page's own return-value section despite returning `int`, and `libfido2` publishes **no thread-safety statement anywhere** in its manual pages or its README; its only in-tree call site is a single-threaded cleanup loop over devices that are not the one being used. Calling it from another thread while a call is blocked on the same device handle is therefore undocumented behaviour, not a supported operation, and this spec does not adopt it. The Linux leg instead bounds the wait with `fido_dev_set_timeout` before dispatch — documented to stop the library blocking longer than the given milliseconds, failing the call with `FIDO_ERR_RX` — and a user cancel settles the ceremony at the main-process boundary while the bounded native call is left to reach its own end; the device handle is closed only after that call has settled, never while it is in flight. The residual is named: a Linux cancel is honoured immediately by the caller and the key may keep waiting for touch until the bound expires. **That residual is what makes single-flight a rule rather than a nicety (2026-09-01, Codex round 5).** Because the caller settles before the native call does, a user who cancels and immediately retries would otherwise have a second `fido_dev_get_assert` dispatched against a device handle the first call still owns — two concurrent calls on one `fido_dev_t`, which `libfido2` documents nowhere as supported and which the same missing thread-safety statement that ruled out `fido_dev_cancel` rules out here. Each binding therefore holds a **per-binding in-flight slot from dispatch until the native callback settles, the timeout arm included**, and a ceremony requested while the slot is held is refused with a typed **busy** result — a distinct outcome, neither a probe result nor a failed ceremony, so the shell can say "that key is still finishing the last request" rather than silently queueing or crashing. The slot is released in the callback and in the timeout path alike, never on the caller's cancellation, which is precisely the moment the two diverge. The rule is stated for both `koffi` legs and is only load-bearing on Linux, where the Windows cancel pair does not exist to make the divergence disappear. Each leg carries a test that drives a deliberately blocking stub through the async path and asserts that the main process stays responsive while it is pending, that the Windows cancel settles the pending call, that the Linux bound settles it with no `fido_dev_cancel` issued against an in-flight call, and — driving the arm rather than only the timeout — that a **cancel followed immediately by a retry** takes the busy refusal while the first call is still outstanding, then succeeds once the slot releases.

**Per-leg verification protocol.** Hosted CI runners verify install and load under Electron on all three OSes, the exact unsigned-macOS error text, the `app.configureWebAuthn` entitlement diagnostic, the custom-scheme `SecurityError` and the two capability reads, `WebAuthNGetApiVersionNumber()` and `WebAuthNIsUserVerifyingPlatformAuthenticatorAvailable()` (both credential-free and UI-free), every FFI struct layout and prototype against pinned `sizeof` / `offsetof` constants, and the `libfido2` manifest-and-setter chain with zero devices attached. They **cannot** produce a single PRF byte on any platform — no Touch ID, no Hello, no TPM, no key — so end-to-end PRF is a **recorded manual matrix**: a signed macOS build on hardware, a Windows host with Hello or a security key, and a Linux host with a security key. The Linux entry runs on a **clean distribution image** with only the declared dependencies installed, because the failure this leg's packaging guards against — a key present and unreadable — appears only on a host that was never hand-configured, and a developer machine with stale hand-added rules cannot show it. The macOS entry additionally waits on the signing, entitlement, and `apple-app-site-association` gate, which stays [BL-108](../backlog.md)-bound: per its own README the reference implementation has no localhost exemption on native macOS, so the associated-domains entitlement and an RP-hosted association file are required even for local testing.

### Console Libraries

_Added 2026-09-01. Every console axis names the libraries considered, the evidence, and the verdict — ADOPT · ADOPT-with-constraints · OWN-BUILD · AVOID — under one rule: a well-maintained library that cuts code we would otherwise write is adopted only where it leaves the visuals, the product requirements, speed, and memory untouched. Citations per axis are in §References §Console Design Research Pass (2026-09-01). Versions are the registry `latest` at authoring and are exact-pinned in `apps/desktop/package.json`; a bump is a lane-3 change that re-reads the row. Terminal row corrected 2026-09-02: the printed-URL link provider is the adopted `@xterm/addon-web-links` 0.12.0 and never own-built, and the own-built piece is the link scheme guard both link paths run — an erratum-class correction of a row that misdescribed the shipped code, moving no Status._

| Axis | Verdict | Constraints and reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Timeline virtualization | ADOPT-with-constraints `@tanstack/react-virtual` 3.14.10 | Headless, 7.6 kB gzip, the only candidate whose scroller we own (`getScrollElement`, `scrollToFn`, `observeElementOffset`); no smooth `scrollToIndex`, stable keys, direct DOM updates. The reading anchor, follow, and window-cap controller is own-build (no library has a sub-row reading anchor). Chromium caps element height at 33,554,431 px, so the window cap is a ceiling, not a nicety. AVOID virtua (internal scroll writes), react-virtuoso (not headless), react-window, react-virtualized. |
| Streaming markdown | ADOPT micromark + `mdast-util-from-markdown` with GFM; ADOPT-with-constraints remend (tail only); OWN-BUILD the delta-fed block segmenter, the mdast-to-React mapper, the reveal router, and the footnote registry | Whole-message re-parse per token is quadratic; settled blocks parse once with a two-block settle lag; `html` nodes render as literal text, so no sanitizer is on the path. AVOID react-markdown, the rehype stack, markdown-it, markdown-to-jsx, and every Tailwind-styled streaming renderer (styling identity leakage, raw HTML rendering, whole-message re-lex). |
| Syntax highlighting | ADOPT `shiki` 4.4.3 **in the daemon**, as the one colourer for the whole product ([ADR-035](../decisions/035-one-syntax-colourer-in-the-daemon.md)); the renderer holds no instance and no Worker colours anything | The daemon colours a file once with the JavaScript engine and lazy grammars, caches the spans against what the file holds, and hands them out; every surface paints the spans it is handed as classes that read the theme's own code tokens, never as a colour written on the element, so a theme or scheme switch repaints the same spans in place and colours nothing twice. A language nobody recognises stays plain with no round trip. A block still arriving keeps the colours it already has, and only the line still arriving is drawn plain until it lands; a block redrawn after a switch starts from the colours it had, so it never flashes back to plain. Never the preset bundles, and never the WebAssembly engine, whose linear memory grows to about 29 MB and is never reclaimed. The package moves from the desktop app to the daemon in the same change, at the same pin. AVOID react-shiki, highlight.js, the prism family, sugar-high, starry-night. |
| Math and diagrams | ADOPT-with-constraints KaTeX (lazy, settled blocks only, `trust: false`, MathML output first); ADOPT-with-constraints `mermaid` 11.17.2 (MIT), lazy and `securityLevel: 'strict'` | No direct DOMPurify dependency. A diagram block draws itself with no press asked for: the picture is made as soon as the block has settled, its source shows in its place while the picture is being made and stays one press away afterwards, and a block that cannot be drawn says so and why — the parser's own reason — above that source rather than showing the bare source. The library is loaded on its own chunk at the first diagram, which is why §Budgets lists diagrams among the lazy chunks: it is 84.0 MB unpacked. It is initialized with the base theme and its theme variables resolved from the Meridian tokens, with its own error rendering suppressed so the block's line is the one a person reads; because its colour maths cannot parse the perceptual and custom-property colour forms the tokens are written in, each token is resolved to concrete 8-bit colour first. Pictures are cached least-recently-used and bounded, keyed on the sanitized source plus a palette identity that changes exactly when the palette or the label face does, with the identifier baked into a cached picture rebased on every hit so two copies of one diagram never share one internal reference; the source is sanitized first (byte-order mark, zero-width and control characters, line endings, entities, smart quotes). The picture is drawn again on a theme change and on a text-size change, and its floor height is derived from the line it is read at. A diagram opens into the quick look and nowhere else — there is no second viewer — where a pinch or a modifier-wheel zooms about the pointer and a drag pans; the block's own hover actions copy it as a picture or copy its source. |
| Terminal | ADOPT-with-constraints `@xterm/xterm` 6.0.0 with the WebGL, fit, search, unicode11, serialize, and web-links addons; OWN-BUILD the React wrapper, the renderer pool, and the link scheme guard | Pool WebGL panes and never churn the addon (a disposed WebGL addon does not release its context, and Chromium drops the oldest context past sixteen); `onContextLoss` falls back to DOM; `allowProposedApi` only for Unicode 11; the printed-URL link provider is the project's own `@xterm/addon-web-links` 0.12.0 rather than a hand-written one — it carries the buffer-index back-mapping (wrapped lines, early-wrapped wide characters, the expansion bound) and its default matcher admits `http`/`https` only, the same closed set the own-built guard allows — while both link paths, OSC 8 and printed, run that guard on activation beside `allowNonHttpProtocols: false`; `disableStdin` plus wire-level gating for watchers. AVOID every published React wrapper and the canvas addon. |
| ANSI in ledger rows | ADOPT `anser` (`ansiToJson` only); own span mapper | JSON chunks mapped to Meridian tokens; never an HTML-string path. AVOID ansi-to-react, ansi-to-html. |
| Electron runtime | ADOPT Electron 44.x | Electron 41 is end-of-life; the `WebContentsView` detached-bounds fix landed on 42 and later only (§Electron Version And Support Window). |
| Native browser view | ADOPT `WebContentsView`; OWN-BUILD the bounds bridge, the airspace policy (hide the view and swap in a `capturePage` image while an overlay is open), focus hand-off, and a `devtools-protocol`-typed `webContents.debugger` wrapper | Bounds scale by `webContents.getZoomFactor()`; the pane is a native view, never an iframe (`frame-src 'none'` stands). The window that hosts it is a `BaseWindow` and never a `BrowserWindow` (§Security Hardening Baseline), and the subsystem is [ADR-036](../decisions/036-embedded-browser-for-preview.md)'s. A captured page's scale is read from the returned image rather than from the platform's reported scale factors, which report a single factor against a genuinely doubled capture. |
| Browser tool set | ADOPT-with-constraints `@playwright/mcp` 0.0.80 (Apache-2.0, 86 kB unpacked) hosted **inside the daemon** through its `createConnection` entry point, one connection per session on its own loopback HTTP route, attached to that session's Chrome-debug endpoint ([ADR-036](../decisions/036-embedded-browser-for-preview.md)); ADOPT `playwright-core` 1.62.1 (Apache-2.0) for the daemon's headless fallback and its element-reference selector engine, and `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` 1.30.0 (MIT) as a direct daemon dependency for the transport the hosting pattern constructs; ADOPT `perfect-freehand` 1.2.3 (MIT, 112 kB unpacked, no runtime dependencies) in the renderer for the mark strokes | The server never owns or launches a browser: each connection is configured with the session's own debug endpoint and attaches to the browser the machine already has, so per-session isolation comes from the endpoint rather than from the server, and the package's own warning about a persistent profile serving one browser instance does not bind. Zero stdio children and zero extra Chromium. The connection is registered into each provider at session start — inline configuration on Claude Code, an endpoint setting on Codex — and the route must be listening before the provider process starts, because its address is in that process's arguments; the two providers' call shapes normalize to one transcript row. Pin exactly and never at latest: 0.0.80 depends on a `1.63.0-alpha` Playwright pair, ahead of the repository's 1.62.1, so the version pair and the element-reference format are re-checked on every bump. The debug endpoint comes from the app-wide remote-debugging switch, so who can reach that loopback port is the record's own subject. |
| Packaging and update | ADOPT `electron-builder` 26.15.x and `electron-updater` 6.8.9 (§Build And Packaging, §Auto-Updater) | AVOID update-electron-app, the built-in `autoUpdater` alone, Electron Forge. |
| Main-process storage and logging | OWN-BUILD the settings file (atomic rename, Zod parse, fail-closed defaults) and a JSONL logger; ADOPT `@napi-rs/keyring` 2.1.0 for credential material; ADOPT-with-constraints `safeStorage` for sealing a small non-credential settings value; ADOPT `@electron/fuses` | The keyring is MIT, 47 kB plus one native package per platform, and it and `safeStorage` both refuse to persist where the backend would store plain text (§Native Keystore). AVOID electron-store, electron-log. |
| Tests | ADOPT-with-constraints Vitest browser mode via `@vitest/browser-playwright`; ADOPT `@playwright/test`, `@testing-library/react`, knip, memlab; OWN-BUILD the bundle-size budget test (brotli and gzip over the built `out/renderer` assets through `node:zlib`, budgets in one JSON, on the tier-1 desktop job — `size-limit` 13.x's engines exclude the 22.14 floor that job runs on); ADOPT bare `axe-core` as a test-only devDependency — the accessibility tier runs in-page, and browser-mode test code receives Vitest's own `page` locator object rather than Playwright's `Page` ("your tests don't have access to Playwright's `page` because it runs on the server"), while the Playwright `Page` that `@axe-core/playwright`'s `AxeBuilder({ page })` requires is reachable only inside a server-side custom command, where it is the orchestrator HTML wrapping the test iframe rather than the component under test | `axe-core` is MPL-2.0 — outside the project's MIT / Apache / BSD / ISC dependency norm — and is admitted for a never-distributed test dependency only, recorded in [ADR-020 §Decision Log](../decisions/020-v1-deployment-model-and-oss-license.md#decision-log). AVOID jsdom, lost-pixel (archived), hosted visual-regression services, msw. **No pixel gate is registered** since 2026-09-09: §Console Test Tiers retired pixel comparison outright, so the visual-regression evaluation in §References records why no hosted service would have been adopted rather than what a build is compared against. |
| Diff viewer | ADOPT `diff` 9.0.0 (jsdiff, BSD-3-Clause) for parse and intraline compute; OWN-BUILD one shared virtualized diff-row renderer for inline cards and the pane, painting the colour spans the daemon computed | No candidate is both headless and virtualized. jsdiff parses an 898 kB patch in 2.1 ms; line diffs above about 2,000 lines run off-thread. No colouring happens in the renderer at all, in a worker or otherwise — the daemon is the one colourer ([ADR-035](../decisions/035-one-syntax-colourer-in-the-daemon.md)), and the cost of doing it here is why: 10,000 TypeScript lines tokenized on the main thread cost 1,960 ms and 84.5 MB of heap. AVOID the highlight.js-bundling diff viewers (315 kB gzip), diff2html, react-diff-viewer-continued, `@codemirror/merge`, monaco, diff-match-patch (archived). |
| Node-graph builder | ADOPT-with-constraints `@xyflow/react` 12.11.6 ([ADR-026 §Decision](../decisions/026-visual-node-graph-workflow-authoring.md#decision) holds); ADOPT `@dagrejs/dagre` 3.1.1 (MIT, 1.4 MB unpacked) for the layout, left to right, in the renderer and in the daemon alike; ADOPT `croner` 10.0.1 (MIT, 155 kB unpacked, no dependencies, Node ≥ 18) in the daemon for workflow schedules and Node's own `fs.watch` with `picomatch` 4.0.7 (MIT, 94 kB unpacked) for the file-watch trigger; OWN-BUILD node and edge visuals, inspector, keyboard connect mode, layout persistence | MIT; 59.4 kB gzip plus the required 13.6 kB `base.css` whose 41 tokens are all set from Meridian; 9.1 MB heap and 60 fps drag at 200 nodes. Exact-pin 12.11.x with `@xyflow/system` in lockstep (12.11.4 shipped broken); never alias `zustand`; `isValidConnection` is not applied by `addEdges`, so every programmatic and keyboard edge path calls the same predicate. The layout is the same library on both sides of the wire, so a definition an agent wrote with no layout at all still opens laid out and the two processes agree on where a node sits; layout bytes are still not definition bytes ([Spec-015 §Canvas layout is not definition bytes (SA-35)](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md#canvas-layout-is-not-definition-bytes-sa-35)). `croner`, `picomatch` and the watch are new daemon dependencies rather than renderer ones, and the trigger surface they serve is [Spec-015 §Entry node and the V1 trigger surface (SA-37)](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md#entry-node-and-the-v1-trigger-surface-sa-37). AVOID elkjs (EPL / GPL, 440 kB), the unmaintained `dagre` 0.8.5 line the `@dagrejs` fork supersedes, d3-dag, graphology, and a scheduler or a file watcher of our own. |
| Layout, panes, drag | ADOPT-with-constraints `react-resizable-panels` 4.12.3 for the pane tracks and `@atlaskit/pragmatic-drag-and-drop` 3.1.0 for drag; ADOPT `overlayscrollbars` 2.16.0 with `overlayscrollbars-react` 0.5.6 for every scroller but the transcript; OWN-BUILD the pane store, separator chrome, keyboard and menu reorder paths, live-region strings, native-pane rect tracking, and toasts | Panels: MIT, 11.7 kB gzip, no CSS, ARIA window-splitter with keyboard; store-owned layout; pin or patch the open ARIA min/max swap on three-plus pane groups. Drag: Apache-2.0, headless, native HTML5 drag so no per-frame renders; it provides no keyboard drag by design, so the menu and chord paths are ours; the rows it drags are the waiting messages stacked under a running turn and the terminal's and browser pane's tabs, never a pane, because the column holds one main pane and carries no tab strip. Scrollbars: both MIT, the core 3.0 MB unpacked with no dependencies, the React binding asking for React 16.8 or newer and a 2.x core; set to hide once the pointer leaves, themed from the console's own tokens for bar width, fade and hover thickening, and drawn over the content so no scroller takes layout width for one in any state. The transcript draws none, its right edge being the ask rail's. AVOID dockview, flexlayout-react, rc-dock, allotment (mouse-only), react-mosaic, `@dnd-kit/*`, react-dnd, and every toast library that injects its own stylesheet. |
| Headless UI primitives | ADOPT-with-constraints `@base-ui/react` 1.7.0 as the one widget family; ADOPT `tinykeys` 4.0.0 as the chord parser only; ADOPT `@floating-ui/react-dom` for our own non-widget overlays; OWN-BUILD the keybinding service and when-clause grammar, the command registry and palette, the fuzzy scorer (a port of VS Code's `fuzzyScore`, MIT), the live announcer, roving tabindex for ledger rows, and the modal `inert` guard | The only family complete for the console's widget list, including combobox and autocomplete, with zero CSS and subpath exports (89.6 kB gzip for the console set). Exact pin; subpath imports only; every popup portals into our overlay root; no body scroll lock; the shell applies `inert` to the app root while a modal is open until the upstream focus defect closes. Runner-up `react-aria-components` with a named flip trigger (a vendor screen-reader matrix becoming a sign-off requirement). AVOID Ariakit, Headless UI, cmdk, kbar, react-hotkeys-hook, focus-trap-react, react-focus-lock. |
| State, storage, forms, search, dates | ADOPT-with-constraints zustand 5 (one store per open session, partitioned entity maps, per-frame event coalescing, per-row selectors, no `persist` or `immer` on the event path); ADOPT-with-constraints `idb` 8.0.3 under an own per-session partition, LRU trim, and quota gauge; OWN-BUILD the JSON Schema field mapper and schema form on Zod `fromJSONSchema`; OWN-BUILD the subsequence scorer shared by the palette, settings search, sidebar filter, and find; ADOPT native `Intl` and `Temporal` | §Security Hardening Baseline's CSP has no `unsafe-eval`, so every schema library that compiles validators at runtime (Ajv, and with it rjsf, JSON Forms, and jsoneditor validation) is disqualified before size is weighed. A flat entity map costs 1.3 ms per event at 20,000 entities and a partitioned one 57 µs. Temporal is renderer-only — the main process and every `node`-environment test project use `Date` and ISO strings, because the toolchain Node the `main-unit` project runs under has no `Temporal` (probe row below; Codex round 2); contracts stay on ISO strings and epoch milliseconds. AVOID jotai, valtio, mobx, immer, idb-keyval, dexie, localforage, `@rjsf/*`, `@jsonforms/*`, ajv, CodeMirror, fuse.js, fzf, minisearch, date-fns, dayjs, both Temporal polyfills. |
| Motion, fonts, icons, meters, formatting | ADOPT platform motion (CSS transitions, `@starting-style`, View Transitions, the Web Animations API); OWN-BUILD a spring sampler emitting `linear()` easings; ADOPT-with-constraints `unplugin-icons` with `@iconify-json/tabler` plus our own signature-glyph collection compiled at build time; ADOPT IBM Plex Sans and IBM Plex Mono variable builds self-hosted from the foundry packages; OWN-BUILD the meters, sparklines, and arcs as SVG and CSS; ADOPT native `Intl` for numbers, currency, bytes, durations, and relative time | An animation library on the render path fights the virtualizer (layout measurement per render, own frame loops, per-child observers). Lucide is the default icon set of the most common component kit, so it reads as the generic dashboard look. The fontsource builds strip the `zero`, stylistic-set, and width features, so the faces come from the foundry packages. AVOID `motion` as a layer, `react-spring`, `@formkit/auto-animate`, Inter, Geist, commercial monospace faces, uplot, visx, recharts, nivo, `pretty-ms`, `filesize`, `bytes`. |
| Structure enforcement | ADOPT knip 6.34.0 (the same tool the Tests row already adopts for dead exports; this row states its scope and its gate and does not re-admit it); ADOPT-with-constraints `dependency-cruiser` 18.2.0; the third gate is the desktop package's own `lint` over the ESLint flat config [ADR-022](../decisions/022-v1-toolchain-selection.md) already admits repo-wide, carrying ADOPT-with-constraints `eslint-plugin-perfectionist` 5.11.0 (MIT) | The third leg joined this axis 2026-09-09 admitting no library, and the same day it admits exactly **one**: the structural rules the retired architecture tier asserted are `no-restricted-imports` / `no-restricted-syntax` / `no-restricted-globals` / `no-restricted-properties` entries in `apps/desktop/eslint.config.mjs`, which add no dependency, no command, and no CI step of their own, and `eslint-plugin-perfectionist` joins them inside that same gate — no new command, no new CI step, one devDependency. The **axis count stays twenty** either way: a leg admitting its first library is not a new axis. No ESLint plugin was adopted for the one-component-per-`.tsx` rule: the nearest candidate, `eslint-plugin-react-refresh`'s `only-export-components`, checks a different property — that a module exporting a component exports only components, a Fast Refresh constraint rather than a count — so that rule stays a review rule stated in `apps/desktop/AGENTS.md`. ADOPT-with-constraints `eslint-plugin-perfectionist` — exactly **two** rules, both scoped to `src/renderer/src/console/**` and both run `type: 'unsorted'`, so the claim is the order of the `apps/desktop/AGENTS.md` §Module shape SECTIONS and never an alphabet: `sort-modules` ranks the exported declaration kinds (types and interfaces → class → function) with every non-exported declaration falling into one trailing unsorted bucket, which is what keeps that section's "a private type that exactly one helper uses may sit directly above that helper" exception followable rather than merely written; and `sort-classes` ranks fields → constructor → public methods → everything else. No other rule of the plugin is enabled and `@typescript-eslint/member-ordering` stays frozen out, since a second member-order authority is a second grammar for one claim — the reason the architecture tier was retired. Two limits are recorded rather than discovered later: `sort-modules` publishes no variable selector and treats every module-level `const` as a **partition boundary** it moves nothing across, so §Module shape section (4) constant placement is convention and existing constants are preserved by construction; and the plugin's dependency ordering is populated for `class` and `enum` declarations only, so caller-above-callee among functions is never disturbed. Defaults left unset are load-bearing: `newlinesBetween: 'ignore'` and `partitionByComment` / `partitionByNewLine` `false` mean neither rule adds or removes a blank line. knip and `dependency-cruiser` are both scoped to `apps/desktop`, both CI gates on the tier-1 desktop job, run as two tasks with `--continue` so one red leg never hides the other's findings — the claims are disjoint (reachability from an entry point versus whether an import may exist) and neither subsumes the other. knip runs from the workspace root, because a package-scoped run sees neither the pnpm catalog nor the root tooling manifest and reports both as findings; it runs with `--treat-config-hints-as-errors`, so a pattern matching nothing fails the gate; its only admissible `ignore` entry names the task that will import the symbol. dependency-cruiser cruises `src`, `build`, and `scripts` in one pass from the package root with `tsPreCompilationDeps`, so a type-only upward import is still a violation, and with an explicit `enhancedResolveOptions.extensions` list rather than `--ts-config`: it resolves a tsconfig's `extends` against the process directory, so a config whose `extends` climbs above its own directory loads only when the cruise runs from there, and this tree declares no path aliases, so nothing is lost. AVOID jscpd — its documented `minLines` / `minTokens` minimums (5 and 50) are the floor of a reported duplication and the duplicates this tree carries are one to four lines, while its tokenizer fires on the house style's near-identical block comments and deliberately parallel data tables; its publication record also carries a 557-day gap (4.0.5 on 2024-07-03 to 4.0.6 on 2026-01-11) with zero releases in calendar 2025, and v5 is a ground-up Rust rewrite shipping seven prebuilt native binaries through `optionalDependencies`. AVOID `eslint-plugin-boundaries` — under flat config it activates no resolver automatically and classifies an import no configured resolver resolves as `"external"`, so against this tree's `.js`-specifier imports it fails **open** and silently rather than loudly; making it correct would mean adding `eslint-import-resolver-typescript` for the `.js` → `.ts` alias map its own TypeScript-support guide requires, pulling the whole `eslint-plugin-import` resolver family in behind it. _Narrowed 2026-09-09 with the perfectionist adoption: the objection is that specific **resolver plumbing** and the fail-open it exists to repair, never a blanket rule that this stack admits no plugin. `eslint-plugin-perfectionist` resolves nothing — it reads one file's own AST — so it costs none of that and is admitted on the same row that refuses `eslint-plugin-boundaries`._ |
| Workflow definition file form | ADOPT `yaml` 2.9.0 (exact pin) as the one codec for the [Spec-015 §Definition file form — export and import (C-17)](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md#definition-file-form--export-and-import-c-17) YAML dialect the console serializes and imports — the definition-detail export and the file-import authoring path — reached only through `await import("yaml")` from the bridge's file-form codec, so the library lands on its own async chunk fetched at the first export or import act and never joins the initial import graph (the codec module itself sits on that graph through the bridge door, which is why the boundary is the dynamic import and not the module) | The deciding property is the node model. `parseDocument` returns a `Document` whose scalars carry the resolved value AND the source text (`Scalar.source`, "Set during parsing to the source string value"), so the `ai-sidekicks-schema: 1.0` marker — a **string** on the wire and in the stored `schema_version` column, but a `!!float` under the YAML 1.2 core schema's plain-scalar resolution, which reads an unquoted `1.0` as the number `1` — is read off the marker scalar's source and refused when it was not written as the spec's string, while the hashed body is read off resolved values through the same tree; `stringify` writes the export from the same model, so the console reads and writes one dialect through one library. Held to the library defaults that are also the spec's: `version` `'1.2'`, `schema` `'core'`, `uniqueKeys` `true` (a duplicate key is a `DUPLICATE_KEY` parse error and an import refusal, never last-writer-wins), and `doc.errors` / `doc.warnings` read and surfaced as typed refusals rather than thrown past. Measured (ours, 2026-09-07, the §Measurement record method): the Document API (`parseDocument` + `stringify` + `isScalar` + `isMap`) is 97.8 kB / 30.5 kB gzip and `parse` + `stringify` 98.1 kB / 30.6 kB — the package does not tree-shake between its layers — against js-yaml 5.4.1's 56.7 kB / 17.0 kB for `load` + `dump`: a 13.5 kB gzip difference paid on a lazy chunk the 450 kB initial-bundle budget excludes. ISC, zero dependencies, `browser` field mapping the build to `browser/index.js`, bundled by esbuild for `--platform=browser` with no shim.
